just the right fit

by pl_at0

Summary

fired from vogue, sharp tongued journalist agatha harkness takes a reluctant gig covering the tetris world championship, only to be unexpectedly drawn to its reigning champion, rio vidal.

Chapter 1: the interview

Chapter Notes

welcome to my silly fic, tetris au.
i'm so glad you decided to give it a chance and i can't wait to read your comments on what you think about it.

update:
dear readers,
starting with chapter 6, the bold and italics are used sparingly. please, bare with me on this until then. this is my first fic i've ever written, and i had no idea what i was doing in the beginning. i'd get rid of it, but honestly, i'm keeping it only to show the growth in my writing over time.

thank you for reading. i appreciate it, more than you could ever know. 💚 💜 🙏🏻

<>

– –

“Really, Agatha?! ‘The Top 10 Ways to Know You’re Stupid??” The Vogue magazine editor croaked, her voice dripping with exasperation. “We’re a fashion magazine, not Cosmopolitan.”

Agatha scrunched her nose. “Listen, our readers are probably stupid, and you said to write something our readers would want to read.”

The editor let out a deep sigh, pinching the bridge of her nose. “Last week, I told you...you were supposed to write an article on ‘The Timeless Appeal of Minimalist Fashion.’”

Agatha rolled her eyes dramatically. “Tsk...but that’s so boooooooring.”

The editor slammed a hand on the desk. “Sit down. We need to talk.”

– –

Fifteen minutes later, Agatha stormed out of Vogue headquarters, a box of her things in hands, now officially unemployed. Again. She had burned through more journalism jobs than she could count, but somehow, somehow, she always landed on her feet.

She returned back to freelancing, this time, her only callback was from…ugh…PC Gamer.

She glanced at the email subject line:

“Freelance: Classic Tetris World Championship”

“Damn diversity hire,” she muttered, clicking it open.

The details were bleak. She had to meet with some dude named Larry tomorrow at 2 pm in their office. She rolled her eyes. This would probably be the first time Larry had been in the presence of a woman in the last year. Or at least one he hadn’t had to pay for her company.

Still, Agatha needed the money. She dressed for the meeting in effortless business formal, debating how much cleavage to weaponize…

‘Screw it’ she thought…’Boobs out.’ It’s not like these people had standards.

When she arrived at PC Gamer’s office, her worst suspicions were confirmed. Larry, the editor, had all the presence of a man whose only experience with women involved anime figurines, empty jars, and body pillows.

“So, uh, you’re gonna be covering the Classic Tetris World Championship.” Larry squeaked out.

“A what championship?” Agatha blinked.

“Tetris…you know…the game?”

“It’s fucking Tetris,” she deadpanned. “Is there even a strategy to it?”

Larry’s nostrils flared “It’s one of the most competitive esports in the world. You’ll be interviewing the reigning champion.”

“Oh, yay. A conversation with the King of the Virgins.”

Larry sighed. “Tetris World Champion Rio Vidal. She’s a woman. She’s been dominating the scene for years.”

She?

Agatha’s eyebrows shot up. Intrigued, she grabbed the press credentials and her itinerary from Larry.

…she had to fly to…Pasadena, California.

Great.

Agatha hated California. It was slow and lazy. But New York, where she lived had bite. But whatever. She had a job to do.

– –

– –

The flight was long. Agatha, nestled into her window seat, with her laptop on her tray table and her notebook on her lap. With a sigh, she cracked her knuckles and started researching, only after taking a sip from her plastic cup of airline vodka in her hand.

A quick Google search brought up the official ‘Classic Tetris World Championship’ website.

‘Oh brother,’ she groaned, already unimpressed.

She skimmed the rules, the prize money a whopping $10,000. Nothing groundbreaking. She moved on to videos they had posted, watching some skinny teenagers absolutely demolish the screen with pieces falling faster than her last relationship had crashed and burned. She winced. Okay, so maybe there was skill involved.

She jotted down the basics in pencil...old fashioned journalism, but that was just how Agatha did it. She had a master’s degree in journalism from Northwestern, for crying out loud. She wasn’t exactly in the running for a Pulitzer with this one, but the payout for this article? Quadruple what she usually made freelancing. So, yeah. She was going to put in some effort.

She scrolled through a list of current players, mostly boys, some younger, some older.

‘God, I need more alcohol,’ she muttered, pressing the call button for a flight attendant.

She found a short bio for the person she was going to interview, Rio Vidal.

Agatha leaned in, her fingers absentmindedly twirling her pencil as she read. 32. From Des Moines, Iowa. Favorite Tetris shape: square.

She raised an eyebrow. ‘The fuck kind of person has a favorite Tetris piece?’ she mumbles.

Curious, she Googles her name on image search.

Oh. Oh.

She had been expecting another basement...dwelling, keyboard...smashing gamer, but Rio Vidal was...stunning. Shoulder-length brown hair, striking features, and big brown eyes that held a quiet intensity that made Agatha’s stomach do something suspiciously close to a somersault.

This just got interesting.

Agatha did a few more minutes of research, then yawned. She put her things away, pulled out an eye mask, and chose to sleep off the rest of the flight.

– –

– –

 

She arrived at her hotel, a Hilton by the Pasadena Convention Center. She scoffed. PC Gamer had splurged more than Vogue ever did. At least she wouldn’t be staying in some roach infested dive. She tucked in for the night, ready to get this over with tomorrow and head back home away from blasted California…

Agatha rolled out of bed the next morning, got showered, dressed in a dark purple suit, and donned her press credentials on a lanyard. She glanced at her notes before heading out.

11 a.m., Room 209.

The convention center smelled…horrid. Like burning batteries? Expired Taco Bell? No, corn chips. Definitely corn chips.

She arrived at Room 209. The door was open. No one inside. The room was small. Plain. A single table and a few chairs. Thrilling. She ran a hand through her hair and plopped her bag on the table before taking a seat in one of the chairs.

She checked her watch. 10:52 a.m. She sighed.

‘Let’s just get this over with so I can fly the hell back home.’ she groaned to herself.

11am

…

11:10am

…

11:19am

…

11:27am…the door creaks open

Rio walks into the room wearing ripped jeans, Birkenstocks with green socks, and a t-shirt that read, “Dinosaurs are my favorite nugget shape," with a picture of a t-rex in the middle, not nuggetized.

‘Christ, she dresses worse than my nephew Billy,’ Agatha thought to herself.

Rio immediately sat in the first chair she saw, her movements efficient, unthinking. She didn’t scan the space, didn’t hesitate, just sat.

Agatha furrowed her brow. Seriously? She let out a dramatic huff, grabbed her chair, and dragged it across the floor until she was sitting directly across from Rio.

She reached out a hand to Rio. “Agatha Harkness.”

Rio stared at her hand for a beat too long before shaking it with a quick, deliberate grip. “I’m Rio.”

Agatha pulled out her notepad and pencil. “So, Rio, I’m here to interview you for PC Gamer as the Tetris World Champion.”

Rio blinked. “No,” she said plainly. “Not this year’s. Not yet. No...not until Sunday. I’ll beat Alex on Sunday.”

Agatha blinked. What the fuck. The certainty in her voice was unsettling. Not cocky. Not hopeful. Just a fact, as if she’d already run through all possible outcomes and this was the only logical one. If Rio wasn’t so unfairly beautiful, this interview would already be the most insufferable one of her career.

Still, she pressed on.

“First question...why do you like playing Tetris?”

Rio tilted her head slightly, like she had never been asked before. “…I don’t know. The shapes are…pleasing.”

Agatha leaned back, very obviously checking Rio out, chuckling to herself. “...your shape is pleasing.”

Rio froze, brow furrowing. “What?”

“Nevermind.” Agatha smirked, flipping a page in her notes. “I read that your favorite shape is...”

“Tetrimino.” Rio interrupted.

Agatha’s smirk vanished. “…What?”

“They’re not just shapes,” Rio corrected. “They’re Tetriminos.”

Agatha just…blinked. She had a master’s degree in journalism, and this was somehow the most complex interview of her career.

“…Right. Okay. So, your favorite Tetrimino is the square?”

Rio’s entire body lit up with enthusiasm as she started physically demonstrating, twisting her body into different positions.

She bent herself into an L-shape. “This one is called Orange Ricky.”

She reversed it. “This one is Blue Ricky. No one likes Blue Ricky.”

She shifted into a jagged Z-shape. “This one is Cleveland Z.”

Then she mirrored it. “And this one is Rhode Island Z.”

Standing up straight, she stretched her arms above her head into an I-shape. “This one is Hero. He saves your ass every game.”

She sat back down and stuck her arms out, making a perfect T. “And this one is Teewee, the shape shifter.” She quickly cycled through a few variations, it almost looking like she was doing the robot.

Finally, Rio curled herself into the fetal position, hugging her legs.

"And this one is my favorite, Smashboy. As you put it, the square one." Her voice was quieter now. "...and I don’t know…he just feels safe to me."

Agatha stared. What the hell was this girl. Her rizz was there, but it was, like...weird rizz.

"Ok…" Agatha said, shaking her head, staring at her notes that now looked like the ramblings of someone having a psychotic break.

What even was this interview?

Rio, shifted in her seat and, suddenly blurted out, “So...like...why are you a girl?”

Agatha scoffed. “What?”

“I guess they thought I would relate to you more,” Agatha said, spinning her pencil in her fingers. “You know. Woman to woman.”

Rio nodded. “Yeah. Girls are nice.”

She took deep sniff that was obviously loud.

“They smell better than boys.”

Agatha’s brows shot up, but before she could say anything, Rio kept going.

“You smell nice. Plus, they’re really pretty…” She looked Agatha directly in the eye. “Like you.”

Agatha’s brain short circuited. She blushed. Like, visibly blushed.

She played along and leaned forward and sniffed dramatically in Rio’s direction in reply. “Well,” she said, smirking, “at least you shower. Some of your competitors obviously don’t.”

Rio laughed, shaking her head. “No, they don’t. Mike never showers.”

Agatha shuddered. She could tell just by her short walk through the space already. Were they all named Mike?

A silence settled between them, the kind that lingered just a little too long. Heavy and weird and strangely comfortable.

Agatha cleared her throat. “So…”

– –

– –

Rio’s eyes darted around, landing on a Tetris 1v1 setup in the corner. She lit up.

"OH NICE!"

Agatha flinched. "Jesus…what?!"

Rio was already standing, moving with purpose toward the corner of the room. Agatha twisted in her chair to see what had caught Rio’s attention.

There, completely unnoticed until now by Agatha, was a Tetris setup.

Rio sat in one of the chairs, and pulled a white glove from her jeans pocket.

"Do you want to play a game with me?" Rio asked.

Agatha squinted. "…Of Tetris?"

Rio, far too excited, starting up the game, “Yea!”

Agatha exhaled. “...sure. Why not." She got up and sat in the other chair in front of the tv, grabbing a controller from the ground.

Rio tilted her head. "You’ve played before, right?"

"... only a few times on an old Gameboy," Agatha said plainly.

“Cool.”

It was a slaughter.

Agatha barely cleared 10 lines. Rio did 174 in under three minutes.

Rio leaned back, stretching lazily, then deadpanned, “I guess my fingers are more nimble than yours.”

Agatha arched an eyebrow and smirked. "Are they really?"

"What do you mean? Yes? I just proved that my fingers are literally faster than yours.” She cracked her knuckles and wiggled her eyebrows. “Hours a day of practice." Rio said like it was just plain words coming out of her mouth, unsure of why Agatha would question her.

Agatha pressed her lips together, fighting the urge to laugh. "Right. Sure. Practice." She leaned her elbow on her knee, watching Rio. She has no idea.

Rio perked up. “You want me to show you the trick?”

Agatha, chuckled, "I have a feeling you’d show me anyway."

Rio grabbed her own controller. “It’s all about the technique.” nodding, completely missing the innuendo.

She did a quick, fluid flicking motion with her fingers, like she was casting a spell.

"Someone once timed me," she added. "I can do this at 30 taps a second."

Agatha burst out laughing. "That must make SOME things easy for you."

Rio stared at her, confused. “Yeah? It does? I mean, it’s efficient.”

Agatha, biting her lip to hold back a very inappropriate laugh, simply nodded. “Mhm. Very efficient.”

Rio then stood up and leaned over Agatha from behind her chair, reaching for her hands.

Agatha froze.

The warmth of Rio’s body, the way she leaned over her so casually...it sent every thought flying out of Agatha’s brain.

God, Rio smelled good.

Rio gently took Agatha’s hands, positioning them on the controller the way she had her own…completely unaware of the effect she was having on Agatha.

“See?” Rio murmured. “It’s all in the technique. Try it...just your fingers.”

Agatha tried. She failed spectacularly.

She could not focus with Rio’s hands on hers, with Rio’s breath warm against her neck, with Rio’s entire presence wrapping around her. Was she even aware? Did she even notice?

Eventually, Rio let go and plopped back into her own chair, adjusting her glove like nothing had just happened.

Agatha exhaled sharply, gripping her controller tightly.

Rio looked at how Agatha was holding her controller, and grabbed her own, tilting her head.

“Want to play another round?”

Chapter 2: falling (two) pieces

Chapter Notes

you beautiful humans 💚 💜

i was so overwhelmed by your response to this, i just HAD to keep writing.

i read, and replied, to every single one of your comments and laughed so much. i am so happy you enjoyed it.

my writing abilities i completely thank my high school english teacher, who beat into me the ability to write what i think unabashedly. also, grad school was, in a nutshell, a writing gauntlet. fun fact: when i write, I'm literally acting these things out to myself in my living room. it helps make dialogue feel real. it also helps to keep me entertained while writing this!

anyways, i hope you enjoy this chapter! happy reading.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

– –

– –

Agatha took a deep breath, willing her hands to stop trembling. She wasn’t nervous. No. It was just...

Rio was a little too close, a little too casual, and a little too devastatingly unaware of the effect she had on Agatha.

“...another round?” Agatha scoffed, shaking out her hands out as if that would make them work better. “You just embarrassed me in front of myself. Do you think I have the kind of ego that can withstand that twice?”

Rio tilted her head. “...yes?”

Agatha exhaled sharply through her nose. This woman had to be joking. But she wasn’t. Rio Vidal was just…like this.

Agatha narrowed her eyes. “You’re enjoying this, aren’t you?”

Rio blinked. “Playing Tetris? ….yea?”

Agatha studied her, tapping her nails against the controller. She wanted to push, to get some kind of reaction that wasn’t just an innocent hyper fixation over a video game. “Let me guess…you’re the kind of person who thinks anything can be solved with enough practice.”

Rio nodded solemnly. “Yes.”

Agatha smirked. “Even talking to women?”

Rio hesitated. Her brow furrowed, and she tapped her fingers against the edge of the controller absent mindedly copying Agatha. “...what?”

‘Oh, this is going to be fun,’ Agatha thought to herself.

Agatha leaned back in her chair. “I’m just saying…you don’t seem particularly skilled in that area….unless…you’re trying to speedrun lesbianism...like you do Tetris.”

Rio sat blankly in her chair…considering what Agatha said for a beat, like what she said was an actual strategy she could optimize. Agatha couldn’t help the laugh that slipped out.

“I’m serious,” Agahta teased. “What’s your PB for getting to a first date with a woman?”

Rio blinked. “...my…my…what?”

“Personal best,” Agatha replied, twirling her pencil between her fingers, leaning forward, “...for getting to a first date with a woman.”

Rio squinted. “I know what PB means…and…I don’t think that’s how it works.”

Agatha shrugged, “...that sounds like something someone who has never had a PB in that department would say.”

Rio’s lips pressed into a thin line, and for the first time, she looked…determined.

Dangerous.

Agatha suddenly felt like prey.

“I’ve had plenty of those PB’s” Rio said, and then...she leaned in closer to Agatha. “I don’t choke under pressure.”

Agatha stilled.

Oh. Ok….

Maybe she’d underestimated Rio.

Clearing her throat, Agatha grabbed her notepad, desperate to regain control of the conversation…this is an interview…remember??? “Right, well. This has been sufficiently weird. Let’s actually talk about Tetris.”

Rio leaned back, but there was a hint of amusement in her eyes now. “That’s what I thought we were doing.”

Agatha ignored her. “What’s the plan for the tournament?”

Rio considered the question, rubbing her gloved thumb against her opposite palm, massaging it. “Same as always...play...win.”

Agatha scoffed. “You don’t even think about the competition?”

Rio shook her head. “Ew..think about the boys???......No point,” she said, shrugging casually.

“No point?” Agatha scoffed.

“If I do what I’m supposed to, it doesn’t matter who I’m playing against.” Rio said plainly.

Agatha stared at her, Rio’s casual confidence almost infuriating. She didn’t doubt that Rio was talented, but this was bordering on delusion. Or maybe…

…maybe she was just that good.

Agatha scribbled some notes, more to distract herself than anything. Then, without looking up, she muttered, “You know…you’re kind of cocky.”

Rio nodded. “Yea.”

Agatha lifted her head. “Not gonna deny it?”

Rio shrugged. “You don’t have to when it’s true.”

Agatha let out a low whistle. “Jesus Christ.”

Rio tilted her head, amused. “No…he won’t help you win, even though people thank him all the time when they do.”

Agatha spun her pencil around her fingers again, but the rhythm was off and it stumbled out of her hands.

Rio, moved like lightning, and caught it. Mid air. One handed. Like a reflex.

Agatha stared.

Rio smirked. “What? …tetris helps with reaction time.” She pointed the pencil back to Agatha, eraser first.

Agatha took the pencil back, shaking her head. “Alright, you little savant. I have what I need for my article.”

Rio looked vaguely disappointed. “Oh..ok.”

Agatha hesitated. “…But.”

Rio perked up.

“…If you want to grab dinner later, I could be convinced to listen to you talk more about Tetriminos.”

Rio blinked. Then, like she was processing code, she nodded once. “Yes. That would be… acceptable...?”

Agatha smirked. “See? Personal best.”

Rio frowned. “Huh?”

“...fastest time securing a date with a woman,” Agatha said teasingly.

Rio’s mouth opened, then closed. Then opened again. “Wait...was that...” and Rio froze unsure what to say.

Agatha stood up and grabbed her bag. She ripped a piece of paper out of her notebook, scribbled her name and phone number on it, and put it on her chair. “Let me know where.”

“See you at eight?...smashboy...” Agatha winked.

She walked out before Rio could recover, but she swore she heard a quiet, bewildered, “…Ok.”

Agatha grinned.

This freelance gig just got a whole lot more interesting.

– –

– –

Dinner was at some hole in the wall sushi place, one of those hidden gems you could only find if you had lived in Pasadena too long.

Rio had chosen it off of Google, claiming she liked the 'efficiency' of sushi. Agatha had rolled her eyes at that. Efficiency. Of course.

They sat across from each other in a booth, chopsticks in hand. Agatha, ever the New Yorker, ordered a dirty martini. Rio stuck with water.

“So,” Agatha started, swirling the olive in her drink. “Is your whole life Tetris, or do you do other things?”

Rio tilted her head. “I like paleontology.”

Agatha blinked. “...I’m sorry?”

“I studied it before I went pro.”

Agatha leaned forward. “You were going to be a…dinosaur scientist?”

Rio nodded. “Yes. But then…Tetris.”

Agatha laughed. “You say that like it explains everything.”

“It does,” Rio said simply, popping a piece of sashimi into her mouth. “There’s something very structured about both things. Patterns. Rules. Strategy. You never know what’s going to come next.”

Agatha smirked. “So…you’re just a nerd in every way possible?”

Rio blinked, then nodded. “Yes.”

Agatha laughed again, taking a sip of her drink. God, she hadn’t had this much fun on a freelancing gig in…well…ever.

Rio set down her chopsticks, took a deep breath, and met Agatha’s gaze.

“I like talking to you” Rio said, staring deeply into Agatha’s eyes.

Agatha’s stomach did a flip. Fuck…her big brown eyes were intense.

Agatha covered her reaction with a smirk. “Yea? Is that your PB for smooth pick up lines?”

Rio considered this. Then, after a long pause…“...yes.”

Agatha sighed, shaking her head. This woman was ridiculous. But maybe, just maybe, she liked that.

– –

 

– –

Agatha didn’t get nervous.

At least, not usually. Not in any way that mattered. She was a professional, and professionals certainly didn’t get nervous over casual dinners with world class Tetris players who had, somehow, against all logic, become strangely compelling.

And yet.

She was hyperaware of every single thing about Rio.

The way she held her chopsticks so precisely, her fingers moving with practiced, fluid movements.

The way she took her time before responding to questions, as if every answer required some kind of internal algorithm before being deemed worthy of speech.

The way her eyes...were so damn unreadable half the time...lit up the second the topic turned back to patterns, strategy, or optimal placement.

Agatha had been on a lot of dates with women. Some good, some bad, most forgettable. But there was something about Rio that made her sit up and pay attention.

And that was new.

“You keep staring,” Rio noted, mouth full, pausing mid bite.

Agatha hummed, unfazed. “Maybe I like looking at you.”

Rio blinked. And blinked again. As if processing the words like a particularly tricky T-Spin maneuver.

(author’s note: T-spin: the act of placing a T-Tetrimino (named, no joke, ‘Teewee’) into a T-shaped space).

Then, finally Rio answers “...that seems…irrelevant to Tetris...”

Agatha nearly choked on her drink. “Jesus, you really don’t have a casual mode, do you?”

Rio set down her chopsticks, tilting her head. “No…I don’t think so.”

There was something almost innocent about the admission, which made it worse.

Agatha could handle almost anything. Arrogance. Cockiness. Overconfidence. But the sheer, genuine lack of self awareness? That was lethal.

“So let me get this straight,” Agatha said, tapping a nail against her martini glass. “You can pull off world record breaking combos in a game that requires strategy, foresight, and reaction speed, but you can’t recognize when someone is flirting with you?”

Rio blinked. “I…recognize it.”

“Oh?” Agatha scoffed.

Rio nodded, like it was the most obvious thing in the world. “I just don’t see how it’s useful.”

Agatha exhaled, staring at this absolute enigma of a woman across from her. “For the…love of god…useful how?”

Rio shrugged. “Flirting doesn’t help you win.”

Agatha pressed her fingers to her temples. “Rio, this isn’t Tetris.”

“That’s what I mean.”

“Ok…let me rephrase...” Agatha huffed.

Rio interrupted and tilted her head. “Are you playing a game with me?”

Agatha paused.

She narrowed her eyes, scanning Rio’s expression for any hint of smugness. But no...Rio was asking in complete sincerity, like this was a question she genuinely needed answered.

And that was when Agatha realized something kind of horrifying.

Rio wasn’t just bad at flirting. She was completely, catastrophically new to it.

Agatha sat back, exhaling sharply. “...Wow.”

Rio frowned. “...What?”

Agatha grinned, shaking her head. “You weren’t kidding. You really do speedrun everything, don’t you?”

Rio considered this. Then, solemnly said “....I’d like to think…I optimize.”

Agatha couldn’t help it...she laughed. Loud. Unrestrained. She bent over. Snorting and all.

Rio just blinked at her, apparently waiting for an explanation.

“You are ridiculous,” Agatha finally managed. “Completely, utterly ridiculous.”

Rio’s lips quirked ever so slightly. “That’s not the worst thing I’ve been called.”

Agatha smirked. “Oh yea? What’s the worst?”

Rio picked up another piece of sashimi, thinking. “Once, during a match, someone called me an ‘unfeeling, block dropping robot.’”

Agatha covered her mouth trying to stifle another laugh. “Wow. That’s…pretty specific.”

Rio nodded. “I don’t think it was meant as a compliment.”

“Well…” Agatha said, swirling the last of her drink, “I think you…feel things.”

Rio’s gaze flicked up, something unreadable flashing across her face. “...you do?”

Agatha held her stare. “Yea…I do.”

The moment stretched. Something shifted. Rio’s fingers twitched against the table they were sitting at, like she was resisting the urge to react.

And then, with a slow, measured blink “I think I’d like to prove you right.” Rio said plainly.

Agatha inhaled sharply.

Ok.

…that was...

…well played Rio.

“Good luck, then,” Agatha said, standing up and dropping a few $100 bills on the table, far more than the dinner was between the two of them, “You’re gonna need it.”

Rio nodded once. “Noted.”

Agatha rolled her eyes, grabbing her bag. “See you at the tournament, savant.”

As she walked out, she could feel Rio watching her.

And for the first time…in a long time…Agatha felt like maybe…just maybe...

She’d met her match with Rio.

Chapter 3: a competitive edge

Chapter Notes

shout out to whoever made the ao3 posting script for formatting. i owe you BIG TIME.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

– –

– –

Agatha felt a strange nervous energy that she wasn’t used to.

She wasn’t the kind to overthink things, but there was something about the…she sighs deeply….fucking….CLASSIC TETRIS WORLD CHAMPIONSHIP…that had her pacing her hotel room, checking her phone for messages she wasn’t even expecting.

She gets showered and dressed. Keeping it sleek in tailored black pants that fall perfectly over her pointed black heels. She complimented it with a deep purple silk blouse and a gold bracelet on her wrist, completing her polished, minimalist look. Her hair in a smooth low ponytail, exuding quiet confidence…all for…again…the fucking CLASSIC TETRIS WORLD CHAMPIONSHIP.

She tells herself it’s to keep her professionalism while she’s technically, still on the job.

…or maybe at this rate, it’s for Rio.

When Agatha arrived, the Pasadena Convention Center was packed. And with more people, which, to her chagrin, somehow amplified the smell. She sighed and chuckled to herself, remembering Rio’s comment about ‘Mike Syndrome.’

She gets to the main space, where there are rows upon rows of tv’s set up. The crowd was dense, buzzing with anticipation as competitors prepared to face off.

‘...The fuck…’ Agatha thought. ‘Don’t people have anything better to do with their lives than to be here?’

Agatha spotted Rio immediately. Not like it was difficult...given the crowd of people surrounding her.

Rio was wearing a slightly oversized snoopy t-shirt that said, “my spirit animal is a napping beagle,” featuring snoopy sprawled out on the roof of his doghouse. Green cargo shorts. ‘Practical’ Agatha huffs to herself. Unwavering to her loyal Birkenstocks, this time worn with mismatched socks. One a dark green color and the other black with tiny UFOs on it. Rio’s hair in a messy bun. Headphones in her ears. A fitted white glove on her right hand.

Rio was sitting at a monitor, her entire posture radiating calm focus. Her hands hovered over the controller, waiting for one of her many matches of the day to begin.

Agatha weaved through Rio’s crowd. Rio piped up when she saw Agatha and took one of her AirPods out of her ears. “Hey guys? Give me a minute?” Everyone around her slowly scattered.

“Obviously.”

A pause.

Rio nodded, “…Good.”

Agatha shook her head, fighting a grin.

This assignment had officially gotten out of control.

And she wasn’t mad about it.

Chapter 4: worth the win

Chapter Notes

note on a change in the rating of this fic. things are heating up!! ;)

i have to admit to you, dear reader, writing the last scene in this...i couldn't help but laugh every time i read it while writing and proofing. i hope you find it both humorous and sensual.

Agatha wasn’t nervous.

She was a professional, an experienced journalist. She had interviewed world class athletes, eccentric science geniuses, and people who had built entire careers off their ability to be unshakable. She had sat across from them, stared them down, and asked questions that cut deep, that forced them to think, to react. And yet…here she was, sitting at a quiet sushi bar across from Rio, staring down at a delicate arrangement of disassembled fish and rice like it was some kind of uncrackable puzzle.

This wasn’t a date. At least, she didn’t think it was a date. Agatha hadn’t said it out loud, but it felt like it. Especially considering they had been at this exact sushi restaurant the night before.

She almost hadn’t suggested it, but when Rio had agreed to have dinner with her again...with that simple, steady nod of hers, like it wasn’t even a question...Agatha had caved. And that was why she was now sitting at the counter, side by side with Rio, again, attempting to not feel like this was becoming a thing.

She wasn’t sure yet how she felt about that…which is why Agatha was fidgeting with her chopsticks, because, in a moment of insanity, she had said yes when Rio suggested sitting at the counter for the full omakase experience.

‘What is wrong with me?’ Agatha thought to herself. She wasn’t the type to go all out at place like this. She was the California roll and a beer type…the eat on the subway before heading home type…the ‘DoorDash is my most used app’ type.

But Rio had looked at her with that same quiet curiosity...the one that made Agatha feel like she was as complex as a Tetris board.

Before Agatha could psych herself out any further, the sushi chef placed the first piece in front of them.

Rio, seated beside her, picked up her piece of sushi with precise, practiced movements, her fingers steady as ever. She was watching the chef with the same intensity she had during the Tetris finals, eyes tracking every movement, cataloging every detail.

Agatha exhaled sharply…“...don’t tell me you’re a sushi savant too.”

Rio blinked and tilted her head. “Yes. Knife techniques. Rice-to-fish ratio. Ideal temperature for serving. I especially like all the knives.”

‘…Of course she is’ Agatha laughed to herself.

Agatha picked up her piece “...you are completely incapable of doing anything at a normal level, aren’t you?”

Rio paused for a beat…“I prefer efficiency.”

Agatha laughed, shaking her head as she popped the sushi into her mouth. It melted instantly, rich and buttery, the wasabi cutting through just enough to make her exhale.

“Alright…” she admitted. “...not bad.”

The problem with omakase...something Agatha had not fully considered...was that it required an ungodly amount of patience.

Rio, of course, had no problem with this. She watched each piece being prepared with laser focus, absorbing every motion of the chef’s blade, every careful placement of rice. She didn’t fidget, didn’t tap her fingers against the counter, didn’t so much as glance at her phone.

Agatha, on the other hand, was losing her mind.

“This is taking forever,” she muttered, twirling her chopsticks in midair like it was her pencil. “Remind me, why does anyone do this instead of just ordering a normal plate of sushi?”

Rio glanced at her for only a moment. “...because the process is important.”

Agatha scoffed… “Yea? For who?”

Rio paused, watching as the chef meticulously cut a paper-thin slice of fish. “...for people who like knowing how things work…and who appreciate the small details that make a larger impact.”

Agatha opened her mouth, then shut it. Because...god help her...that made sense.

She rolled her eyes, nudging her empty plate forward. “Alright, fine. But if this turns out bad, I’m never trusting you to pick where we eat dinner again.”

Rio nodded, serious…“Understood.”

Agatha sighed, resting her elbow on the counter, staring at the next piece of sushi being prepared. It looked good. Maybe too good.

They sat in silence for a moment, watching the chef work, the quiet hum of the restaurant filling the space between them.

Rio, perfectly still.

Agatha, not perfectly still.

And before Agatha could stop herself, before she could second guess the words...she turned to Rio and muttered, “You’re enjoying this, aren’t you?”

Rio met her gaze and took a deep breath...“...yes.”

“What?” Agatha raised an eyebrow.

Rio tilted her head slightly. “You get impatient when things take too long.”

“Yea? And what do you get?” Agatha crossed her arms.

Rio blinked, considering her answer carefully…“...I…I don’t mind waiting. If it’s for something worth it.”

Agatha felt her breath hitch just slightly, but she covered it with a smirk. “Oh, so now you’re flirting with me?”

Rio’s brows furrowed. “Was I not before?”

Agatha let out a sharp laugh, shaking her head. “Jesus, Vidal. You are unbelievable.”

Rio just shrugged, like she wasn’t currently responsible for the low, simmering heat in Agatha’s stomach.

The worst part? Agatha didn’t hate it at all…

“So…” Agatha said, shoving her hands into her pant pockets, “Now that you’ve mastered sushi, what’s next on the optimization list?”

Rio glanced at her, tilting her head slightly as they walked. “...I…I haven’t decided.”

Agatha smirked. “No spreadsheet for that?”

“...no…not yet ” Rio blinked.

Agatha shook her head, nudging Rio’s arm with her elbow. “Ok…serious question. Do you ever just do something? Without a plan?”

Rio was silent for a moment. Then, slowly and softly said “...sometimes.”

Agatha leaned in to her, raising an eyebrow. “Yea?”

Rio nodded. “...like this.”

It was about them. It had always been about them.

Rio’s lips hovered just over hers, breath warm, steady. She shook her head once. “No.”

So instead, she reached out...fingers brushing against Rio’s cheek, slow and deliberate, feeling the way Rio’s breath hitched ever so slightly at Agatha’s touch.

Rio didn’t pull away.

And for once...neither did Agatha.

Then her gaze landed on a slim shelf near a desk in the corner of the room, and her curiosity got the better of her.

Agatha strolled over, tilting her head as she scanned the titles.

‘Tetris: The Games People Play by Brian Brown’

‘The Psychology of Judgment and Decision Making by Scott Plous’

‘From Pinballs to Pixels: An Arcade History of Williams-Bally-Midway by Ken Horowitz’

Her lips curled into a smirk. Of course.

Rio frowned slightly…“...books can be instructional.”

Agatha sighed, sitting on the couch in the middle of the room. She rubbed her temples. “For the love of...do you read anything that isn’t about strategy or optimization?”

Agatha grinned. “That’s what I thought.”

“You. Your complete and utter lack of casual reading material.”

Rio raised an eyebrow. “....I…I don’t see how that’s a problem?”

Agatha huffed, turning to face her fully. “It’s not about problems, it’s about balance. You can’t just feed your brain an all strategy diet. Where’s the joy? Where’s the story?”

Rio considered that for a moment, her expression as unreadable as ever. Then, finally…she said, “...would you recommend something?”

Agatha blinked.

That wasn’t the response she was expecting. She had been gearing up for an argument...some witty banter back and forth about Rio’s clinical approach to life, some exasperated remark about how non-fiction was necessary for being a fully realized human.

But instead, Rio was just looking at her. Steady. Curious. Like she actually wanted to know.

"The Journalist and the Murderer," she said, handing it over to Rio. “Classic. It’s about ethics in journalism…and the way people manipulate truth. I think you’ll like it.”

Rio looked up, brow furrowing slightly. “...yes?”

Agatha huffed out a quiet laugh, shaking her head. “...god, you are so different.”

Rio nodded once. “Noted.”

But now, sitting together on Rio’s couch in the quiet lull between conversation, Agatha found herself actually asking.

“...so…what’s with the glove?”

Rio glanced at her.

Agatha gestured toward her hand. “You always wear it. Is it a gamer thing? A lucky charm? Some bizarre form of Michael Jackson idolatry?”

Rio flexed her fingers, watching the fabric shift with a quiet sort of focus. Then after a pause... “...I…I don’t like how it feels when my hand isn’t covered.”

Agatha tilted her head “...what do you mean?”

Rio exhaled and in a hushed voice said… “It’s sensitive.”

But then she thought about how Rio played. How impossibly fast her fingers moved, how precise every action had to be. How she never wasted a second.

Agatha hesitated. “...like… tactile sensitivity?”

Rio nodded. “Yea.”

Agatha blinked, then smirked. “You’ve got…gamer hands.”

Rio raised an eyebrow. “That’s not a real thing.”

“It is now.” Agatha grinned. “You ever think about how you’re basically a precision instrument?”

Rio considered that, then said, completely serious, “Sometimes.”

Agatha let out a sharp laugh, shaking her head. “Jesus. You’re impossible.”

Rio just shrugged, fingers twitching absent mindedly, then clasping her hands together, rubbing the fabric of her gloved hand with her ungloved one.

Chapter 6: hard drop

Chapter Notes

this chapter, my most ambitious not only in length, but in plot is dedicated to all the great fan fiction writers out there. i don't know how you do it. you endlessly both fascinate me and keep off my crippling depression from taking over my life. i think i can speak for all those who adore your writing, stories, and emotions you can elicit from just a few words you're able to weave. thank you for what you do...now get back to writing!! :P

updated: a new character joins the party later in this chapter ;)
fun fact: I used to have a pet rabbit. she was adorable and unfortunately, she passed 8 years ago. this might be the most realistic fic description of rabbit behavior out there lol

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Agatha woke up warm.

She wasn’t used to that. She wasn’t used to the quiet feeling of being comfortable in someone else’s space. She felt Rio before she heard her, the warmth of her presence, the quiet steadiness of her breathing.

When Agatha cracked her eyes open, the first thing she saw was Rio, sitting next to her, reading. A book. Her book. She stared.

Rio turned a page. “You snore.”

Agatha blinked, her brain still booting up. “...excuse me?”

Rio glanced at her. “It’s quiet…but noticeable.”

Agatha groaned, rubbing her eyes and yawning. “Greeeeeat…love that for me.” She sat up, stretching. “What time is it?”

“Almost 10.”

Agatha froze. “...Shit.”

Rio raised an eyebrow. “You…had somewhere to be?”

Agatha hesitated, the morning pressing in around her, heavy with something unspoken. “I…my…my flight back to New York is at three.”

Rio froze. Just for a second. Not in surprise, because this was inevitable, but as if she wasn’t sure how to acknowledge what they both already knew.

“Yea…” Rio said “...and I have to turn in this Airbnb today.”

A silence stretched between them, the air thick with something unsaid.

Agatha swallowed. “Ok.”

Rio nodded, gaze steady. “Ok.”

No argument. No dramatic plea. Just fact.

Agatha exhaled and stood up, gathering her clothes off the floor, slipping back into them, piece by piece. She ordered a Lyft on her phone, grabbed her bag, ran a quick hand through her hair, and checked herself in the mirror in the living room.

She looked…like a one night stand. Like someone leaving, because that’s what she always did when the morning after arrived. She had done this before. Countless times. Her ‘relationships,’ if she could even call them that, never lasted long. A few weeks, a few months, sometimes just a night. People got sick of her. Sick of the sarcasm, the sharp edges, the way she pushed instead of pulled.

Behind her, Rio closed the book she had been reading and got out of bed. She got dressed in a 3x too big black The Smiths band t-shirt and green basketball shorts. The outfit was already laid out, neatly folded, like it had been waiting for her since last night. ‘Because, of course, it had’ Agatha thought to herself.

Rio hesitated feeling Agatha’s eyes on her, then carefully walked over, Agatha’s book in hand. Without a word, Rio held it out to her. Agatha stared at the book, her fingers hovering over the cover before taking it. Her grip tightening around the worn edges as she took it.

Then, an idea struck Agatha. She dug into her bag and pulled out her pencil. She flipped the book open to the inside cover and scribbled something quickly. Snapping it shut, she handed it back to Rio.

“You can keep it…” she said, voice softer than she meant it to be.

Rio went to say something, but Agatha interrupted.

“–but…don’t open it until I’m gone.”

Rio took the book, eyes flickering with something unreadable. She didn’t say anything. Just nodded once.

Agatha’s phone chirped. The Lyft arrived far too quickly. She just nodded back at Rio and left, stepping out into the California sun that now felt too bright, too harsh. Agatha didn’t look back until she was in the car, the door shutting behind her.

She watched Rio through the window, still holding the book, as her Lyft sped away.

Rio stood in the doorway of the now empty Airbnb, thumb running absentmindedly over the book’s spine. She inhaled once…steady…and flipped it open.

Inside, scrawled in Agatha’s sharp handwriting “Next time, let’s try Orange Ricky. – A. Harkness”

Rio stared blanky at it, eyes welling with tears. Then, slowly, she closed the book and held it against her chest, like it was something fragile and sacred.

– –

– –

Before Agatha knew it, she was at her hotel, shoving the last of her things into her suitcase, checking out, and making her way through airport security in a daze.

By the time she was in her seat, the plane climbing steadily into the sky, her laptop was already open, fingers hovering over the keyboard.

She was supposed to be writing an article. An objective, professional article…but…Rio was still in her head, and worse, Agatha wasn’t sure she wanted her to leave.

She spent the next hour pretending to work, writing and rewriting the first paragraph at least twenty times before giving up entirely. Nothing felt right. How the hell was she supposed to write about Rio without acknowledging that she was already in too deep? She sighed, rubbing her temples, staring at the blank word document.

She had never struggled to write before. But then again, she had never met anyone like Rio Vidal. Agatha exhaled, slow and measured, forcing her shoulders to drop, willing the tension in her chest to settle.

“Pull it together, Harkness” she muttered to herself.

She cracked her neck, stretched her fingers, and stared at her laptop, like this was a challenge she couldn’t quite possibly win, but she would, because this was her job. And Rio, insufferable, unreadable, impossible Rio, was just a story. A damn good one. One she could write. So she did.

The Unshakable Logic of a Champion: How Rio Vidal Became the Best Tetris Player in the World

By Agatha Harkness

There’s a moment in every great Tetris match where the board starts falling apart. The screen gets messy, the gaps grow larger, and the speed becomes nearly impossible to see.

Most players panic. Some freeze. Others scramble to correct their mistakes, frantically shifting pieces, hoping for a miracle. Rio Vidal does none of that.

She doesn’t flinch. She doesn’t hesitate. She doesn’t waste time hoping. She calculates. She executes. And then, somehow, she wins.

I watched her do it in real-time, watched as she crushed her competition with ruthless efficiency. Her fingers moved so fast they blurred against the controller, her gaze locked onto the screen with terrifying focus. No wasted movement. No hesitation. Just absolute control. That’s Rio Vidal in a nutshell.

Tetris is a game of precision and pattern recognition, of relentless speed and the decision making ability to see ten steps ahead. It requires a mind that can process chaos and tame it. Rio Vidal has a mind like that.

Her brain moves fast. Faster than most. It picks up patterns instantly, builds strategies before other people even realize a strategy is needed. And, like everything else, she’s optimized it.

Tetris, for her, is perfect. A game with rules, with structure, with an answer to every problem if you’re fast enough or in her case smart enough to find it. And she is.

She’s the reigning Classic Tetris World Champion, the best in the game, and yet, when I asked her why she plays, she didn’t say for the title, the money, the fame, or for the competition. She just said, “…the shapes are…pleasing.” And, honestly? That might be the most maddening thing about her.

Because Rio Vidal doesn’t gloat. She doesn’t bask in her own talent. She just exists in her brilliance, as natural as breathing, completely unaware of how frustratingly impressive she is. Or maybe she does know. And just doesn’t care.

Either way, one thing is clear, she’s not just playing the game. She is the game. And no one else is playing at her level.

~~~~~~~~~~

Agatha sat back, staring at the words, heart pounding in her chest.

The plane hummed around her, the low murmur of passengers, the occasional ding of an announcement overhead. But none of it felt real. Because this article, it wasn’t just about Rio. No, not really. It was about the way Rio felt like an inevitability. The way she moved like she already knew the outcome. The way she made Agatha feel like she had no idea what her next move was supposed to be.

Agatha exhaled. Then, without overthinking, she saved the document, and emailed it to the PC Gamer editor, closed her laptop, and stared out the window at the endless stretch of sky.

She’s just a story, she told herself. So why does it feel like I’m still playing her game?

AGATHA: Doesn’t matter. You still lost.

RIO: …I disagree.

AGATHA: Too late. It’s already on the leaderboard.

Three dots appeared. Stopped. Appeared again.

RIO: …There is no leaderboard.

AGATHA: Oh, I’m definitely keeping score.

RIO: That’s ridiculous.

AGATHA: Welcome to my game, Vidal.

A longer pause. Dots thinking for entirely far too long.

RIO: I think I like your game.

Agatha’s breath caught for just a second. Just long enough for it to matter.

She locked her phone and leaned against the railing, letting the city noise fill the silence between them. She closed her eyes for a moment, exhaling slow, letting the weight of the conversation settle. The air was thick with the summer city air, distant sirens weaving through the restless hum of the city. It was alive, constant, always moving…just like her, just like Rio. And yet, for once, Agatha wasn’t in a hurry to keep up.

– –

– –

She locked the window behind her coming back into her apartment and turned around, her gaze landing on the small, compact form of Scratchy, her lop rabbit, planted firmly in the middle of the living room rug. His soft white fur speckled with flecks of brown and black, ears drooped, eyes fixed on her with the kind of silent scrutiny only he could manage or give to Agatha.

He didn’t move. Didn’t twitch. Just sat there, unimpressed, like he was quietly cataloging her life choices and finding them completely questionable.

Agatha exhaled, rubbing her temple. "What?"

Scratchy just wiggled his nose, like he could smell that she was just smoking outside, when she clearly wasn’t suppose to, per the lease agreement for her apartment.

She sighed. "I swear to God, you were a fucking narc or a tax auditor in another life."

Scratchy thumped his back legs against the floor, the distinct sound sharp and deliberate. A huff, if rabbits could huff.

Agatha crossed her arms. "Well, sir, did you accomplish anything today? Pay some bills? Write a novel? Or did you just sit there and look adorable like a useless freeloader?"

Another thump.

Agatha snorted. “Yeah, that’s what I thought.”

She bent down, scratching gently behind his head, feeling the soft fur under her fingers before picking him up and carrying him like a baby to her small recliner in the corner of the living room. "I had a day too, buddy. Got a job offer I don’t want but can’t turn down. And…" she sighed, shaking her head. "I met someone who might be smarter than me. It’s a problem."

Scratchy wiggled his nose at nothing, entirely unimpressed.

She leaned back in the recliner, shifting Scratchy so he was sprawled lazily across her lap, face looking up to her, his small paws resting against her stomach. His ears flopped to the side as she ran a slow hand down his back, his fur ridiculously silky beneath her fingers. Scratchy had always been like this, silent, observant, constantly judging her like an unpaid therapist who worked for hay and occasional blueberries.

"Don't look at me like that," she muttered, smoothing a thumb over the top of his head. "I know I’m being an idiot. You don’t have to remind me."

Scratchy flicked an ear, an unmistakable gesture of disapproval.

Agatha sighed, letting her head tip back against the chair. "It’s just...this is not how things are supposed to be Scratch. I was supposed to do a job, write an article, collect a paycheck, and move on. Simple. Easy. No unnecessary complications."

Scratchy sat unnervingly still, his big, round eyes locked onto her with an intensity that felt like he was saying ‘and how do you feel about that?’

"But no," she huffed, waving a hand in the air. "Instead, I have a video game genius with a brain like a goddamn supercomputer worming her way into my life, making me think about things I have no business thinking about."

Scratchy twitched his nose, then thumped his back legs twice, his way of saying ‘I told you so.’

Agatha narrowed her eyes. "Oh, shut up."

Scratchy blinked at her. Slowly.

Agatha groaned, dragging a hand down her face. "...god, you’re worse than my boss."

Scratchy thumped his foot against her arm, very much unimpressed.

"Yea, yea…" Agatha muttered, scratching behind his ears. "I know. If I wanted someone predictable, I would’ve gotten a goldfish."

Scratchy responded by dramatically flopping onto his side, the rabbit equivalent of an exhausted sigh.

Agatha smirked, shaking her head. "I’m serious, though. She’s...something else."

Scratchy gave her a knowing look, like ‘...yes…and?’

The words sat heavy in the air, heavier than she wanted them to be.

She stroked Scratchy’s back in slow, absentminded movements, feeling his tiny heartbeat beneath her palm, the rhythm steady, constant. Maybe she needed that. Something steady. Something that didn’t make her overthink.

"What do you think, buddy?" she murmured. "...you think this is a bad idea?"

Scratchy sat up slightly, staring at her with the exact same expression she imagined a disappointed parent would give her. Then, as if to make his stance perfectly clear, he stretched, yawned dramatically, and flopped right back down.

Agatha scoffed. "Oh, so helpful. Thanks Scratch."

Scratchy wiggled his nose.

"Whatever," Agatha muttered, rolling her eyes but smiling despite herself.

She sighed, running her fingers gently down his back again. "...god, I need better friends."

Scratchy let out the smallest, quietest little sigh, a noise that meant he was content. Smug, even.

Agatha side eyed him. "Don’t act like you just won something."

Scratchy wiggled his nose again.

Agatha groaned. "Jesus Christ, I’m having a conversation with a rabbit."

Scratchy flicked his ear, like to say ‘...and yet, here we are.’

Agatha sighed. "Unbelievable." But she didn’t stop petting him.

Sometime later, she fed Scratchy, then drifted into the kitchen, opening the fridge only to stare blankly at its contents. Half a bottle of white wine. A sad looking takeout container. An unopened pack of blueberries she’d bought last week and promptly forgotten about. She wasn’t hungry. Not really. But she needed something to do, something to pull her out of her own head. After a beat, she grabbed the wine, poured herself a glass, and leaned against the counter, taking a slow sip.

Her phone sat on the counter beside her, dark and still. No new messages. Not that she was waiting for one. Not at all.

She exhaled, chugged back the rest of the glass in one go, and headed for bed, knowing damn well sleep wasn’t coming anytime soon.

Slipping under the covers, she let the cool sheets press against her skin, the familiar weight of exhaustion settling over her. She reached for her reading glasses, catching sight of the small Amazon box on the nightstand, delivered same day, while she was still in the air.

With a resigned sigh, she tore the box open and pulled out ‘Tetris: The Games People Play’ by Brian Brown, flipped to the first page, and started reading.

Chapter End Notes

i love your comments so much they genuinely help me be a better writer and also inspire me to keep writing when i get the writer's block.

Chapter 7: one player game

Chapter Notes

updated to a new character coming into the arena.

the pining in this one is set to MAX.

also, the music choices in this one are my favorites so far. i hope you enjoy these and you're learning about some new music you might've not known about already. i probably spend more time on picking the precise song to put than i do writing some of these sections.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

– –

– –

Agatha had been through breakups before. Flings that burned fast and hot and left nothing but ash. She had spent years perfecting the art of not caring, of walking away from things before they could leave any kind of permanent mark.

But Rio Vidal was different.

It wasn’t like they had history. They hadn’t spent years tangled in some grand, aching romance. They had spent three days together. Three. And yet…Agatha couldn’t shake her.

Couldn’t shake the way Rio felt in her hands, the way she moved, the way she thought…so precise, so efficient, like she had already calculated every possible outcome and still chose Agatha.

And now, Rio hadn’t messaged her in seven hours and thirteen days. Not that Agatha was counting or anything.

She tried everything to get Rio out of her system.

She poured whiskey down her throat like it might burn Rio’s name from her tongue. She took whatever people handed her at after hours bars, let her nights bleed into mornings, let neon lights blur into something hazy and weightless.

She fucked anyone who could press her into a mattress hard enough that, for a fleeting moment, she could pretend she wasn’t thinking about Rio. But it never lasted. She always found herself staring at the ceiling afterward, hollow and bored, because none of them knew how to touch her the way Rio did. Like study. Like strategy. Like she was a puzzle to be solved.

It was infuriating.

She picked fights at work. Turned in edits late just to see how much she could get away with. She told her boss she would rather die than write another esports feature, but still found herself scrolling through YouTube at two in the morning, watching old Tetris tournament footage of Rio.

She tried to keep herself busy. That was supposed to help, right?

Mornings started with coffee that was never strong enough, a half hearted attempt at skimming emails before slamming her laptop shut in frustration. Work was just something to get through, a hazy loop of meetings she barely paid attention to, and articles she edited without reading twice.

Lunches were spent at her desk, half eaten sandwiches growing stale beside her while she scrolled through news headlines she didn’t actually absorb. She chewed without tasting, heard without listening.

Evenings were worse.

She would leave the office, convinced she’d go straight home. Instead, she always ended up somewhere she shouldn’t be…a bar, a club, a classic New York late night diner that smelled like burnt coffee and bad decisions.

She let strangers buy her drinks. Let them flirt, let them talk about their jobs, and their lives and their meaningless little hobbies. Sometimes she even smiled. Sometimes she even let them take her home, never to her home though, always theirs.

She always left before sunrise, slipping out of unfamiliar apartments and hotel rooms with the taste of regret still lingering on her tongue.

She walked the city in the early morning hours, when the streets were empty and the skyline was bruised with the first hints of daylight. New York at dawn had a different rhythm, slower, less demanding. It should have been calming. It wasn’t.

She kept telling herself she was fine. That this was just another fling, another temporary distraction, and she was being ridiculous.

But then she’d be in the middle of something completely normal…writing an email, ordering an UberEats (sponsor me, please), reading a book, and Rio would sneak in. Her thoughts would drift to the way she moved. The way she listened. The way she touched Agatha like she mattered.

Anytime this would happen, she would groan, rubbing a hand down her face like that would somehow scrub the memory clean.

By the end of the second week, she gave up pretending she wasn’t a mess.

Scratchy could tell. He could always tell.

The rabbit had spent the last few days watching Agatha with something close to…no…it was exactly…disapproval. His usual judgmental stare had only gotten sharper.

“Yea, yea,” Agatha muttered, running a hand through her hair as she collapsed onto the couch. “I know. I’m pathetic. Get over it.”

Scratchy thumped a single, decisive time, as if you say ‘how down bad can one be, mom??’

Agatha groaned. “I know. I know.”

She grabbed her phone before she could stop herself, scrolling through old texts, opening her last message thread with Rio.

Still nothing. Of course.

She exhaled sharply, locking her phone and tossing it onto the coffee table like it had personally offended her.

“Fine,” she muttered, standing up. “We’re doing something else.”

Scratchy flicked an ear, surprised.

Agatha grabbed her coat. She didn’t know where she was going. She just needed to move. She walked. Past the bars she usually haunted. Past the coffee shops she used as an excuse to kill time.

She ended up in Central Park and strolled through it glossy eyed. A street musician was playing something soft on a saxophone. Not a song she recognized, slow and aching in a way that settled into her bones. She sat on a bench, pulled out her phone, and stared at Rio’s name in her contacts.

Her thumb hovered over the screen.

She could text Rio. She could say something stupid, something reckless, something that would force a response. She could just…call??? She shouldn’t.

Instead, she turned her phone off, shoved it back into her pocket, and headed back home.

She tried everything. And yet, Rio was still nagging at the edges of her mind, haunting her.

Agatha wished her mind could be wiped of Rio like a line clear in Tetris...clean, efficient, gone. But the thing about line clears? They only worked if you stacked everything perfectly…and Agatha’s mind…was a fucking mess.

– –

– –

The knock at her door was sharp, impatient, three solid raps that made Scratchy’s ears twitch from his spot on the couch. Agatha groaned, barely lifting her head from where she had been slumped over her desk, an untouched glass of whiskey beside her, her laptop open to a half written article she had no intention of finishing.

Agatha was wearing a worn out purple t-shirt, her matching shorts striped in faded lavender and gray, loose at the waistband but tight at the thighs, frayed at the hem like they’d survived multiple apartment moves and at least one breakup. Her hair was a mess, piled into a half hearted bun that had clearly been slept in, and there was a smudge of eyeliner under one eye like she’d forgotten to wash her face two nights ago and simply gave up trying.

The knocking roared again.

"Jesus Christ, I’m coming," she muttered, pushing herself up and stumbling toward the door, not even checking who was there before swinging it open.

Jen stood there, arms crossed, eyebrow raised, looking entirely unimpressed. Jen looked like she’d walked straight off the runway at Paris fashion week. She wore a structured, rose pink blazer with satin lapels over a pale fuchsia v neck that hugged her frame like a glove. Her high waisted trousers were wide legged and perfectly pressed. Gold hoops, winged eyeliner sharp enough to kill, and a sleek pair of heeled ankle boots completed the look. Her bald head gleamed under the apartment lights, her skin flawless, her expression deeply unimpressed.

Agatha exhaled. "Of course it’s you."

"Nice to see you too, asshole," Jen said, stepping inside without waiting for an invitation. She looked around, at the empty takeout containers on the counter, the unopened mail stacking up, the sheer disaster of Agatha’s current existence, and sighed. "Goddamn. This is worse than I thought."

Agatha rolled her eyes, flopping onto the couch. "Spare me the intervention speech, please."

Jen ignored her, walking into the kitchen and helping herself to a glass of water. "I mean, I could, but I feel like the visual speaks for itself. You smell like stale weed and your rabbit looks like he’s been left in the care of a deadbeat dad."

Scratchy thumped his foot against the floor in protest.

Agatha sighed, rubbing her temples. "I’m fine, Jen."

Jen snorted. "Fine people don’t disappear for two weeks, ignore all their texts, and then emerge from their apartments looking like a 19th century poet dying of consumption."

Agatha shot her a look. "You’re dramatic."

"I’m correct," Jen said, taking a sip of water and sitting on the edge of the couch, looking at Agatha like she was a puzzle missing half its pieces. "Talk to me."

Agatha groaned, sinking deeper into the couch. "About what?"

Jen didn’t answer. Just raised an eyebrow.

Agatha sighed. "I don’t know. Life? Work? The fact that I’m stuck writing about video games when I hate video games? Or the fact that, against my better judgment, I’m still thinking about someone who probably hasn’t thought about me in…" she checked the clock on her phone "...two hours and fourteen goddamn days?"

Jen’s lips twitched. "Wow…That’s specific?"

Agatha scowled. "Shut up."

Jen smirked, but then her expression softened. "Look, I don’t know who this chick is or what she did to you, but I do know you. And I know when you’re in a spiral. This?" She gestured vaguely around the apartment. "This is a spiral."

Agatha exhaled, stretching her legs out on the couch. "Yea…no shit."

Jen looked at her for a moment longer before setting her glass down on the coffee table. "Alright. Get up."

Agatha didn’t move. "Pass."

"Not optional," Jen said, standing up. "You’re putting on real clothes, and we’re going outside. Sunlight, Harkness. You remember that?"

Agatha groaned. "Jesus. You’re like an aggressive self help audiobook."

"Yea, well, someone has to be," Jen shot back, already moving toward Agatha’s bedroom like she owned the place. "I’m giving you ten minutes to get dressed, or I’m dragging you out in your ratty pajamas."

"These are not ratty," Agatha muttered, but she was already pushing herself up, stretching out the stiffness in her shoulders. She knew Jen well enough to know she wasn’t getting out of this.

Jen reappeared a second later, tossing a clean white t-shirt and a pair of jeans at her face. "Chop chop."

Agatha caught them with a glare but didn’t argue.

She threw on the clothes Jen had chosen…reluctantly admitting to herself that she did, in fact, feel slightly more human once she was dressed. When she emerged from her bedroom, Jen was leaning against the kitchen counter, scrolling through her phone like she had all the time in the world.

"Alright, what’s the plan?" Agatha asked, folding her arms.

Jen pocketed her phone. "Breakfast."

Agatha frowned. "It’s three in the afternoon."

Jen smirked. "Then brunch. Whatever. I’m getting you out of here before you start talking to your rabbit like he’s your therapist."

Agatha cast a glance toward Scratchy, who blinked back at her. "He is my therapist."

Scratchy thumped.

Jen shook her head, exasperated. "Get your shoes on."

Jen took her to a cafe a few blocks away, one of those cozy, overpriced places with baristas who looked like they had deep, personal opinions on oat milk. ‘Yuck’ Agatha groaned to herself.

She ordered black coffee and a croissant, Jen got something that involved ‘machah,’ or whatever, and they took a table by the window.

Agatha sipped her coffee in silence, staring out at the city moving around them. It was a warm day, but not stifling, people milled about in shorts and sundresses, cars honked impatiently, a street musician played something soft on an acoustic guitar. It was New York in its usual rhythm.

"You gonna tell me what happened?" Jen asked, stirring her drink.

"What do you think happened?"

Jen didn’t answer right away. She just studied Agatha, the way she had since college, like she was trying to figure out which part of the equation was missing. Finally, she said, "I think you actually liked this one."

Agatha scoffed. "Please. It was three days."

"Sure." Jen took a sip of her drink. "And you’ve been self destructing for two weeks. Totally normal."

Agatha looked away. "It’s not like that."

Jen raised an eyebrow. "So what’s it like?"

Agatha exhaled. "It’s like…I met someone who completely rewired my brain, and now she’s gone, and I don’t know how to switch it back."

Jen tilted her head, smiling big, and saying teasingly "Sounds like feelings."

Agatha groaned. "Don’t say feelings at me like that."

Jen smirked but let it drop. "You want my advice?"

"No, but you’re going to give it to me anyway."

Jen set her drink down. "You’re sitting here acting like this is over, but you haven’t even reached out…have you?"

Agatha frowned. "I shouldn’t have to."

"Maybe," Jen said. "But maybe that’s not how this one works."

Agatha stared at her coffee.

Jen leaned back in her chair. "Look, I’m not saying you need to write her some grand love letter. But maybe stop pretending like you don’t want to hear from her."

Agatha sighed, rubbing her temple. "You’re annoying."

Jen grinned. "I usually am."

They finished their coffee in comfortable silence. Outside, the city moved on.

When they got back to Agatha’s apartment, Jen paused at the door, giving her a look that was softer than before. "Just…don’t sit in your misery forever, ok?"

Agatha smirked. "That was the plan."

Jen rolled her eyes. "Dumb plan. Change it."

Agatha chuckled, shaking her head. "Yea, yea. Love you too."

Jen shot her the finger on her way out. "Always."

The second the door clicked shut, silence filled the apartment again. Agatha let her head fall back against the couch, staring up at the ceiling.

Then…silence. Agatha sat there, still as the room around her, listening to the faint hum of the city beyond her window.

Scratchy hopped onto the couch beside her, pressing his small, warm weight against her thigh. She let her fingers drift absently through his fur, grounding herself in the softness of it, in the way he always seemed to just be there.

She exhaled slowly, running a hand through her hair.

Scratchy wiggled his nose, watching her with that quiet, knowing stare.

Agatha scratched gently behind his ears. “Well…at least you never leave.”

– –

– –

Agatha had told herself she was done thinking about Rio Vidal…it was a lie, of course. Because no matter how much she worked, drank, fucked, or pretended to be too busy to care, Rio was still there, lurking in the corners of her mind like an unsolved puzzle.

Agatha could ignore it during the day, let the noise of the city drown it out, but at night…when her apartment was too quiet, when she stared at the ceiling, exhausted but restless…it was impossible to shake Rio.

Did Rio ever think about her? Had she hesitated before sending that last text? Had she typed something else and deleted it, fingers hovering over the screen like she did when calculating the perfect Tetris move?

Agatha pulled out her phone and clicked the screen on, swiping straight to her texts with Rio.

The last message burned into her brain, into the insides of her eyelids, into the goddamn fabric of her being.

“I will message you later.”

…Later was now over fifteen days ago.

Agatha had every right to be angry. Any normal person would be pissed. But Agatha couldn’t bring herself to completely feel that way…not with Rio. Because she understood. She understood that Rio operated on a different wavelength, that she didn’t process emotions the way other people did. That silence wasn’t cruelty, wasn’t a game, it was just…Rio being Rio. Slow, calculated, and careful.

And god…she couldn’t stand how Rio, in all her stupid (affectionate) idiot ways made her felt.

She stuffed her phone back into her pocket and kept walking, the city moving around her in a blur of neon reflections against wet pavement.

It was raining that night in New York.

Not an aggressive downpour, but that steady, rhythmic drizzle that made the streets slick and turned the lights into watercolor smears. The kind of rain that crept under your clothes and settled in your bones. Agatha barely noticed it.

She walked the same route home from PC Gamer, her heels splashing against the pavement, umbrella tilted just enough to keep the worst of the rain off her face. The pattering against the fabric was steady, like an unwanted metronome, matching the quiet ache in her chest. She wiped at her face and wasn’t sure if it was rain or something else.

“Fuck the diet.” she murmured. Her stomach had been running on caffeine and bad decisions for far too long.

She passed by a flickering neon. PIZZA BY THE SLICE.

She pushed open the door, stepping into the warmth of a tiny, grimy pizza joint, the kind of place that smelled like grease and regret.

The guy behind the counter barely looked up. "What you want?"

"Two slices of cheese," Agatha said, not even thinking.

A few moments later, she sat at a wobbly plastic table, folding the slices into the perfect taco shape and taking a deep, indulgent bite. The first taste of real, disgustingly good New York pizza she’d had in years. Maybe decades.

Jesus Christ. She would die if the people at Vogue saw her now. PC Gamer’s newest video game editor, shoving down pizza in a hole in the wall joint, sitting alone like a washed up old journalist. What a fall from grace.

She let out a deep, slow sigh, staring at the grease pooling on the top of her pizza.

And then…a ruckus arose from the back of the pizza joint.

A group of guys, huddled around an old video game cabinet, shouting and jeering, their voices rising over the sounds in the shop. Agatha barely glanced up at first, just another bunch of dudes acting like they were gods at a game they barely understood.

And then…

"High scores hate to see me coming."

Agatha’s breath caught in her throat.

That voice.

She turned her head. Got up from her table and moved closer as if she were in a trance.

Her heart slammed against her ribs.

And there she was.

Rio. Her Rio.

Standing at the back of a seedy New York pizza joint, joystick in hand, absolutely destroying whatever poor bastard had challenged her.

Agatha felt like she had been thrown into a fever dream.

Rio was the same, but also...somehow not.

She was dressed in an oversized hoodie, black with big block white letters on it reading “your mom also asks me for extra quarters” on the front of it, with a big quarter graphic behind it. Her the sleeves pushed up to her elbows in that half careless, half calculated way. Paired with black jeans, and chuck taylors. She still wore that stupid white glove on her right hand, fingers twitching at the game console with precise movements as she played. She still had that hyper focused expression, like the rest of the world didn’t exist beyond the screen.

But she was here…in New York….what the fuck was Rio doing in New York?

Agatha barely processed it before she was moving, her body acting before her brain caught up. She pushed past an empty chair, closer to the arcade machine, closer to Rio.

Rio’s opponent, a guy in his mid 20s, wearing a hoodie that had definitely not been washed in at least a month, was leaning forward, gripping his controller like he was physically trying to force the game to go in his favor. It wasn’t.

Rio, meanwhile, looked like she was barely trying.

"You’ve got to be fucking kidding me," the guy groaned as Rio executed some impossible move in Street Fighter.

"Dude…how could you not account for the A-B-B-LEFT-LEFT-B-LEFT stick and button pattern," Rio said matter of factly, her voice even, as she slammed in the final move. Game over.

The screen flashed. RIO #1.

The guy threw up his hands. "Who even are you?"

Rio adjusted her glove. "Someone who wins."

Agatha snorted. The sound came out before she could stop it, sharp and loud enough that Rio froze. Her shoulders stiffened.

Then…slowly, like she was processing a glitch in the system…she turned.

Their eyes met.

Agatha felt her pulse skitter.

Rio blinked once. Twice. Her lips parted slightly, and for the first time in the entire time Agatha had known her, she looked genuinely caught off guard.

"Agatha?"

Her name on Rio’s lips did something stupid to her chest.

It lodged itself between her ribs, sharp and unexpected, sent a pulse of something dangerously close to relief straight through her veins. Like she had been holding her breath for weeks without realizing it. Like all the frustration, the silence, the unanswered questions had been temporarily overridden by one simple, undeniable fact…

Agatha missed Rio.

Chapter End Notes
