Chapter 8: a hidden level

Chapter Notes

updated tags.

this chapter, was originally, not supposed to happen, but the more i sat thinking about it, i just HAD to write it.

now the story as plotted on my beat board, takes 12 chapters to tell. will it go longer? time will tell ;)

here it is, chapter's 6 and 7, but, what on earth was rio doing that whole time? here it is, a whole chapter, from rio's perspective.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

– –

– –

– –

Rio moved with the same precision she brought to everything in life, packing, planning, executing.

She tapped a few buttons on her phone, officially checking out of the Pasadena Airbnb, then turned to survey her belongings. Three rubbermaid bins. That was it. The entirety of her possessions. Some people would call it sparse, maybe even depressing. To Rio, it was optimized. Unnecessary things only slowed her down. Possessions, nostalgia, clutter, it was all inefficient.

She carried the bins to her car in two trips, packing them neatly into the back of her 2002 white Subaru Outback. Each one nestled against the other like perfectly placed tetriminos, no wasted space. Satisfied, she returned to the Airbnb one last time, scanning the now empty space for anything left behind. Nothing. Perfect.

The last thing she carried out was Agatha’s book. It wasn’t packed away with everything else. She placed it in the passenger seat instead, buckling the seatbelt over it with the same care someone else might give to a living, breathing person. Rio sat in the driver’s seat and, without thinking, reached over, running her fingers along the worn cover, and giving it a small, appreciative pat. It was only logical she thought to herself. The book was important. A gift. A piece of Agatha.

– –

– –

Was Rio a good driver? That depended on who you asked. According to the law? Absolutely not. According to anyone who had ever played Sega’s Outrun competitively? She drove like she was trying to set a new record, because, in her mind, she was.

The drive from Pasadena, California (34°09'35.0"N 118°07'42.9"W) to Des Moines, Iowa (41°36'57.8"N 93°40'10.3"W) was estimated at 26 hours, minimum, for the average driver. Rio was not the average driver, working off exact GPS coordinates helped know her exact target. She had researched the optimal speed traps along the route for police to be. Knew exactly where law enforcement tended to camp out, where she could push the speedometer past 100 mph and where she had to reluctantly drop below 80. Her Outback could handle the strain. She had tested it before. The limit was 110 mph before gas mileage became inefficient. If she executed perfectly, she could make it in 20 hours. A true speed run.

And so, with a case of a dozen RedBull on the floor of the passenger’s seat, she drove.

The road stretched out endlessly before her, the hum of the engine a steady background noise, but Rio’s mind was elsewhere. She replayed every moment with Agatha with near photographic clarity. Every glance, every smirk, every sharp witted remark. The way Agatha had pulled her in with words alone, like she was playing some long form strategy game that only she knew the rules to.

With Agatha, Rio didn’t have to mask. She didn’t have to script her responses or rehearse her facial expressions. She didn’t have to filter her thoughts into something more normal or palatable. The exhausting, constant performance she gave the rest of the world, the calculated eye contact, the adjusted tone, the learned rhythm of small talk, it all dropped away around her. Agatha didn’t flinch at the bluntness. She didn’t misread the silences. She met Rio exactly where she was, sharp edges and all, and somehow, for the first time in her life, Rio felt like herself was enough.

For the first three hours, she told herself she wasn’t thinking about Agatha. For the next seventeen, she gave up lying to herself.

At hour twelve, somewhere on a dark, empty stretch of Utah highway, Rio reached into her bag and pulled out her rose toy. Logic had long since abandoned her somewhere outside the Nevada border. Restraint? Left to melt in the desert along with any pretense of emotional regulation. She hadn’t even hesitated, just one hand on the wheel, the other pulling the toy from her laptop bag like it was an essential tool in an emergency kit.

At hour twelve and two minutes, she groaned. Just a low, guttural noise that vibrated in her throat. The orgasm had hit her fast, as if her body had finally broken through some internal dam. Sharp. Relentless. Completely inconvenient.

At hour twelve and five minutes, she came again. Her thighs twitched, her legs locked, and she barely managed to keep the car centered in the lane. Her entire body vibrated, overstimulated and buzzing with something dangerously close to euphoric chaos.

At hour twelve and fifteen minutes, she glanced down at the seat beneath her. “I’m gonna have to get this detailed,” she muttered, saying in an almost apologetic tone to the seat as an ‘I’m so sorry.’

By hour thirteen, the rose toy gave out…its motor wheezing out one last pitiful hum before dying in her hand. Rio, unfazed, just flung it out her window as she sped outside Vail, Colorado. “Rest in pieces,” she said, deadpan.

Rio was nothing if not adaptable. If a tool failed, you used your hands. She could win Tetris tournaments with a busted controller. This was no different. In her mind, she told herself it was “tournament endurance training,” a ridiculous mental justification that made her feel better about the fact that she was, in fact, masturbating her way across the great American highway system like a woman possessed.

At hour fourteen, promptly after stopping to gas up her car, and downing 3 RedBulls in a row, she peeled off her shorts and underwear with all the efficiency of someone who’d had enough. They were flung into the backseat. Naked from the waist down, Rio sat back into the seat, sighed, and nodded, “Optimal.”

Every time her mind settled for even a second, it filled with Agatha.

Agatha standing over her in bed, that smirk like a challenge.

Agatha dragging her nails down Rio’s spine, murmuring something only half legible through the haze.

The way Agatha had growled "You really don’t hesitate, do you?" after Rio had pinned her against the wall in that alleyway, hands steady, eyes hungry.

By hour fifteen, she was drenched in sweat. Her jaw locked tight, her lip raw from being bitten. Her hands trembled, her calf cramped from the gas pedal, but she didn’t stop. She couldn’t stop.

Agatha’s voice was in her ear. Agatha’s breath was against her throat. The exact sound Agatha made when Rio’s hands finally mapped her like a level she’d been training for all her life filled every space in Rio’s ear.

By hour sixteen, Rio muttered to herself, “At what point does this become a medical concern?”

At hour seventeen, she determined the answer was probably four hours ago.

At hour eighteen, she tried to focus. But even her brain betrayed her, usually a tight algorithm of logic and direction, she started lagging. The numbers blurred. Landmarks lost meaning. Her grip on the steering wheel, white knuckled and desperate, was the only thing keeping her tethered to reality.

Her hands on the steering wheel felt useless. They weren’t wrapped around Agatha’s hips. They weren’t tracing the line of her ribs. The gearshift didn’t feel like her thigh.

At hour nineteen, Rio exhaled a long, shaky breath. “This is getting ridiculous,” she muttered, voice dry, cracked from dehydration.

And then, finally, at hour twenty, she rolled into Beaverdale, Des Moines, Iowa. 2404 39th St., a new speed run record, according to Rio.

– –

– –

– –

She pulled into the driveway and reached into the back seat to retrieve her discarded underwear and shorts, slipping them on before stepping out of the car. Her legs ached from being in the same position for nearly a full day, and she stretched slowly, savoring the relief. The night air was cool against her overheated skin, a welcome contrast as she grabbed her bins and made her way to the front door.

Home…if it could still be called that.

The house was small. One story, dark forest green, an old bungalow style home, no more than 800 square feet. It had been her father’s house, left to her after he passed away four months before the tournament. She had been trying to decide what to do with it ever since. Sell it? Stay? Sell it? Stay? She could never quite land on an answer.

Inside, the house was just as she had left it. Sterile. Too clean, like a staged house nobody actually lived in.

The futon, her couch and bed in one, was perfectly positioned against the far wall, a single pillow and blanket folded neatly on top.

A TV and a Tetris gaming rig sat in front of the living room window, the only piece of technology in the house besides her laptop.

The kitchen was small and compact. One table, one chair. The drawers were empty. The cabinets held nothing. On the counter sat exactly one fork, one spoon, one knife, one bowl, and one plate. Nothing more.

…Not anymore.

Rio dropped the bins by the door and collapsed onto the futon with a heavy sigh, sprawling out and staring at the ceiling. The silence settled over her like a blanket. Too heavy. Too familiar.

And then, as if summoned by silence itself, Agatha filled her mind like a glitch in the system.

Her voice. Her sharp tongue. Her smirk. Her hands. The way she moved. The way she touched. The way she looked at Rio, like she was something worth slowing down for.

Rio clenched her jaw, breathing slowly through her nose. Control it. She could control this. She had always been able to control what went through her mind, shift her focus, optimize her time. She could compartmentalize grief, anxiety, hunger, fatigue, turn them off, bury them under strategy and timing.

But Agatha? Agatha was chaos. Uncontrollable. Unpredictable. She was like a bug in Rio’s code. The kind that didn’t crash the system outright but lingered, replicating slowly, corrupting line after line until nothing worked the way it was supposed to, and still…Rio didn’t want to delete it.

She sat up, spine straightening automatically, eyes landing on the three bins in the corner. Her gaze flickered to the three bins stacked in the corner, and on top, staring back at her like it knew, was Agatha’s book. Rio exhaled sharply, stood up, crossed the room, and picked it up.

She ran her fingers over the cover before flipping it open, her mind already shifting gears, preparing to analyze, to dismantle it piece by piece like a difficult game mechanic she was determined to master. She settled in, curling up on the futon, eyes scanning the lines where she left off reading.

Her eyes read each line, pulling apart sentences like code, identifying structure and argument, tone and intention. She read slowly. Carefully. Like she always did. But something felt different. Because for the first time in what felt like years…this reading wasn’t for optimization. This wasn’t for a score. This wasn’t to be the best at something, she was reading just to understand.

This was the first time, in a long time, that she had chosen to read in her free time. Not play Tetris. Not practice. Not optimize. Just…read.

And for a moment, Rio hesitated, gripping the book’s spine just a little tighter.

Because choosing to read instead of play…choosing to feel instead of win? That? That was almost more terrifying than the fact that she was still thinking about Agatha Harkness.

– –

– –

Rio had been staring at her phone screen for an embarrassingly long time, without replying.

‘Thinking of you too.’

She typed it. Stared at it. Deleted it. Too direct. No context. No immediate justification for sending it. Her fingers hovered over the keyboard, recalibrating. The first attempt had been a miscalculation. Emotional without purpose. No structure. No expected response. She needed a new approach. She tried again.

RIO: This book is impossible.

That was better. A fact. A neutral statement. Something that required a response but didn't leave her vulnerable. Rio phone buzzed almost immediately.

AGATHA: There’s worse out there.

Rio frowned, tapping the edge of her phone against her chin. Statistically, yes. But I have no frame of reference for bad books because I don’t read bad books.

She typed it out and hit send without overthinking.

A pause. Three dots appeared.

AGATHA: That’s the most pretentious thing you’ve ever said.

Rio blinked. That was…not the reaction she had anticipated.

Her mind whirred, running a quick mental check through past conversations with Agatha, searching for a response that had been more pretentious than this one. Nothing stood out. She frowned.

RIO: That’s unlikely.

AGATHA: Oh? You got an example of something worse?

Rio hesitated. If she had been keeping track of her own statements in a more structured way, she could reference a past example. She had not. That was an oversight.

RIO: If I did, I would’ve made a list. This one being first.

She stared at the message after she sent it, wondering if that was the right thing to say. Agatha liked snark. She responded well to it. That was an observable pattern. A few seconds passed before Agatha’s next response came in.

AGATHA: Jesus Christ. Okay, book snob. What’s so impossible about it?

Rio exhaled, relieved. Not a failure. She could keep going.

RIO: The logic is inconsistent. Malcolm’s framing of journalism ethics in the opening is contradicted by how she engages with her own subjects later.

That was the actual reason she found the book difficult. She had considered this thoroughly, marked pages, even cross referenced previous works. It was a valid critique.

AGATHA: …are…are you reading my book like it’s a fucking math proof?

Rio hesitated. Was she? That seemed…plausible.

RIO: …Should I not be?

Three dots. A longer pause.

AGATHA: No, Vidal. It’s literary journalism, not a goddamn algorithm.

RIO: That’s unfortunate. It would be better if all books were like an algorithm.

That was something she meant, but the moment she sent it, she realized how it sounded. Agatha’s response came instantly.

AGATHA: I hate you.

Rio’s chest clenched. She responded without thinking.

RIO: No, you don’t.

Another pause. The longest one yet. Her fingers twitched.

AGATHA: …you’re right.

Rio exhaled, the tension in her shoulders easing. She had been right. She liked being right. She especially liked being right about Agatha. She stared at the words, running them over in her mind, calculating the exact weight of them. It felt…good. An unknown kind of good. A distracting kind of good. That was dangerous. Her mind struggled with it, tried to find a box to fit it into. Agatha was a variable she had not accounted for properly. She threw off the numbers. She was a puzzle that didn’t fit into neat rows.

Rio’s fingers hovered over the keyboard. Try something else.

RIO: What are you doing?

The response came slower this time.

AGATHA: Existing.

That answer made no sense.

RIO: That seems inefficient.

AGATHA: And yet, here I am.

A flicker of something unfamiliar twisted in Rio’s chest. She didn’t understand it, but she knew she wanted to keep talking. To keep Agatha talking to her.

RIO: Unoptimized.

AGATHA: Deliberately.

Rio’s pulse spiked. That wasn’t the kind of response she was used to. That was deliberate. That was flirting. Was she supposed to say something back? What was the appropriate response? She didn’t have a script for this. Her fingers tapped anxiously against the screen. She sat on the edge of her futon.

RIO: …I like that.

It was the first thing that felt completely uncalculated. She meant it. She stared at the screen, waiting. Waiting. She hated waiting. She wasn’t used to it. She was used to immediate feedback, to knowing what the next move was. The response finally came.

AGATHA: You would.

Rio swallowed. The unfamiliar feeling curling tighter in her chest.

She had studied patterns her entire life, analyzed data, found efficiencies where others couldn’t. But Agatha? Agatha was a glitch. A beautiful, maddening, unsolvable glitch. She wanted to figure her out. She wanted to win her. Rio’s fingers hovered over the keyboard. She wanted to try flurting more with her.

RIO: I bet you sleep at illogical intervals.

There. Something pointed. Something to force a response.

AGATHA: That’s a complicated topic.

Rio frowned.

RIO: It’s a binary one. Yes or no.

AGATHA: Then no.

Rio’s heart jumped.

RIO: That’s suboptimal.

AGATHA: And yet, here I am.

Rio’s grip on her phone tightened.

RIO: That’s the second time you’ve said that.

AGATHA: Maybe I like the way it sounds.

Something about that made Rio feel warm. A different kind of warm than usual. It was a feeling she couldn’t quantify, and that made her nervous. But she didn’t want it to stop.

RIO: Noted.

Agatha’s response came slower this time. Almost thoughtful.

AGATHA: You always like collecting data on me, Vidal?

‘Yes’ Rio said to herself.

RIO: I collect data on everything.

AGATHA: Right. But some data sets interest you more than others.

Rio hesitated. This was a trap. A direct challenge. She should redirect. She should say something neutral. Instead…

RIO: …Yes…when it comes to you.

She felt the pause on the other end. The weight of it. Her heart pounded against her ribs. She was bad at this. She wasn’t built for flirting. She wasn’t built for people like Agatha…clever, sharp, fast, and unpredictable. But god she wanted Agatha to like her.

AGATHA: So what you’re saying is…you’re interested in me?

This was the moment where Rio could backtrack. Where she could clarify, change the wording, fix the mistake.

RIO: …That is an accurate statement.

The pause was the longest yet. Rio felt hot. Unsteady. She clenched her jaw, trying to regulate her breathing, to not care how Agatha would respond. Her phone vibrated.

AGATHA: I win.

Rio blinked. What?

RIO: That depends on how you define winning.

AGATHA: Doesn’t matter. You still lost.

No. No, that wasn’t right.

RIO: …I disagree.

AGATHA: Too late. It’s already on the leaderboard.

Rio exhaled sharply, trying to slow her thoughts. She laid down on the futon.

RIO: …There is no leaderboard.

AGATHA: Oh, I’m definitely keeping score.

Three dots. A long pause. Rio swallowed. Her fingers trembled slightly as she typed.

RIO: I think I like your game.

Rio locked her phone and exhaled deeply.

The tightness in her chest didn’t fully disappear, but it loosened. Just a fraction. Just enough. And finally, exhaustion took over and Rio fell into the deepest sleep of her life.

– –

– –

– –

Rio woke the next morning to stillness. The air in the house was stale, unmoved from the night before, as if it hadn’t even bothered circulating while she slept. She sat up slowly on the futon, spine cracking, joints stiff, and rubbed a hand over her face like she could scrub the fog out of her brain.

She inhaled deeply, held it, then exhaled through her nose, steady, methodical. It was her routine. Calibrate. Reset. Assess.

She checked her watch, 11:17am. She scanned the room without moving more than her head. Everything was in its place. Nothing had changed. The same precision. The same stillness. And yet, something inside her felt...different. Off kilter.

There was no checklist yet for the day. No list of objectives. But her brain was already assembling one without her permission.

Step one: food. Probably.

Step two: a shower. Maybe.

Step three: the future. She blinked once. Nodded to herself.

“Alright,” she murmured softly to the silence.

The plan was forming. It didn’t look like what she was used to. It didn’t feel efficient. It didn’t even have clear outcomes.

But it started with movement.

And it would end when she got to New York.

Her fingers moved before she could second guess it, dialing a number she had saved for months but never actually intended to use.

The real estate agent picked up on the second ring. "Miss Vidal! I was hoping to hear from you…"

"Sell the house. Today."

A stunned pause. Then, barely contained excitement. "A-Are you sure? I mean, I can start the process immediately, but we’d have to…"

"I’ll be there in an hour to sign what you need me to" she says, cutting him off, already pacing the room, already moving on to the next step.

Overjoyed, he agrees. She hangs up.

This was happening.

The house was a relic. A place she had once needed but no longer did. The thought of keeping it had felt logical, but logic had its flaws. Efficiency did not mean permanence. This house had been nothing but a holding pattern.

And now? Now she had something, someone, pulling her forward.

She moved to the closet, pulling out a massive green duffel bag, easily twice her size in length and height.

Three bins of belongings stare back at her. Too much.

Rio kneeled as she tore open the bins, eyes scanning, sorting, compartmentalizing into two piles, keep or donate.

She moved fast, stripping her life down to what matters, no excess, no sentimentality. Clothes go first, simple, neutral, foldable. Tournament essentials, gloves, custom controllers, a spare joystick.

Books. Too many books. She hesitates, fingers grazing over them, before setting most aside to donate. No attachments. Just function.

Her laptop and charger. The tool that connects her to everything, went into her laptop backpack.

A single framed photo, her and her dad, from many years ago, standing in front of a Tetris regional championship banner. She stared at it for half a second longer than necessary before slipping it into her laptop bag.

Everything else? Donate.

The living room turned into an assembly line, belongings shifting in and out of piles like Tetris pieces in a speedrun. The donation stack grew. The duffel bag filled.

Every inch of space was optimized, each fold precise, each item placed with calculated intent. No wasted space. She Tetris'd all of her belongings perfectly.

Twenty seven minutes later, Rio zipped up the duffel bag, threw her laptop bag over one shoulder, and surveyed the room.

‘It’s empty of possessions that mattered’ she thought to herself.

She should feel something. A sense of loss, maybe. A whisper of hesitation. But all she feels is the weight of Agatha’s book in her hand. She looks down at it, tracing the cover with her thumb. She had spent hours poring over it, dissecting its structure, unraveling its logic, trying to understand what made the book Agatha’s choice for her.

She exhaled, put the book in her laptop bag and adjusted the straps. She moves her stuff to her Subaru, then one more pass around the house for good measure, touching walls that once held fond memories. As she turned off the porch light and locked the door behind her. She didn’t look back.

She got in her car again after meeting with the real estate agent and started driving. The route from Des Moines to New York City was a clean 17 hour drive. Nothing compared to the endurance gauntlet she’d pulled the other day. On paper, this was easy.

But twenty minutes into the trip, as the flat Midwestern roads stretched out endlessly ahead of her, Rio did something she almost never did…she second guessed herself.

The thought startled her. She never second guessed herself. Not with her thoughts, not with games, not with anything. But this? Dropping everything and chasing a woman she’d only spent three days with? A woman who made her brain short circuit and her pulse stumble? A woman who made her feel seen without effort and accepted without performance?

That was chaos. That was Agatha.

It was stupid, really. Abandoning structure and abandoning predictability, for someone she barely knew. She’d had other relationships. Casual ones. Calculated ones. Ones where she masked and mirrored and adapted because that’s what people did, especially when they didn’t understand why she never made eye contact or didn’t know what to say when someone cried.

But Agatha had been different. She’d called Rio out. Teased her. Stared her down. Agatha hadn’t treated her like a problem to solve. She had looked at Rio like she was already the answer. And for someone like Rio, who had built her entire life on precision and logic and cool emotional distance, that kind of magnetism wasn’t just rare. It was once in a lifetime.

So she kept driving.

The second guessing didn’t go away. But she didn’t stop either. Because deep down, something in her gut kept insisting that this was right. That maybe, just maybe, Agatha felt it too. And even if she didn’t, even if Rio got to New York and found nothing but silence waiting for her, at least she would know. At least she would have tried…and maybe that was the most logical decision of all.

For better or for worse, she was about to find out. And if it all fell apart? If the whole thing went sideways and she crashed and burned in a city too big and loud and strange for someone like her? Well, there were more arcades in New York than anywhere else in the country. She’d be fine.

– –

– –

If Rio Vidal could turn around right now, she would.

If she had any impulse control when it came to Agatha Harkness, she would have stayed in Des Moines, optimized her life, and gone about her precisely calculated routines. It would have been the logical thing to do. The smart thing to do.

Instead, she was standing in a dimly lit mechanic’s garage at a rest stop, roughly 30 minutes outside of Chicago. The mechanic kicked at the front tire of her Subaru like it had personally offended him.

"Yea, this thing’s done," he said, wiping grease off his hands. "How’d you even make it this far?"

Rio exhaled, "I maintained it regularly."

The mechanic snorted. "You maintain it regularly, but you didn’t notice the radiator was about to explode?"

Rio stared at him. She didn’t like being called out.

The man sighed, glancing at her car like it was a dying animal. "Look, I’ll give you four hundred bucks for it. Scrap it for parts."

Rio hesitated. She had planned for contingencies, but not this.

"Fine," she said, handing him the keys. "Keep it."

She took the cash, stuffed it into her pocket, and called a Lyft to the bus terminal in Chicago…which was as expensive as the money she just made from selling off her car.

As she sat in the Lyft, she started recalculating everything in her head. This was not optimal. Nothing about this was optimal.

She unlocked her phone, her fingers moving before she could think about it too hard. Opened her texts. Stared at Agatha’s name, the last thread still sitting there, waiting.

"I will message you later."

She sent it before she could overanalyze, locked her phone, and kept moving.

That should have been the end of it. She had delivered an update. Efficient. Informative. But for some reason, her hand twitched toward her pocket like she wanted to check for a response. She clenched her fist. No. That wasn’t part of the plan.

She had a goal. Get to New York. That was all that mattered.

‘God…how could this get any worse’ she thought to herself. Oh, she had no idea.

– –

– –

– –

Rio had never been on a Greyhound bus before.

She had also never sat next to a man eating an entire rotisserie chicken with his bare hands before. It was impossible to ignore. The smell alone was assaulting her senses. He ripped off pieces of meat like a feral animal, his fingers glistening with grease, occasionally licking them before going back for more. Rio’s eye twitched.

She adjusted herself to move further away from the chicken man. ‘This is why I’m only into women’ she thought to herself ‘they’re at least reasonable, clean, and well kept…’

She shifted her weight and kept to herself reading Agatha’s book. Occasionally, staring out the window, willing herself to focus on anything else.

This was going to be a long trip. 20 hours of this Rio thought…oy vey…

Somewhere around the first hour, a baby started screaming. Not a normal, fussy baby cry. No, this was an otherworldly, demonic wail that pierced through every other sound on the bus, echoing in Rio’s skull like a critical error alarm.

As if on cue to over stimulate her senses more, a woman behind her began to loudly break up with her boyfriend over speakerphone. "You ain't shit, Braderick!" she shouted, her voice carrying over the entire bus. "You got a whole baby I ain't know about?!"

Rio exhaled through her nose, fingers flexing against the cover of Agatha’s book in her lap. Increasing the volume on her headphones past the acceptable level, dying for the sound of her music to drown out the bus. This was inefficient. This was unbearable. This was hell.

A whole two bus breakdowns and 6 hour delay at a later, they finally made it to Cleveland. A typical 7 hour journey, 5 if you’re Rio driving, now had taken her twice as long…Rio was furious.

Then Mike happened.

Mike, who sat next to her without even asking.

Mike, who talked on the phone obnoxiously like he had never once considered shutting up.

Mike, who was a half punk, half feral raccoon, with bleached tips and an oversized Ramones hoodie on.

Mike, who had no sense of personal space or manners, suddenly, once he was done with his phone call, blurted out to Rio, "You look like someone who either solves murders in their free time or just committed one."

Rio blinked. "That…is an unhelpful statement."

Mike threw his head back and laughed. "Oh…I like you."

Rio did not like Mike…at least, not at first.

But then, as the bus trudged its way across state lines, something strange happened.

Mike started talking about Billy.

Billy, his punk rock, leather wearing, chaos gremlin of a boyfriend who was waiting for him in New York. Billy, had a tattoo of a possum wielding a switchblade and had once thrown a Molotov cocktail at a cop car (allegedly). Billy, who had broken into a zoo at three in the morning just to see if the penguins were awake.

Rio listened. Because it was data.

And Mike, for all his relentless chatter, was...fascinating. Against all logic, Mike became her travel companion.

By the time they reached Pittsburgh, Rio had been propositioned by Mike that there was an arcade there that she just had to try. Not just any arcade, according to him, but the retro arcade ever, some place he swore would blow her mind. Rio, still exhausted from the cramped bus ride had initially planned to nod politely, offer a mechanical excuse, and stay on schedule. But Rio just grabbed her duffel, slung her laptop bag over her shoulder, and followed him out of the bus like it was part of the plan. Even though it absolutely wasn’t.

The arcade was nestled between a laundromat and a donut shop in a part of the city that looked like it had been stuck in the 1980s and liked it that way. The sign out front was a glowing, buzzing neon relic "RUST BELT GAMES" and inside was a blast of sound and color so overstimulating it nearly sent Rio reeling. Pinball machines lined the far wall. Rows of classic cabinets stretched into the dim space like a cathedral built for joystick worship. The carpet was sticky. The air smelled like nacho cheese and teenage sweat. It was horrible.

Mike was already gone to the bar the moment the door opened, disappearing into the crowd like a sugar high goblin. Rio lingered at the entrance for a second longer, letting her eyes adjust, letting her brain settle.

She didn't need to look. Her body naturally scanned the room the way it always did, locating patterns, determining sound rhythms, mapping lights, organizing chaos into something she could understand. And there, tucked between a Galaga machine and a busted Time Crisis unit, was the holy grail.

An Atari Tetris cabinet.

It was older, the screen slightly dimmed, buttons worn down by thousands of frantic fingers, but Rio’s pulse quickened all the same. Her fingers itched. Without thinking, she walked straight to it, slid in a quarter, and rested her palm lightly on the controls.

She didn’t even need to warm up. Her brain had already switched modes. In her head, blocks were falling. Pieces rotated before they even appeared. Her eyes scanned four moves ahead before the first tetromino even hit the screen.

And for fifteen minutes, everything disappeared. The exhaustion. The journey. The noise. The restlessness. There was just her, the screen, and the rhythm of the precision of Tetris.

It wasn’t until the fourth high score screen popped up with "RIO" at the top that she noticed Mike standing behind her, holding a large Slurpee like a proud parent at a middle school talent show.

“You’re so fucking cool it makes me furious,” he said.

Rio blinked at the cup, then at Mike. “...thank…you?”

“You know,” he said, slurping loudly from his straw, “if Billy ever leaves me for a woman, I really hope it’s you.”

Rio turned back to the machine. “That would be statistically unlikely.”

“Love that for you,” Mike said, patting her on the back and wandering off again to harass the air hockey table.

Rio stayed a little longer, playing a few more rounds, her brain finally quiet. But even in that small window of stillness, she found herself wishing, just for a second, that Agatha could see her now. That she could watch her play. That she’d smirk and say something sharp and clever that would make Rio want to win even harder.

She sighed and moved through the arcade to find Mike to continue the journey to New York.

– –

– –

"Let's just hitch a ride," Mike said, hands in his pockets.

"That is unsafe," Rio stated.

Mike shrugged. "So is trusting public transportation, don’t cha think?"

Rio considered this and agreed.

Mike found them a ride in twenty minutes.

A U-Haul? No. It used to be a U-Haul, but now it was some kind of post apocalyptic party van held together by spray paint, bad decisions, and the sheer will of two stoners named Chad and Lenny. The dividing wall between the cab and the cargo area had been sawed clean off with what Rio could only assume was either a chainsaw or raw optimism. A few windows had been haphazardly carved into the sides, uneven, jagged, and definitely not regulation, and the outside was covered in layers of graffiti like it had parked too long in a skate park and been claimed by feral teens.

Rio stared at it for a long, long moment and thought, ‘This vehicle is not street legal. This vehicle is not parking lot legal. This vehicle might not even be physics legal.’

Chad and Lenny, insisted they were just moving "vintage furniture" but whose entire truck smelled like a dispensary exploded inside of it. Mike didn’t care but Rio recalculated her life choices in real time.

The ride was long. Painfully long. A slow crawl across Pennsylvania and New Jersey that felt like someone had stretched both states into infinity just to test Rio’s will to survive. The “U-Haul” rumbled like it was on its last legs, every bump in the road rattling straight through the frame and into Rio’s bones. The smell of stale weed, spilled energy drinks, and something Rio could only describe as ‘vintage mildew’ clung to the air like an oppressive fog.

Chad, the driver, was a beanie wearing string bean of a man who spoke almost exclusively in abstract philosophies and half baked culinary metaphors. He had taken it upon himself to explain to Rio, at length and with increasing passion, the "spiritual properties of pizza rolls."

“They’re, like…tiny flavor portals, man,” Chad said, eyes wide, hands flailing within inches of the steering wheel. “You bite in, and boom, parallel dimension. Pure nostalgia. That’s astral projection, baby.”

Rio stared at him, expression neutral, mentally calculating how hard it would take to roll out of the moving truck and survive the impact. “That is an objectively false interpretation of both portals and food.”

With a sudden purpose, she pulled on a pair of shorts from her bag, slipped on her Birkenstocks...socks and all...and grabbed her phone, messaging back something with quick, efficient movements. She was moving with a sense of purpose now, like she suddenly had somewhere to be, her usual methodical pace giving way to something slightly rushed.

"Yea," she said, still glancing at her phone, "I got a message from...someone. I really have to call them back. Do some paperwork. Maybe at a Starbucks?"

She was already gathering her stuff, grabbing her laptop bag, moving toward the door with a kind of absentminded focus that made Agatha frown slightly.

Agatha sat back, watching her. "Rio...are we going to talk about what happened?"

Rio paused mid motion, fingers still tapping at her phone. She hesitated for half a second before answering, her voice careful. "Maybe later."

Agatha’s brow furrowed. "Maybe later…"

Rio tapped at the screen again, calling a Lyft. "Yea..kust...there's something I have to do first."

Agatha studied her, trying to read between the lines of Rio’s perfectly even tone, the way she wasn’t making direct eye contact now. She could feel it...something shifting, some internal calculation Rio was making that Agatha wasn’t privy to.

Rio finally looked up at her, expression unreadable. "Can I leave my other bag here?" she asked instead. "I’ll come back for it later, when you’re done with work."

Agatha’s stomach twisted, but she forced herself to nod. "Ok."

Rio nodded too, like she was confirming something to herself, then slung her bag over her shoulder and stepped toward the door.

The moment the door closed behind her, Agatha sat still, staring at it for a long beat, unsure of how to process what had just happened. Agatha was always the first one to leave, but this time, it was someone else. The roles were reversed now, and for the first time, she wondered if this was what it had felt like when she had left Rio.

She should have expected this. Rio had her quirks. Had her own way of doing things. She didn’t owe Agatha an immediate conversation, didn’t owe her a breakdown of whatever thoughts were running through that perfectly calibrated brain of hers.

And yet…the way she had left so suddenly, the way she had brushed past what had just happened between them like it was something that could be put away, filed neatly for later...it unsettled her.

Agatha clenched her jaw, shaking her head. She didn’t know what Rio was doing. Didn’t know why it seemed important enough for her to leave like that.

But she knew one thing for certain...She wasn’t going to meddle, not right now anyways.

So instead, she finished her coffee, got dressed, and went to work.

And Rio, off to Starbucks to finish the paperwork to sell her house in Iowa.
