Chapter 10: stacking up nicely

Chapter Notes

updated tags.

this chapter is my longest one yet. it's VERY ambitious. there's a lot of ground we cover here plotwise.

my poor beta reader's review: "i cried like 5 times at this chapter you bitch."

buckle up?

See the end of the chapter for more notes

– –

Agatha waltzed into the office at PC Gamer like she had just won a championship of her own. Head high, lips curled into a smug little smile, wearing confidence on the collar of her tailored light blue suit she was wearing. Her heels clicked with purpose across the floor, her hair freshly styled, the faintest hint of sandalwood perfume trailing behind her.

She didn’t look like someone who’d spent the previous night in an emotional tornado. She didn’t look like someone who had spent weeks unraveling at the seams. No, she looked composed. Self aware. Powerful. Energized in a way that made people glance up from their desks and blink. Twice.

Not that she’d ever say why. Not that she’d admit Rio Vidal had anything to do with it. That would be too much. Too real. Too vulnerable.

Still, the moment she passed her boss’s office, he did a double take so sharp it nearly gave him whiplash.

“Hark…ness?”

Agatha dropped her bags onto her desk with more enthusiasm than necessary and dropped into her chair, booting up her laptop.

Her boss knocked on her door and gave her a look. Suspicious. Almost offended. “You’re…early.”

Agatha raised a brow, already half into her email tabs. “Barely.”

“You are never early.”

She shrugged. “Maybe I had a really good cup of coffee.”

He narrowed his eyes at her like she’d just committed a felony. “Where from? Don’t say Dunkin Donuts. Please don’t say Dunkin Donuts.”

“Seriously? Relax…” Agatha said, waving a hand at him. “You act like I’m usually crawling in here at noon in yesterday’s eyeliner.”

He opened his mouth, then shut it. The thing was she was usually crawling in here at noon in yesterday’s eyeliner. Or sunglasses. Or half a hangover. Agatha Harkness didn’t do early. Or brighteyed…or…whatever the hell this was.

Agatha threw herself into her work for the first time in weeks. Her fingers flying across the keyboard, she carved through her inbox with surgical precision, responding to flagged emails, killing passive voice with a vengeance, and leaving inline comments that were somehow both brutally honest and weirdly motivational.

She edited two pieces before noon. Didn’t even mutter once about the writer’s complete inability to differentiate between “affect” and “effect.” She sat through back to back meetings without rolling her eyes once. Not even during the one where her coworker tried to pitch an article titled “How Fortnite Saved My Marriage.”

She didn’t threaten to set Slack on fire. Didn’t passive aggressively rename the shared Google Drive folder “the abyss.” Didn’t even put her AirPods in when the sales team got too loud.

Her coworkers noticed. Of course they noticed. Her intern even tip toed up to her desk, gently placing a sandwich, and iced coffee beside her keyboard with all the caution of someone approaching a lion mid nap.

"Uh…a peace offering…" they said, half joking, half fearful.

Agatha blinked at them. “For what?”

They shrugged. “You’re just…different today. Less stabby.”

She stared at the sandwich like it might explode. “...Thanks?”

They practically sprinted away.

She took a sip of the coffee. It was her favorite. Exactly how she liked it. Weird.

She stared at the screen in front of her, unsure of whether to be offended or impressed that her version of “balanced” was apparently alarming enough to warrant bribes.

In reality, everything was back to the way it should be. She was being productive. Efficient. Unbothered. Good at her job.

But beneath the surface?

There was a Rio shaped glitch in her system that she couldn’t debug.

Rio was in her head. Loud. Quiet. Everywhere. In flashes. In fragments.

Her mind continued to linger on the soft brush of her fingers down Agatha’s spine. The way she studied her like a challenge. That look she gave her right before pulling her apart. Focused. Curious. Like Agatha was a riddle she’d been dying to solve.

It had been…casual. It was supposed to be casual. Right?

Agatha clenched her jaw and refocused on her monitor. Re-edited a sentence that didn’t need fixing. Typed a Slack message, deleted it, then typed it again.

She wasn’t obsessing. She wasn’t. This was manageable. She was a grown woman for crying out loud, she could survive a fling, even one that came with nerdy metaphors and one very dangerously sexy brain.

Still, this morning had been...different.

Agatha had woken up with a warm body beside her and sunlight slipping through the curtains like it belonged there. Rio’s breathing had been slow, steady, her face half buried in the pillow, one hand so impossibly close to Agatha’s she wondered if Rio had left it there to see if Agatha would hold it.

The morning was comforting in a way that made Agatha's chest ache just thinking about it.

And then Rio had left.

Abruptly. Politely. With that strange, matter of fact tone that Rio managed to make seem both robotic and endearing. She mentioned paperwork. A phone call she needed to return. Some vague nod to a responsibility she didn’t name and Agatha didn’t ask about.

She replayed Rio’s words in her mind, “Can I leave my other bag here? I’ll come back for it later, when you’re done with work.” Not if. When. Like it was inevitable she’d return. Like the morning hadn’t just been some fleeting anomaly they were both pretending not to overthink.

Buzz.

Her phone vibrated across her desk, jolting her out of her thoughts.

She glanced at the screen. Unlocking her phone.

RIO: Do you like pinball?

Agatha blinked. She stared at the message for a solid ten seconds, heart rate accelerating like someone had just queued up boss battle music. She didn’t know what she expected, silence, awkwardness, just a blank message asking when to come get her stuff.

But not “do you like pinball.”

Agatha snorted under her breath and typed back.

AGATHA: I’m starting to think you just collect weirdly niche talents.

A pause. The little typing bubble blinked once, then stopped. Then blinked again.

RIO: Maybe I do. Do you want to find out?

Agatha leaned back in her chair, phone still in hand, chewing her bottom lip like she could gnaw through the part of her that wanted to say ‘yes’ immediately. Wanted to bolt from the building. Wanted to see where this went, even if it ended in a dumpster fire.

She wasn’t supposed to be this curious. Wasn’t supposed to care.

But she was already grinning. Already shaking her head.

She looked at the time. 5:06 p.m. Close enough.

Without bothering to log off Slack or close her tabs, she slid her laptop shut, grabbed her bag, and stood up. Her chair creaked behind her as it spun lazily.

“Early and leaving on time?” her boss called after her from down the hall, confused and vaguely alarmed.

Agatha didn’t stop. “I’ve got…research to do.”

And before anyone could stop her, or ask what kind of research involved pinball machines and a Tetris prodigy, Agatha was already out the door.

This was a mistake. Probably. But God help her, she wanted to make it.

– –

– –

Agatha had been to a lot of bars in her life.

Dive bars. Sports bars. Pretentious cocktail lounges where drinks were named after dead poets and served in glasses that looked like stolen chemistry equipment. She’d flirted on rooftops and been dumped in basements. She’d drunk whiskey with anarchists and espresso martinis with tech bros who tried to explain blockchain to her like it was magic. But she had never been to a bar quite like this.

It was tucked away in a graffiti smeared alleyway off a street she swore she’d walked down a hundred times. From the outside, it looked like nothing...a single, flickering neon sign above the door that simply read TILT.

No one was smoking outside. No one was loitering. It didn’t even show up on Google Maps. Which, in New York, was rarer than a clean subway bench.

Inside? It was a temple to pinball.

The entire place hummed with flashing lights, mechanical chimes, and the rhythmic clatter of balls hitting bumpers. Rows upon rows of vintage pinball machines stretched along the walls, their artwork bright and obnoxiously loud. The air smelled like aged wood, beer, and a faint, metallic tang that Agatha couldn’t place. She was overdressed from work, looking and feeling entirely out of place in a space like this, however, she wouldn’t be with Rio by her side.

Agatha spotted Rio leaning against a ‘Medieval Madness’ (author’s note: PLEASE read knight!rio by Visadero, thx) pinball machine like it was hers by birthright, arms crossed. She was still in the same outfit Agatha had seen her in last, “Autism Powered Fuck Machine” proudly displayed across her chest. Agatha adored the way Rio couldn’t give a fuck about what she wore or who had anything to say about it.

Rio’s hair was half tamed in a messy bun, face set in that unreadable expression that made Agatha want to grab her and kiss the mystery out of it. She looked...right here. Like she'd always belonged in a room full of machines and flickering light. Like this was her world, she had invited Agatha to. Like Rio had already mapped out the entire room and optimized the best pinball machine to play on, and the spot to stand when Agatha met her, because she did.

Agatha rolled her eyes. "I swear to God, if you tell me you hold a world record in pinball, I’m walking out."

Rio blinked. "No."

Agatha let out a relieved sigh.

Rio added, "But I am ranked."

Agatha groaned. "Of course you are." She rubbed her temples.

Rio tilted her head. "I like anything with patterns."

Agatha raised an eyebrow. "Pinball has patterns?"

Rio nodded like she was surprised the question even needed to be asked. “Everything has patterns.”

That shouldn’t have made sense. But coming from Rio? It did.

Agatha shook her head, dropping her bag onto a high top table and gestured to the machine. "Alright, fine. Show me what you got, wizard."

Rio didn’t hesitate. She stepped forward, pulled a quarter from her pocket, and slid it into the machine like she was offering a tribute to a god. She pulled back the plunger with precise control.

The second the ball launched onto the playfield, Agatha knew. She wasn’t just good at pinball. She was stupidly good.

Rio’s fingers barely seemed to move, but the ball shot through ramps and loops with impossible efficiency. She nudged the machine at just the right moments, avoiding tilts by the slimmest margin. She didn’t even look at the flippers...just tracked the ball with that same laser focused gaze she had when she played Tetris.

Within five minutes, she had triggered every major event on the board, sending the machine into a frenzy of flashing lights and jackpot chimes. Agatha could not believe this shit.

The game finally ended, and Rio stepped back, shaking out her hands. Her name blinked onto the high score list. RIO – #1.

Agatha exhaled slowly. "You’ve got to be kidding me."

Rio glanced at her. "Did…did you like it?"

Agatha laughed, sharp and disbelieving. "I think I just witnessed a crime."

Rio tilted her head. "It’s…legal."

"That’s not the point."

Rio paused. “Would…you like to try?”

Agatha raised an eyebrow. “Do you want to watch me publicly embarrass myself?”

Rio shrugged and smirked slightly. “Only a little.”

“Wow. That’s generous.” Agatha held out her hand as she squared up to the machine. “Fine…quarter me.”

Agatha had played maybe once or twice before...maybe as a kid, maybe in a bar half drunk, but never with someone who approached games like a full contact sport like Rio did. But Rio had a way of making everything feel like a test. And, somehow, Agatha could feel like this was Rio testing Agatha. Game on.

Rio passed her at quarter with a near ceremonial seriousness, Agatha slid the quarter into the machine, then slung up her hair into a quick ponytail.

"Alright, let’s see if I can at least pretend to be decent."

Rio didn’t move away. Instead, Agatha felt Rio step in behind her, moving closer...too close...her body just barely brushing against Agatha’s as she leaned in to her from behind. Watching Agatha play from over her shoulder.

"Start here…" Rio murmured into Agatha’s ear, fingers ghosting over the plunger. "Pull back just enough to control the launch."

Agatha’s breath hitched. Not because of the pinball tips, but because Rio was right there...her voice low and steady, like this was just another thing she could optimize. Her breath was right at her neck.

“Okay, alright, coach..." Agatha muttered, focusing on the plunger. She launched the ball and tried to remember how fingers worked.

For the first thirty seconds, she was fine. Not great, not terrible...just fine. She hit a few ramps, landed a couple of bonuses.

And then Rio leaned in even closer to Agatha, her body now pressed against her from behind and murmured into her ear low and husky, "You’re gripping too hard."

Agatha’s hands twitched, her mind went blank. The ball went…straight down the drain.

Agatha stood there, completely still. Then, slowly, she turned her head toward Rio. “Did you…did you do that on purpose?”

Rio blinked and chuckled softly. “I would never.”

Agatha squinted. “Liar.”

Rio smiled smugly. “That’s unverified.”

Agatha narrowed her eyes, suspicious, but the corner of her mouth twitched. “Alright. Again. And this time? Back the hell up.”

Rio blinked innocently. “I was only trying to help.” She slid another quarter into the machine.

“You were trying to sabotage..." Agatha muttered.

Rio didn’t move.

Agatha could feel Rio behind her again, warm, present, the faintest press of her chest against Agatha’s back. Not enough to be overt. Just enough to drive her fucking insane.

“I said back up..." Agatha warned, voice low.

Rio didn’t budge. “Your form is better this way.”

Agatha exhaled, frustrated and aroused all at once, like her nervous system didn’t know which signal to process first. She pulled the plunger back, launched the ball, and tried her best to ignore the very warm, very smug woman hovering directly behind her.

This time, she focused. Nailed the first few shots. Ramped the ball clean through the left loop, flipped it up into a bonus multiplier. It was going fine until Rio exhaled deliberately against her ear.

“You’re doing really well..." she murmured, her voice low and soft, as the sound reverberated like static through every goddamn nerve in Agatha’s body.

Agatha jerked the flipper too early. The ball bounced. Wobbled.

“Stop talking..." she hissed through gritted teeth. Eyes wide. Pupils dilated. Begging her brain to focus on the machine.

Rio leaned even closer, her lips brushing just beneath Agatha’s earlobe. “I think you like it when I talk.”

The ball zoomed straight down the middle.

Agatha slapped her hand against the machine and groaned. “Goddamn it, Vidal.”

Rio stepped back then, hands up, completely unrepentant. “You scored higher that time.”

Agatha turned to face her, eyes narrowed. “You’re evil.”

Rio tilted her head. “You say that like it’s a bad thing.”

Agatha opened her mouth, probably to snap something sarcastic, but the second she met Rio’s gaze, sharp, soft, knowing, her brain melted.

Agatha took a step forward. Rio didn’t move. Her voice was low, breathless. “Are you seriously trying to seduce me…with pinball?”

Rio shrugged, like it was the most obvious answer in the world. “Is it…work…ing?”

Agatha blinked.

Then, with a defeated groan, she grabbed Rio’s shirt and dragged her in to herself, spinning them around, then pinning Rio against the pinball machine, pressing her body flush against her, mouth hovering just inches from Rio’s.

“You are the most infuriating woman I’ve ever met..." Agatha said.

Rio smiled. “But I think you like it…” and gave a slight eyebrow wiggle.

Agatha didn’t answer. She just kissed her.

Rio kissed her back, with a barely there twitch at the corner of her mouth, like she was fighting a smile.

Agatha groaned, stepping back, crossing her arms. “Alright, fine. You win.”

Rio tilted her head. “I thought you didn’t like losing.”

Agatha scowled. “Oh, I hate losing. But I think I just learned something more important.”

Rio raised an eyebrow. “What’s that?”

Agatha smirked, leaning in just slightly, her voice low. “You play games outside of Tetris.”

Rio considered that for a long moment, then finally she nodded. “I don’t play unless I think I can win.”

Agatha’s smirk faltered. Because that? That felt dangerously like a confession. Agatha could see it in the way her gaze didn’t waver, the way her voice didn’t lift with irony or amusement. She wasn’t being coy. She wasn’t flirting. She was being honest.

And something in Agatha’s chest tightened at that.

They stood there in the buzzing warmth of the arcade, surrounded by flashing lights and bursts of synthesized sound, but Agatha couldn’t hear any of it anymore. All she could hear was the beat of her own pulse, loud and erratic in her ears. All she could see was Rio, standing there in that stupid t-shirt, looking too composed for someone who had just casually admitted she’d only play a game if she believed she could win.

It wasn’t just a flirt. It wasn’t just a throwaway line. It was a dare. A promise. A challenge.

Agatha’s throat tightened. Her fingers curled slightly at her sides. She wasn’t used to this, this kind of clarity. This kind of intent. Most people stumbled their way through relationships. Most people said ‘maybe’ or ‘I don’t know’ or ‘let’s see where this goes.’ But Rio? Rio looked at her like she was the next puzzle she’d already decided to solve. Like she wasn’t backing down. Like she’d already started.

Agatha swallowed hard, the moment stretching between them like a taut wire. Then she shifted her weight, voice quieter, less teasing now. “Come on..." she said, jerking her head toward the door. “Let me show you somewhere.”

Rio blinked. “Where?”

Agatha smirked. “A place that doesn’t have flashing lights and a leaderboard.”

Rio hesitated. “but I…like leaderboards.”

Agatha rolled her eyes. “Yea, I know, Smashboy. But this time, we’re playing by my rules.”

Rio paused, considered, then gave a single, solemn nod. “Alright.”

Agatha raised a brow. “No objections?”

Rio met her gaze. “I trust you.”

Agatha ignored the way her stomach twisted at Rio’s response and grabbed her bag with a little more force than necessary, like maybe the weight of it could anchor her in something real, something manageable. She motioned for Rio to follow, and without a word, Rio fell into step behind her.

The door to the arcade shut behind them with a soft clunk, muffling the last echoes of pinball machines and synth music. Outside, the alley was dim and damp, the kind of New York shine that turned pavement into mirrors.

They didn’t speak at first. The silence wasn’t uncomfortable though, it was charged, like the static that builds before a thunderstorm. Agatha’s body was humming with adrenaline and something else she didn’t want to name yet.

Manhattan at night was still alive, buzzing, breathing. The rhythm of it all animated around them. The soft blare of a distant taxi horn, the faint thump of music from an upstairs window, the quiet murmur of other night wanderers crossing intersections lit in amber. Agatha liked the city like this, when it was less showy, more honest. It made space for the strange, the spontaneous, the not quite figured out.

She glanced sideways at Rio, who walked just to the side of her, somehow impossibly trying to keep in step with her, hands tucked into the pockets of her shorts, shoulders relaxed, expression open in that unreadable way that still somehow said everything.

Rio didn’t ask where they were going. She didn’t interrupt with questions or commentary. She just walked. Observed. Like she was gathering data in real time, taking in everything around her like it was a new level in a game she hadn’t played before, because it was.

Every once in a while, Rio’s gaze would flick upward toward the buildings, tall, gleaming, impossibly crowded, and linger there, like she was calculating their structural integrity. Then her eyes would drop back to Agatha. Following her. Not just through the city, but into something uncharted. A path that wasn’t optimized. A decision made without a probability chart. A risk.

Agatha felt a pulse between them. She could almost hear it, thudding steady in her chest, whispering ‘don’t fuck this up, Harkness.’

She didn’t know where this night was going to lead. But for once, she didn’t need to.

– –

– –

After about ten minutes of weaving through narrow streets and sidestepping puddles, Agatha came to a halt in front of a tiny, nondescript storefront. It was tucked between a shuttered bookstore and a dry cleaner that looked like it hadn’t seen a customer since 2009. The window was fogged, concealing whatever lay beyond, and the only marker was a small, handwritten card taped to the door in fading blue ink: The Blue Room.

Agatha glanced back at Rio, whose eyes flicked over the door, the sidewalk, the dim reflection in the glass. “Ever been to a jazz club?”

Rio shook her head, slow and sure. “No.”

Agatha smirked. “Figured.” She didn’t elaborate. She just took off her blazer, now showing a creme colored blouse and put it on Rio to hide her t-shirt, which was definitely not the dress code for a place like this.

“Better, but not best.” Agatha nodded to herself. She reached for the handle and pushed open the door.

The shift was immediate. The moment they stepped inside, it was like entering another world, one untouched by the buzz and grind of the city just beyond the threshold. The air was warm, humid from too many bodies and the slow burn of something rich and smoky that might’ve been pipe tobacco, or some expensive cologne soaked into the velvet booths over decades.

The lights were low, casting everything in gold and navy. The walls were painted a deep midnight blue, almost black, making the space feel smaller, closer. Safe. On the stage, tucked at the front beneath soft amber spotlights, a jazz trio played like they weren’t performing but remembering, an upright bass, brushed drums, a saxophone singing in lazy, winding arcs that filled the room like a slow exhale. It wasn’t loud. It wasn’t showy. It was soft and intentional. Like the music knew it was being listened to.

Rio stood just inside the entrance, her hands in her pockets, eyes sweeping across the room like she was trying to memorize it all at once, the texture of the sound, the ambient clink of glasses, the way everyone moved slower here. She looked like someone trying to solve a riddle in a language she’d never heard before, and still somehow getting half the answers right.

Agatha nudged her gently toward an empty table nestled into the far corner of the room, tucked between a velvet curtain and a cracked old lamp whose shade glowed like honey.

“Sit." Agatha said, already sliding into her own seat with practiced ease.

Rio obeyed, sliding into the chair with that same composed, methodical grace she applied to almost everything. She sat up straight, hands folded for a moment on the edge of the table, as if awaiting instruction. Agatha, already halfway reclined in her chair, flagged down the server with a casual flick of her fingers, like she’d done this a hundred times before.

When the server appeared, Agatha barely glanced at the menu before ordering a whiskey neat, her usual, her default, her armor in liquid form. Then she turned her head toward Rio, one brow lifting in a wordless ‘your turn.’

Rio opened her mouth. “Wa–”

Agatha’s eyebrow rose. It wasn’t exaggerated. Just a small shift, but Rio caught it immediately. The arch of skepticism. The flicker of amusement. The precise moment Agatha silently went, ‘Seriously, Vidal?;

Rio’s mouth closed. Her eyes flicked down to the menu, still half tucked into the candlelight between them. She scanned it in a second, internal systems kicking into overdrive. A place like this wasn’t built for practicality. It was built for mood, for warmth, for memory.

Water was efficient. But it wasn’t the move. Wine? Too slow. Beer? Inconsistent. Vodka? Too harsh. Whiskey? Likely optimal...but unpredictable, especially on an empty stomach and a heart already moving at unsafe speeds.

“Fine…” Rio muttered, like she was accepting a dare. “...an Old Fashioned.”

Agatha grinned like she’d just won a bet she hadn’t needed to say out loud. “Look at you, making interesting choices.”

Rio straightened her posture slightly. “It seemed the most appropriate selection.”

Agatha chuckled, shaking her head. “You’re ridiculous.”

The server nodded, disappearing toward the bar.

Rio drummed her fingers lightly against the table. “Do you…approve?”

Agatha tilted her head, watching her. “I think I’m just impressed you didn’t order the most efficient drink on the menu.”

Rio considered. “That would’ve been a shot of vodka.”

Agatha laughed. “Christ, you did think about it.”

Rio didn’t deny it.

The server came back with their drinks. Rio wasn’t sure what surprised her more, the sharp burn of the drink a few minutes later pulling at her eyeballs, or how easily the warmth of it settled in next to the way Agatha was looking at her.

Rio didn’t drink often. Too many variables. But now, in this space that pulsed with low brass and old soul, with Agatha across from her, framed in gold light and shadows, Rio found herself not just adapting. She found herself wanting to.

Agatha leaned back in her chair, crossing her legs. “Well, congrats. You’ve officially had a real drink at a jazz club. How does it feel?”

Rio looked around again, the low hum of conversation mixing with the steady rhythm of the band.

Then, voice perfectly even, “Like I unlocked a new achievement.”

Agatha barked out a laugh, shaking her head. “I am so glad I dragged you here.”

Rio tilted her head, listening closely to the subtle intricacies of the trio playing on stage. Her fingers began to move without thinking, tapping a quiet, rhythm against the edge of the table in perfect time with the upright bass. Her eyes narrowed slightly, not in confusion, but in focus, as if she were dissecting the melody in real time, breaking it down into movements, equations.

“This is…different..." she said at last, her voice soft.

Agatha raised an eyebrow, swirling the whiskey in her glass with a lazy tilt of her wrist. “Yea? How so?”

Rio didn’t answer right away. She seemed to be searching for the right phrasing, one that wouldn’t sound too detached or robotic. “There’s no clear objective..." she said eventually, her gaze never leaving the stage.

Agatha let out a low chuckle, her lips curling around the rim of her glass. “That’s kind of the point, Vidal. No win condition. No scoreboard.”

Rio’s fingers didn’t stop tapping. “The patterns are interesting, though..." she murmured, almost to herself.

“Of course you’d analyze jazz..." Agatha said, rolling her eyes affectionately. “Only you would come to a place like this and treat it like a math problem.”

Rio turned to look at her, that thoughtful intensity returning to her eyes. “You…like this place.” It wasn’t a question. Just a simple statement of fact, observed and catalogued.

“Yea…” Agatha said after a beat, her voice quiet. “Helps me think. Helps me breathe, sometimes.”

Rio nodded slowly, taking that in with the same focus she gave to a complicated tournament bracket or a particularly tricky T-spin setup. “So…this is your version of an arcade.”

Agatha blinked, then snorted into her drink. “Not how I’d put it, but sure.”

Rio didn’t laugh, but her mouth quirked slightly. “It makes sense.”

Agatha leaned in across the table, her elbows propped up, chin resting on her hand. “You’re saying my coping mechanism is...jazz?”

“I’m saying it’s optimal for your temperament.”

Agatha barked a laugh, the sound low and delighted. “God, you’re such a little weirdo.”

Rio met her gaze, unwavering. “You invited me.”

And just like that, Agatha’s grin softened, her heart clenching in that subtle, dangerous way it always seemed to around Rio. Because she had. Because she wanted her here. Because, for some reason, this brilliant, awkward, maddening woman made her feel more like herself than she had in months. Maybe years. Maybe ever.

“I did..." Agatha said quietly, lifting her glass. “And for once, I’m not regretting it.”

Rio’s fingers paused in their rhythm, just for a second. Then they started again, this time, keeping time with Agatha’s breath instead of the music.

Rio looked around again, her eyes scanning the corners of the room like she was assessing its architecture, memorizing the dimensions, cataloging the colors. The low amber lights bathed everything in warmth, softening the edges, blurring the sharp lines she was used to. The quiet murmur of low conversation, the muted clink of glass, the way the saxophone curved through the air like a drawn out breath, none of it demanded attention. It just…existed. It filled the space without needing to conquer it.

“It’s efficient..." Rio murmured, almost to herself. “Simple mechanics. But the execution is what makes it work.”

Agatha tilted her head, lips curling into a lopsided grin. “You’re really comparing jazz to Tetris right now?”

Rio shrugged her shoulders, totally unbothered. “The best players understand improvisation. The structure comes first. Then comes instinct.”

Agatha blinked at her, then let out a soft, almost disbelieving laugh. “Good grief…”

Rio took a measured sip of her drink. “Is that an agreement?”

Agatha rolled her eyes but there was a softness in it now, an affection she wasn’t bothering to hide anymore. “Yea, Vidal. That’s an agreement.”

And that was it. No follow up, no punchline. Just the quiet, weightless feeling of two people realizing something at the same time and choosing not to ruin it by naming it.

Rio reached out and held Agatha’s hand that was placed on the table, her fingers tentative at first, thumb brushing lightly over Agatha’s knuckles, she was still asking permission even after everything they'd shared. But Agatha didn’t hesitate, she turned her hand palm up, inviting, steady, and Rio slipped her fingers to rest on top of Agatha’s hand like they belonged there. Rio’s grip was warm, a little uncertain, but honest.

Rio didn’t look at Agatha right away, just focused on the feel of their hands joined in the quiet, the way Agatha’s thumb automatically began to move in slow, soothing circles over Rio’s skin. It grounded her. Softened something inside Agatha’s chest she hadn’t realized she’d been holding tight. Rio’s breathing slowed, syncing unconsciously with Agatha’s, their hands nestled between them like a bridge that no longer needed to be tested.

They sat there in companionable serenity, the music rising and falling like a tide around them. The saxophone dipped into a solo, and somewhere near the back, someone let out a low appreciative whistle. Neither Rio nor Agatha flinched. They didn’t need to speak. The stillness between them wasn’t awkward, it was full. Full of everything that didn’t need to be said.

There was no competition here. No leaderboards. No timers counting down the seconds to the next move. Just the music. Just the low, velvety sounds of a city that never really slept.

Just them. Still learning each other. Still sitting at the edge of something they didn’t quite have the words for. And for once, neither of them was in a rush to define it.

They finished their drinks in a quiet that didn’t press or pull, just existed, warm and calm, like the last note of a song held in the air. Agatha nursed the final sip of her whiskey, letting it roll on her tongue before swallowing, the burn anchoring her in the moment. Rio, across the table, treated her Old Fashioned like it was a science experiment, careful, methodical sips, her fingers resting lightly against the glass, her eyes occasionally flicking back to the stage as if she was still decoding the patterns of the music.

It wasn’t a long night. But it didn’t need to be. There was a fullness to the quiet between them. Not heavy. Not unfinished. Just enough. They had opened something here, not loudly, not dramatically, but in that way something true sneaks in when no one’s looking. A shift. A realignment. Something small, but impossibly important.

Dragging it out would’ve felt wrong. Like touching a healing bruise just to make sure it still hurt.

So Agatha reached into her bag, pulled out a few bills, and tossed them onto the table with casual ease. Rio opened her mouth, brow already furrowed like she was about to offer a breakdown of standard tipping rates, but Agatha caught it with a dry look and a soft, “Don’t.”

Rio paused, lips parted, then nodded, slow, thoughtful, and stayed quiet.

Agatha stood. “Come on. Let’s go.” she said, her voice low but not unkind. An invitation, not a dismissal.

Rio followed without question, rising to her feet with that same precise grace she always carried, like every movement had been calculated in advance. They didn’t speak as they left the club, stepping back into the city night, the sound of the saxophone still faintly echoing behind them, as if the evening wasn’t quite ready to let them go.

Outside, the city buzzed, alive and electric in that uniquely New York way, headlights gliding down slick streets, voices echoing off brick walls, neon signs bleeding color into the darkness.

Agatha glanced at Rio beside her, hands tucked into the pockets of her shorts, eyes wide in that way that only happened when she was quietly cataloguing everything. The world. The people. Maybe even Agatha.

– –

– –

They didn’t speak, as they walked to Agatha’s apartment. Both wanted to. It was there, just under the surface, words pressing against the backs of their throats like a tide, aching to be released.

The city was too exposed, too loud and open for what needed to be said. The things they carried between them weren’t made for sidewalks and streetlamps. They needed walls. Shelter. A safe space.

As they reached the steps of Agatha’s building, Rio hesitated. She looked up at the door like it meant more than just protection, like it was the edge of something, and she wasn’t sure what waited on the other side.

Agatha pulled her keys from her bag and glanced at her. “You coming in?”

Rio nodded once, simple and sure, but her throat bobbed like it took more effort than she let on.

Agatha didn’t wait. She unlocked the door, pushed it open, and held it there.

Rio stepped through. The air changed. Quieter. And the city, along with whatever courage they didn’t have out there, was left behind on the sidewalk, as they climbed up the stairs.

Inside, the apartment welcomed them like nothing had changed. Warm air met them at the door, laced with the soft scent of lavender and something faintly woodsy from the candle Agatha had left burning on the windowsill. The city noise faded behind the walls, dulled to a low thrum. Familiar.

Agatha kicked off her heels and dropped her bags, her movements loose, casual, almost too casual for how tight her chest felt. She draped herself onto her couch, stretching her arms overhead, rolling the tension from her shoulders with a low sigh, like the walk had shaken something out of her that she didn’t quite know what to do with.

Rio lingered in the doorway, her eyes moving slowly across the room like she was absorbing every detail again. The framed photos. The books strewn haphazardly over every surface. She didn’t step fully in until Agatha patted the cushion next to her on the couch.

“Sit.”

Rio kicked off her Birkenstocks, and moved toward her, deliberate as ever, and lowered herself onto the couch with the kind of precision that made Agatha’s throat tighten. She sat carefully, as if the space beside Agatha was a line she wasn’t sure she was allowed to cross yet. As if her proximity for what they were about to talk about would tip the balance in a way she couldn’t predict.

They didn’t speak. Not right away.

The quiet settled like a blanket around them, thick, present, but not oppressive. Not awkward. Just full of things neither of them had figured out how to say yet.

Agatha exhaled slowly and let her head fall back against the couch cushion. She stared at the ceiling for a beat, her jaw working as if testing the weight of words that didn’t want to come out.

Then, finally, her voice low, Agatha asked, “Did you really mean that?”

Rio turned to her. “Mean what?”

“What you said before. About going through the same thing.”

Rio considered that for a long moment, like she was trying to find the exact right way to answer. “I wouldn’t say it if I didn’t mean it.”

Agatha exhaled sharply, pressing her fingers against her temples. “Do you ever not give the most logical answer possible?”

Rio blinked. “Would you prefer an illogical one?”

Agatha laughed, dry and tired. “That’s not the point.”

Rio tilted her head slightly. “Then what is the point?”

Agatha turned to look at her. “The point is...” She cut herself off, groaning. “God, I don’t know. I just...” she huffed, sitting up straighter, adjusting the throw pillow behind her like it would somehow help her get a grip. “I thought...” Her voice faltered. “I thought you were gone.”

Rio’s gaze didn’t waver. “But you weren’t gone to me.”

Agatha let out a sharp, humorless laugh. “That’s because I live here.”

Rio just shook her head. “That’s not what I meant.”

Agatha stopped.

Rio inhaled, measured and steady. “I wasn’t trying to disappear.” She met Agatha’s gaze. “I just…needed time.”

Agatha narrowed her eyes. “Time for what?”

Rio continued unflinching. “Time to tell you the truth.”

She shifted where she sat, her hands folding neatly in her lap like she was preparing a thesis presentation instead of unraveling her entire life. Her voice was calm, measured, but underneath it was something raw, uncertainty, maybe. Or fear.

“I sold the house in Iowa..." she said. “The one my dad left me. I packed everything I owned into my backpack and a duffle bag. There’s nothing for me there anymore. No family left. No real friends. Just empty routines I’ve already optimized into oblivion.”

Agatha blinked, her expression unreadable.

Rio kept going. “I didn’t plan it. Not really. I was staring at the wall one day, thinking about how quiet everything had gotten, and then…” Her throat bobbed. “Then I thought about you.”

She looked down at her hands, then back up again at Agatha, eyes steady. “And suddenly, staying in Iowa didn’t make sense anymore. But getting on a bus? Cramming into a U-Haul? Taking seventeen detours and somehow ending up in a pizza shop in Manhattan? That did.”

A pause. “I came here for you. Not because I thought you owed me something. Not because I expected anything from you. Just because…it’s the only choice that felt right.”

Rio added, quieter this time, “I didn’t have a plan beyond that.”

Agatha didn’t answer right away. Her brain short circuited somewhere between ‘I came here for you’ and ‘no plan beyond that.’ She stared at Rio, and for once in her life, she had no snark locked and loaded, no clever retort to shield herself with. Just that familiar, terrifying rush of emotion threatening to undo her. Her chest felt too tight, like it couldn’t contain the weight of what Rio had just handed her. Not a plan. Not a pitch. But a choice. A leap. A goddamn act of faith.

And Rio, sweet, awkward, infuriating Rio, was sitting on her couch, steady and quiet and waiting. Like she didn’t realize she had just torn open Agatha’s chest and handed back to her, her own heart.

Agatha swallowed. “And you ended up here?”

Rio nodded. “Yes.”

Agatha scoffed, shaking her head. “You know, for someone so obsessed with optimization, your life choices are terrible.”

Rio hummed, tilting her head. “Not all of them.”

Agatha stilled. Because fuck, that...that was something. She let out a slow breath, running a hand through her hair, trying to shove down the heat curling in her chest. “You’re unbelievable.”

Rio just watched her, like she was waiting. Waiting for Agatha to make the next move. Not as some calculated move, not because she had strategized it all beforehand.

Agatha exhaled sharply. “So what now?”

Rio considered that. Then, simply...“I don’t know.”

Agatha laughed, shaking her head. “That’s new for you.”

Rio nodded. “It is.”

Agatha looked at her...really looked at her. The way she sat, posture straight but somehow open, hands resting on her lap, fingers twitching like she was resisting the urge to fidget. Like she wasn’t sure what to do next. It was so...human.

Agatha sighed, rubbing a hand down her face. Then, before she could talk herself out of it...she reached over, fingers brushing against Rio’s hand then holding it.

Rio looked down at the touch, then up at Agatha.

There was a pause, small, fragile, but electric. Like the moment before a Tetris piece locks into place. Rio didn’t flinch, didn’t pull away. Instead, her fingers turned slightly, aligning with Agatha’s like it was second nature, like she'd been waiting for the exact moment Agatha would finally reach out. Her touch was warm, steady, and deceptively soft, considering everything she’d just said. She looked at their joined hands, then back at Agatha with that same quiet intensity, like she was logging the moment, storing it somewhere permanent. Not as data. Not as a strategy, but as something real. Something she didn’t want to forget.

“But I know being with you, Agatha, makes me feel...not so weird as everyone makes me feel."

Agatha blinked.

Rio’s voice was quieter than usual, like she wasn’t sure how to phrase what she was saying but needed to say it anyway.

She hesitated, her fingers twitching once on her lap before continuing, her voice soft but steady. "It’s like…a nice warm feeling I get when I’m with you. But not just like, surface level. Not just…fun or comfortable. It’s here." She tapped her fingers against her chest, right over her sternum. Her eyes flicked up to meet Agatha’s for just a second, and it hit with the force of something raw, something unfiltered. "Right here. And I’ve never felt that before. Not with anyone. Not ever."

Agatha didn’t speak. She couldn’t speak. Rio, always so composed, always so exact in her words and logic, had just handed her something so real, so honest, it made Agatha feel like her lungs had stopped working. Like her whole body had to catch up with what she’d just heard.

Rio shifted again, fidgeting slightly, like even saying that much had cost her something. Her voice dropped lower, almost shy. "So I came here. To find you. Because I didn’t know what else to do. But somehow…you found me instead."

It landed in Agatha’s chest like a slow explosion. Not loud. Just deep. Radiating through her, blooming warmth where she had forgotten warmth could exist. She exhaled slowly, her voice catching despite herself. "Yea, well. New York is full of surprises."

Rio tilted her head slightly at that, brow furrowing in that quietly curious way that made Agatha’s heart ache just a little. “Apparently.”

Agatha let out a shaky laugh, barely more than a breath.

"Why were you even in that pizza place?" Rio asked after a moment, voice low, sincere. It wasn’t accusatory. Just…wondering.

Agatha’s lips curled into a small, reluctant smirk, the kind that tried to protect something tender. "Downward spiral…"

Rio blinked. "Oh." She tilted her head, studying her.

"Don’t try to analyze it, Vidal. You’ll ruin the magic."

Rio considered this. Really considered it, like she was rearranging internal code to make space for the concept of something being unquantifiable.

"I don’t believe in magic..." Rio said finally, but there was no edge to it.

Agatha leaned in, just a little. Her voice was softer this time. "You don’t have to believe in it for it to work."

Rio stared at her, unreadable. Or maybe not unreadable, maybe just full of things Agatha hadn’t learned to read yet.

"Do you feel it too?" Rio asked. Barely a whisper. Like the question had been hovering in the air since the moment they locked eyes when they first met, and only now was brave enough to surface.

Agatha blinked. Her jaw tightened. And then she gave the only answer she could. "Yea. I do."

She didn’t say how much. Didn’t say how terrifying it was. Or how impossible. Or how inevitable it had started to feel.

But the way Rio looked at her, steadily, fully, like she saw everything and wasn’t afraid, made her think maybe she didn’t need to.

Maybe, for once, someone else could meet her halfway. And for now, that was enough.

– –

– –

Before Agatha could respond, a sudden thump from across the room made Rio flinch, her entire posture stiffening like someone had just triggered a trapdoor under her feet. She turned sharply, every muscle tense, eyes scanning the room for a threat.

And then, something small and furry darted across the floor.

Rio’s breath caught as she curled her legs up to her chest in a defensive posture. “What…the hell?”

Agatha glanced casually in the direction of the blur. “Oh. Right.”

Scratchy.

The rabbit barreled toward them, a determined little ball of white fuzz with twitching ears and zero concept of personal space. He skidded slightly as he came to a halt near Agatha’s feet, then gave a content little shuffle, staring up at her expectantly.

Rio did not move.

Scratchy blinked.

Agatha, already biting back a grin, nudged Rio. “Forgot to mention…I live with someone.”

“You…” Rio’s voice was strangled. “You have a pet rabbit.”

“Obviously..." Agatha said, with the self satisfied smugness of someone who definitely enjoyed the chaos she caused.

Rio stared at Scratchy. Then at Agatha. Then back at Scratchy. Her eyes narrowed, and Agatha could practically see the gears turning in Rio’s head. She blinked rapidly, her mind clearly running through its entire database of knowledge. Rabbits. Domesticated species. Herbivores. Fast.

"...I don’t know anything about rabbits..." Rio admitted, her voice almost horrified.

Agatha smirked. “Oh my God. You’ve found your next optimization project, haven’t you?”

Rio remained frozen, staring at Scratchy like he was the final boss in a game she hadn’t prepared for. "...Maybe." she muttered.

Agatha snorted. She reached down, scooped Scratchy up like a baby and cradled him against her chest with one hand. The rabbit, of course, leaned into it like royalty receiving tribute.

“Alright..." Agatha said, settling onto the couch again next to Rio. “Step one: pet him.”

Rio hesitated like she was being handed a live grenade. “...Where?”

Agatha gently guided Rio’s hand. “Here. His head. Like this.” demonstrating how to pet him.

Rio slipped off her gamer glove, her movements delicate, almost reverent. She reached out, fingers brushing Scratchy’s forehead with the caution of someone disarming a trap. Scratchy closed his eyes, pressing into the touch, blissed out like this was the apex of his existence.

Rio’s eyebrows shot up. “He’s…soft.” She blinked again. “He’s so soft. Agatha, I don’t understand. How is this texture even real?”

“You are so dramatic..." Agatha said, unable to keep the amusement out of her voice. “He’s a rabbit, not a mystery substance.”

Rio didn’t respond. She was still petting, slowly, almost mesmerized. “I don’t think I’ve ever touched anything like this. It’s like…microfiber, but alive.”

“You’re comparing my rabbit to a fabric?”

“I’m describing a sensory event..." Rio said flatly, as if it were obvious.

Agatha smirked. "You are ridiculous."

Still, she let Rio continue getting used to the concept of a rabbit. After a few more pets, she stood carrying Scratchy like a sleepy toddler over to the fridge.. "Alright, time for step two: feeding him."

Agatha opened the fridge, pulled out a bunch of dandelion greens, and shut the door with one foot. Returning to the couch, she set Scratchy down between them and held up the greens like an offering. The rabbit’s entire demeanor shifted. His eyes locked onto the food like a laser guided missile.

Agatha dropped the greens in front of him. Scratchy attacked, the leaves vanishing with terrifying speed. Not chewed, but erased from existence. The noise was startlingly aggressive for such a tiny creature.

Rio flinched. “What…” she pointed helplessly at the bare couch. “Where did it go?”

Agatha burst out laughing. “Oh, you should see him with a raspberry. It’s like a horror movie.”

Rio stared at Scratchy, who was now licking his paws innocently. “He’s terrifying…I really like him.”

“He’s a delight..." Agatha said, picking Scratchy up and putting him on her lap. She stroked a finger slowly down the front of his head, between his twitchy ears, and along his spine. The rabbit melted into the touch, a puddle of rabbit contentment, his little nose twitching like he was dreaming of world domination. If rabbits could smile, Scratchy absolutely was.

Rio watched all of it stunned. Her fingers twitched slightly, like she was resisting the urge to take notes. Then she looked at Agatha, really looked, and something softened in her expression.

Agatha leaned back, arching one brow. “Alright, step three: hold him on your lap.”

Rio’s eyes widened slightly, like Agatha had just suggested defusing a bomb with a spork. “You want me to…what?”

Agatha didn’t wait. She shifted Scratchy gently from her lap and leaned over, placing the rabbit carefully on Rio’s lap. “It’s fine. He doesn’t bite. He only judges.”

Scratchy settled immediately, legs tucked underneath him like a little loaf of living bread. He looked up at Rio, ears twitching, nose wiggling like he was evaluating her soul.

Rio blinked. Then looked down.

They made eye contact. A long, unbroken stare between woman and rabbit.

Rio cleared her throat softly. “Hello…Señor Scratchy.”

Agatha snorted, “Oh my god..." she said, “you gave him a title.”

Rio didn’t look up. She was too busy trying to figure out where to put her hands. “He has earned it.”

Agatha slid closer on the couch and reached over, taking Rio’s hands in hers and guiding them slowly toward Scratchy’s ears. “Ok. Here. Like this. Gently knead behind the ears, see? It’s like little circles, soft pressure.”

Rio’s fingers followed the motion. The moment she did, Scratchy reacted, his eyes half lidded, head tilting slightly to the side, and one paw twitching in reflex before he lifted it to begin cleaning his face with a slow, deliberate swipe.

Rio froze. “Did I break him?”

Agatha laughed. “No. That’s his happy place.”

“I…unlocked a reaction pattern?" Rio said, visibly pleased. “That’s…deeply satisfying.”

“You’re playing him like a rhythm game.”

Rio didn’t argue.

Instead, she kept petting him, shifting slightly to allow Scratchy to lean fully into her hands. She was being absurdly gentle, focused like she was tuning a very delicate instrument, but her whole posture had changed, softer, less tight, less braced. She didn’t even realize it, but Agatha saw it clearly. Rio’s shoulders had lowered. Her jaw unclenched. Her breathing slowed.

Agatha watched her with a quiet smile. “You know…” she said after a beat, “he doesn’t sit like this with just anyone.”

Rio glanced up. “He sat with you.”

“Well, yeah, he knows I feed him.”

Rio’s mouth twitched. “I respect that.”

Agatha leaned in a little closer, her voice warm, low. “He likes you.”

Rio looked down at the rabbit, who was now flopped slightly to the side, head resting lazily against her arm. “…I think the feeling is mutual.”

Agatha stared at Rio, at the smile at the corners of her mouth, and something squeezed in her chest.

This woman. This brilliant, exhausting, awkward, infuriatingly precise woman, was petting her rabbit like it was the most sacred act on Earth.

“This is…unexpected..." Rio said quietly.

Agatha raised a brow. “Me owning a rabbit or you liking him?”

Rio paused. “Both.”

Agatha couldn’t help it. She laughed. The sound was warm, easy, and real.

Rio might have looked like she’d walked out of a logic simulator, but here she was, eyes wide, lap full of rabbit fur, brain quietly melting from overstimulation, and Agatha had never found her more endearing, Agatha was so screwed.

– –

– –

Rio’s watch beeped one, insistent tone. Midnight.

She blinked down at it, surprised. “Oh. It’s late. I should probably go. Mike said I could sleep on his couch.”

Agatha felt it before she could name it, an ache, sharp and sudden, somewhere in her chest. She kept her expression neutral, watching as Rio shifted, slow and careful, rising to her feet like she didn’t want to disturb the air too much. Scratchy hopped down from her lap with a lazy thump.

The warmth that had built in the room, in her chest, began to slip away with the movement. Like it had only ever existed because Rio was still there, because her presence created a gravitational pull that made the air feel thicker, softer, more meaningful.

Without her, the room felt colder somehow, dimmer, like the glow had been turned down a few notches. The cushions where Rio had been sitting seemed too empty, the space beside Agatha too wide. It wasn’t just physical, something intangible had shifted. The quiet felt different now. Not peaceful, but hollow. Like the absence of Rio had peeled something open in her that didn’t want to be closed again.

Agatha’s heart beat faster, too loud in her ears. She opened her mouth. Closed it. Then forced herself to speak, the words coming out quieter than intended. “Don’t go.”

Rio paused.

She didn’t turn around. She just stood there, only a few steps away from the couch, one hand twitching by her side. Agatha stood up and walked towards her.

“Stay” Agatha said, more clearly this time. “Stay with me.”

Stillness settled between them, not heavy, not awkward, just charged. The air felt impossibly full, like even the quiet had weight. Rio turned to face Agatha slowly, her movements careful, deliberate, like she didn’t want to disturb whatever fragile thing had begun to form between them. Her eyes searched Agatha’s face with quiet intensity, as if she were trying to read a language she didn’t quite trust herself yet to understand.

It wasn’t just curiosity in her gaze, it was awe. Hesitation. A deep, aching need for proof. Like she couldn’t quite believe Agatha meant it, that this closeness, this comfort, this sense of being wanted, was something real and hers to hold. And Agatha didn’t rush her. She just breathed slowly, letting Rio look, letting her take the time to feel safe enough to believe it.

Then, finally, softly Rio said “...Ok.”

Agatha let out a sharp, shaky breath she hadn’t realized she was holding, like something deep inside her had finally unclenched. The invisible tension she’d been carrying melted away, her shoulders loosening, jaw relaxing, as she stepped just a little closer to Rio, her body catching up to what her heart already knew. It felt like relief, but more than that, it was a quiet, fragile hope blooming in the space Rio hadn’t left.

Rio’s brow furrowed slightly. “Are you sure?”

Agatha didn’t hesitate, no second guessing, no guarded calculation. She simply reached out, fingers curling around Rio’s wrist with a light but certain touch, guiding her back to the couch. The smirk on her lips was subtle, almost imperceptible, but laced with something deeper, a quiet promise, a dare, soft and unshakable. And when Rio sank beside her, their bodies close again, Agatha didn’t let go.

“Yea, Vidal..." she murmured, her voice low and sure, her eyes not leaving Rio’s. “I’m sure.” And this time, she meant it with her whole chest.

Rio moved like she was committing to something. Her movements weren’t slow, exactly, but measured, like this choice needed to be fully intentional. And once she sat down again, once she settled into the space next to Agatha like she belonged there, like she always had...it felt right.

Scratchy, perhaps sensing the shift, hopped up again and made a beeline for Rio’s lap. He curled there immediately, as if no time had passed, as if this was just the natural order of things.

Rio stared down at him, thoughtful. Then she looked up at Agatha. “...can I…research rabbit care?”

Agatha dropped her head back against the couch, groaning. “Fine. But no spreadsheets. I’m drawing the line at formulas for carrot portioning.”

Rio made a soft humming noise, considering. “...that doesn’t seem optimal.”

Agatha groaned louder, turning her head to press her face into the slope of Rio’s shoulder. “You’re a menace.”

“I know…” Rio said, her voice low and smug with the kind of confidence that only came from being absolutely right, “...but you want me anyways.”

Agatha didn’t answer right away. She just stayed there, half buried in the curve of Rio’s body, her nose tucked against warm skin, breathing in that maddening, familiar scent of Rio that always made her chest ache.

Agatha fingers reached out to pet Scratchy, who had curled into a warm, pile of fluff in Rio’s lap, his fur rising and falling with each tiny breath. But before she could stroke him, Rio caught Agatha’s hand in hers, gently but with certainty, like she had every right to do so. Their fingers slipped together easily, naturally, like they were always meant to fit this way. Agatha’s hand stilled, and she let it be taken. Rio brought their joined hands up and pressed her lips to Agatha’s knuckles, softly, reverently. Like she was tasting something sacred.

“You drive me insane..." Agatha murmured into Rio’s shoulder, her voice muffled, her words falling somewhere between surrender and accusation. But there was no real bite left in her tone. It had lost its sharpness, worn down by Rio’s steady warmth, softened by the quiet safety that curled around them like a blanket.

“And yet…” Rio said, her voice teasing, but laced with something gentler underneath, “...here you are. Still not moving away.”

Agatha let out a sigh, but it was less about exasperation now and more about release. Her body had relaxed against Rio’s almost unconsciously, the tension in her spine giving way to gravity and trust. Her breath touched the skin of Rio’s throat, sending a slow shiver up her spine. A reluctant smile tugged at her lips, like she didn’t want to admit it but couldn’t help herself.

“You’re lucky you’re cute..." Agatha muttered, voice dry, affectionate. “Or I’d have fed you to the pigeons in Central Park by now.”

“You say that like it’s a bad thing…” Rio whispered, the words brushing against Agatha’s scalp.

Agatha tilted her face up, just enough to look at her, and the look she gave was half warning, half want. Her eyes, sharp and clever, were softened by the light, by the closeness, by the quiet beating of Rio’s heart against her own.

“I swear, if you weren’t so…”

“So what?” Rio asked, a little too pleased with herself, one brow arched in playful defiance.

“...So stupidly beautiful..." Agatha said, barely louder than a breath. It slipped out before she could cage it, and once it did, there was no taking it back. Her voice cracked on the last word, just slightly. Not enough to be obvious, but enough for Rio to hear it.

Rio stilled for a moment. Then that crooked, devastating smile returned, so tender it hurt to look at. “Stop talking about yourself…” Rio whispered in reply, her thumb brushing slow, grounding circles across Agatha’s knuckles.

Agatha’s lips parted, but the words didn’t come. Her throat worked like she was about to say something flippant, something cutting, something to put a little distance between them again. But instead, her gaze lingered on Rio’s face. Agatha searched Rio for the ache she thought was hers alone, and found instead something unwavering, something like being seen.

Rio let out a long sigh, then ducked her head to press a kiss into Agatha’s hair, just near her temple, lingering there longer than necessary. She breathed her in, lavender and something deeper, like rain on old paper or midnight ink, scents that wrapped around her senses, leaving her both dizzy and anchored.

Rio nodded. “He did. Even when no one else understood me...he did.”

Agatha smirked. “You wouldn’t dare.”

Agatha sighed. “Ok, champ.”

Rio’s eyes flicked to hers. “Oh god, yes.”

Just two people standing in a space small enough to feel the charge of every breath, every shift in weight, every brush of fingertips against fabric.

“I’ve…I’ve never worn silk before..." Rio admitted, touching the fabric cautiously with the tips of her fingers. “It’s…slippery.”

“It’s soft..." Agatha corrected, pressing it into Rio’s hand. “And luxurious. You deserve to feel good.”

“I don’t know..." she murmured. “Like...I’m allowed to be someone softer. Someone who isn’t just practical and efficient and planning ten moves ahead. Someone who can just feel nice. For no reason.”

Agatha got into bed. “You are allowed. And you are soft. You just hide it behind all those Tetris blocks.”

Rio smiled, shy, getting into bed with her. “Maybe.”

She lay on her back at first, arms at her sides, posture too straight, like she was trying to calibrate her own presence within the space, as if proximity to Agatha required some kind of delicate, mathematical precision. She stared at the ceiling for a moment, brow slightly furrowed, every muscle holding a quiet tension.

She was far too far away from Agatha.

Agatha noticed immediately. She was on her side, facing Rio, one arm bent under her pillow, watching the line of Rio’s jaw tense and release. She could practically feel the distance like a draft between them, like Rio had constructed an invisible wall out of caution, or habit, or fear of being too much. Or maybe not enough.

“You planning to sleep all the way over there?” Agatha asked, voice dry but warm, tugging at the edge of her blanket. “What is this, a catholic school dance? You don’t need to leave room for the holy spirit, he’s not coming.”

Rio turned her head slowly to meet her gaze, lips parting slightly in protest, though nothing came out right away.

“I just...I didn’t want to assume..." she said finally, her voice low. “I didn’t want to take up space you weren’t offering.”

Agatha exhaled, soft and almost amused. She reached out and tapped the edge of Rio’s silk sleeve, then gently curled her fingers around Rio’s wrist.

“Sweetheart..." she said, pulling with just enough pressure to make her point, “if I didn’t want you close, you wouldn’t even be in my bed.”

Rio blinked once. Then twice. Her brow relaxed slightly, and a small, embarrassed smile flickered at the corner of her mouth. “Right.”

“So..." Agatha said, tugging again, “come here.”

Rio hesitated for half a second longer, but then she scooted across the mattress, careful and quiet, until their bodies were a breath apart. The silk of her borrowed pajamas brushed against Agatha’s bare skin, and both of them stilled, just for a moment, to feel it. The contrast was intoxicating, cool, smooth fabric against warm, open flesh.

Rio draped an arm around Agatha and nuzzled her head lightly against Agatha’s chest, her eyes fluttering shut, feeling her heart beat against her.

“Better?” Agatha asked softly.

Rio nodded. “Yea…much better.”

They lay like that for a while, limbs tangled with gentle intention, their bodies finally learning the shape of rest beside one another. Agatha ran her fingers slowly up and down Rio’s back through the silk, tracing small shapes, feeling the rise and fall of her breathing.

“I’m not used to this…” Rio whispered after a while. “...to being held like it’s ok.”

Agatha kissed her temple. “It’s more than ok.”

Rio didn’t answer right away. But she shifted closer, until there was no space between them at all.

Agatha smiled into the top of her head. “There she is..." she murmured.

Rio let out a tiny laugh, barely a breath. “I didn’t mean to move in this close. I think gravity pulled me in.”

“Sure..." Agatha said, teasing. “Blame physics.”

They fell quiet again, but it wasn’t empty. It was the kind of hush that buzzed with comfort, with meaning. Outside, the city hushed itself into sleep, and the apartment settled around them like it was exhaling too.

Rio’s hand found Agatha’s beneath the blankets, their fingers lacing instinctively, naturally. Agatha gave a gentle squeeze.

And Rio, for the first time in what felt like forever, felt entirely held, not just in arms, but in trust, in patience, in something she hadn’t known she needed until this very moment.

Something soft. Something steady. And something she thought that maybe felt a lot like ‘love.’

Chapter 11: fitting into place

Rio’s new life in New York began with three things.

A new pet.

A new high score at the pinball arcade.

A new girlfriend.

The sun spilled through the half closed blinds in buttery gold ribbons, slicing across the sheets and the slow rise and fall of two barely awake bodies. The hum of the city buzzed faintly through the window, horns, voices, the occasional bark of a dog, all of it softened by the quiet hush of a lazily spinning ceiling fan in the bedroom. The serenity wrapped around them like a held breath. The city below felt distant, muffled by walls and windows, the world narrowed down to just the two of them, suspended in a quiet that felt earned, in the afterglow of something real.

Agatha woke first, tangled in the sheets, her hair a wreck and her shirt slipping lazily off one shoulder. She blinked through the haze of sleep, disoriented for half a second, until she felt it.

Warmth. Breathing. Silk.

Rio was curled beside her, deep in sleep, one arm folded beneath her pillow, the other stretched toward Agatha, their hands almost touching. Her brow was faintly furrowed, like even dreaming required quiet concentration. The silk pajamas clung to her, wrinkled and soft, the shine catching the faint morning light.

Agatha smiled, slow and fond. The sight of Rio was so tender it made something ache in her chest. Rio curled beside her felt like a gift she hadn’t dared to ask for.

She inched closer and brushed their fingers together, just enough to feel the contact, not enough to wake her. Just enough to know that Rio in this way was real.

“Hey..." Agatha whispered after a while, her voice scratchy and low with sleep. “You alive in there?”

For a beat, there was no response. Then Rio’s eyelids fluttered open. She blinked slowly, as if the process of waking required negotiation, not just surrender. Her gaze drifted toward the window then back to Agatha.

“What…time is it?” she asked, voice soft, almost hoarse.

“Too early..." Agatha muttered, rolling to grab her phone from the nightstand. She squinted at the screen and winced. “11:13am.” She rolled back to face Rio. “Morning, champ.”

Rio stretched with the slow, careful grace of someone who wasn’t entirely sure she’d earned the right to take up space. She moved like unfolding paper, long limbs extending bit by bit, a soft groan catching in her throat. Her spine arched, then settled, breath leaving her lungs in a quiet exhale.

“Even the silence has noise..." she murmured, her voice trailing into the air between them.

Agatha yawned and shrugged one shoulder, letting her cheek press against the pillow. “It’s New York, baby. The city doesn’t sleep. It just naps with one eye open and a megaphone.”

Rio sat up slowly, moving the sheets off her, as if waking required a deliberate transition, body first, then mind. She let her feet find the floor, her gaze sweeping the room like she was cataloguing it all over again.

“This is my home now." she said, almost to herself. The words weren’t a question. They were an observation. A quiet truth being tested in the air.

Agatha sat up and looked at her. “Is…that ok with you?”

Rio looked over her shoulder, eyes soft. She nodded once, slowly. “It is. But I need to learn it. The city, I mean. I don’t want to just...exist here. I want to understand it.”

Agatha groaned, rubbing her face. “God, you're so earnest in the morning. It’s disgusting.”

Rio said nothing.

Agatha sighed. “Ok, but for the record, that’s adorable. And also impossible. This city doesn’t make sense. It thrives on nonsense.”

Rio shifted to face her fully, now wide awake, her expression thoughtful. “But I want to try.”

There was something so sure in her voice. Not loud. Not insistent. Just steady.

Agatha got up out of bed. “Fine. What do you want, then? A guided tour? A crash course in overpriced coffee and getting yelled at by a guy selling bootleg Hamilton tickets?”

Rio tilted her head thoughtfully. “Yes. All of it.”

A smile tugged at the corner of Agatha’s mouth. “You’re lucky you’re cute.”

Rio shrugged, casual. “I’m also very committed to efficient learning.”

Agatha snorted. “Yea, well, New York isn’t exactly optimized.”

Rio glanced toward the window, then back to her. “Then I guess I’ll need a good teacher.”

Agatha blinked up at her, heart skipping just a little. She tried to brush it off with a dramatic groan. “Ok, you want the Agatha Harkness Summer in the City Experience? You’ll get it.”
