The first lesson started the moment they stepped outside.

It was the kind of summer heat that stuck to your skin and made everything feel a little too loud. The pavement shimmered under the early afternoon sun, taxis screamed down the streets like they were on fire, and the air smelled faintly of hot garbage and possibility.

Agatha locked the door behind her and Rio as they walked down the stoop of the apartment building and started walking uptown.

Agatha was dressed like she was headed to a rooftop fashion editorial. Glittery gold pants that caught the sun like sequins at a disco, a silky, sleeveless lavender blouse that tucked in just so, and matching gold heels, like she had somewhere important to be even if she was just going to the corner bodega. A pair of oversized sunglasses shielded her eyes. Her hair styled in gentle waves.

Beside her, Rio wore faded black Levi’s with tears in the knees, her trusty well loved black Doc Martens, and a green t-shirt with “you wouldn’t download a t-shirt” written in white text on the front of it. A pair of black Ray Bans were on her head like a headband, with her hair in a messy bun.

The contrast between them was so stark it bordered on performance art.

Agatha side eyed Rio with the kind of grin that said she wasn’t sure whether to kiss her or pretend not to know her in public.

“Alright, genius. Rule number one…” She swept a hand out toward the busy street in front of them, the honking, sweltering chaos that was New York in the summertime. “You cannot optimize this city.”

Rio blinked at her. “Can’t…optimize?”

“Nope.” Agatha popped the ‘p’ as she stepped into the crosswalk and around a puddle that had no business being there in this heat. “It’s a mess. It’s always a mess. You try to apply logic to New York, it’ll slap you with a bagel and steal your wallet.”

Rio tilted her head slightly, following next to Agatha closely. “So…I should...adapt?”

Agatha gave a tight, approving smirk. “Yea. Adapt. Improvise. Surrender is more like it. New York is a middle finger to urban planning, not a flowchart.”

Rio nodded slowly, mentally filing that under ‘city protocols.’ “Noted.”

They kept walking, shoulder to shoulder, swallowed by the hum of the city, horns blaring, someone yelling in the distance, a courier nearly clipping a tourist at full speed. Typical chaos. The sidewalk shimmered ahead of them in long stretches of cracked concrete and scattered gum, and still, they walked the streets together like it was something they’d always done together.

And then reality hit Agatha. Sudden. Quiet. Heavy. Rio Vidal, Tetris champion, human computer, all logic and soft silence and wide eyes, was living with her here, in her apartment, in her space, in her life.

Agatha stared straight ahead, her stomach was in knots at the thought.

‘What the fuck do I do with that?’ She didn’t say it aloud, but she could feel it echoing in her chest like a song she hadn’t realized was stuck in her head. Beside her, Rio just kept walking, calm, observant, unconcerned, as if moving through Agatha’s world wasn’t something wild at all.

Agatha sighed, adjusting her sunglasses and running a hand through her hair, like it would lighten the remark. “I cannot believe you’re actually living with me.”

Rio, deadpan as ever, glanced at her. “Would you rather I had stayed with Mike?”

Agatha hesitated, scrunching her nose like she’d just tasted something bitter.

Mike. The thought of Rio’s wild, brilliant little brain, and her quiet, awkward softness, being housed under the same roof as a gamer boy with LED lights and a mysterious sticky substance covered Xbox controller? Agatha almost gagged. She could practically see it. Rio, couch surfing for weeks, living off Red Bull and Hot Pockets, slowly absorbing whatever toxic energy Mike gave off like a houseplant in bad lighting. At least with her, Agatha reasoned, Rio would get some culture, some taste. A throw pillow that wasn’t shaped like a PS5 controller. Literally anything better than, Mike.

Slowly, Agatha said, “No.”

Rio nodded, completely unbothered. “Good.”

A cab blared its horn right behind them, and Agatha instinctively grabbed Rio’s arm. Rio, meanwhile, turned her head, and barely blinked. Her eyes followed the cab, her expression calm, as if she’d already accounted for that possibility three steps ago.

“You seriously don’t flinch?” Agatha asked, releasing her grip.

Rio shrugged. “The cab wasn’t going to hit us.”

“You don’t know that.”

“I estimated the odds at 4.3% based on its velocity, angle of approach, and the driver’s facial expression.”

Agatha stared at her. “You are infuriating.”

“And yet..." Rio replied, glancing at her with a faint, knowing smile, “...here we are.”

Agatha huffed, but it wasn’t real. She was already smiling again, despite herself.

It was maddening how Rio, in all her quiet, calculated weirdness, somehow managed to sidestep every one of Agatha’s carefully placed emotional landmines. Like she could see them coming a mile away and just...rearranged the board. There was something undeniably smug about it too, even if Rio didn’t mean it to be. She didn’t gloat. She didn’t rub it in. She just kept being calm, unfazed, bizarrely competent. And Agatha, for all her sharp edges and practiced indifference, kept falling for it. Over and over.

Agatha had been fully prepared to spend the entire afternoon guiding her little Tetris genius through the beautiful disaster of Manhattan, expecting to witness some delicious, inevitable sensory overload. The chaos, the people, the smells, the car exhaust, the total disregard for personal space, it was supposed to break her just a little. Just enough to prove a point.

But it never happened, Rio was analyzing, memorizing, processing, with every step they took. Every street sign they passed, every traffic pattern, every subway map posted on a pole, she soaked it all up like a sponge that had been waiting its whole life for such a challenge like this, worthy of its capacity. She didn't gawk. She didn’t panic. Her eyes just moved constantly, quickly, calmly, like a machine learning a new system. It was the same look she had when studying a Tetris board seconds before a flawless line clear.

And honestly, it was starting to piss Agatha off a little. So, naturally, she did what she did best, she upped the stakes.

They reached a corner near Columbus Circle, the sun hanging heavy in the sky, casting sharp angles between the buildings. Agatha threw out a hand and flagged down a yellow cab, stepping up to the window and leaning in to whisper something to the driver. Then she turned, all cool confidence and smug delight, and opened the back door for Rio.

“Alright..." she said, jerking her chin toward the cab. “Time to see if you can survive on your own.”

Rio’s brows pulled together. “Is this...a test?”

Agatha grinned, teeth flashing. “Oh yea.”

Rio hesitated and Agatha caught the flicker in her eyes, interest, calculation, something that looked a lot like determination wearing a poker face.

Agatha leaned a little closer, one hand braced on the car door. “The test of a real New Yorker..." she said, her voice dropping into something almost conspiratorial, “...is finding your way home without directions. No help from your phone. No cabs. No GPS. Just you, the grid, and your tiny computer brain.”

Rio blinked. “You mean...walk?”

Agatha nodded, that annoying grin still in place. “Welcome to the maze, Vidal.”

Rio didn’t flinch. Didn’t protest. She looked up, squinting at the skyline, then back at Agatha. And with the same quiet, unshakeable confidence she had when locking in a game winning T-spin, she simply shook out her hands, and got into the cab.

Agatha closed the door and leaned toward the driver one last time and said under her breath, “44th and 7th. Drop her there. She’ll figure it out.”

The cab sped away and Agatha watched the blur of Rio disappear into the chaos of yellow taxis, pedestrian shoving, and someone playing drums on an upside down bucket across the street.

She folded her arms, half smiling, half sighing.

“Of course, she just went with it..." she muttered to herself, shaking her head as she walked back to the apartment. “What the hell did I just unleash into this city?”

Because now Rio was in it, alone, with nothing but her absurdly analytical brain, her emotional sponge of a heart, and the vaguest notion of where their apartment was.

– –

– –

From inside the cab, Rio stared out the window, eyes darting across the skyline like it was a Tetris board waiting to be solved. Every building was a block, every crosswalk a potential rotation point. She imagined the shapes of it all, the chaos, the motion, falling into place like tetrominoes, slotting together just right.

She was already mentally optimizing pedestrian flow, traffic light timing, and which sidewalk would give her the lowest probability of getting splashed by a bus. A man in a foam Statue of Liberty costume waved at her, and she blinked once, filed it away under ‘irrelevant distraction.’

The problem was, Rio had no idea where she was going.

Agatha had leaned into the front seat and whispered something to the driver before Rio got in, like she was orchestrating a secret mission. And now? The cab’s path was anything but logical. They’d circled the same set of blocks at least twice. Maybe three times. The driver’s movements were inconsistent, no clear route, no methodical turns, no adherence to grid structure. Just pure chaos.

Was he lost? Was this a test? Was he trying to disorient her? Who knew.

It was like someone handed the wheel to a human random number generator.

Still, Rio didn’t panic. She just recalculated. And recalculated again.

Agatha had thrown down a challenge to her, and Rio had no intention of losing. Not here. Not in this oversized, over caffeinated maze of a city. Not when Agatha was waiting at the other end, smirking, smug, and almost definitely underestimating her.

She wasn’t just walking home. Rio was playing Agatha’s game now…even if the opening level involved a cab driver with no apparent concept of efficiency or purpose.

The cab jerked to a stop, and the driver muttered without looking back, “The pretty lady already paid. Now get the hell outta my cab.”

Rio stepped out into a riot of light and sound. Times Square swallowed her instantly.

She didn’t move after the cab sped away. She just stood there on the corner of 44th and 7th, one foot on the curb, the other on the street, as the city surged around her like a living organism made entirely of impatience and fluorescent overload.

It was loud, so loud. Horns, heels on pavement, snippets of music crashing into one another like static, someone beatboxing on a corner, someone else yelling into a megaphone dressed like a giant Elmo. Steam billowed up from a manhole cover. The smell of hot dogs and engine grease mixed with sugar from a street vendor selling roasted nuts. There were lights, massive, blinking, scrolling, moving, and crowds pressing in from all sides, a blur of limbs and colors and bags and shouting.

Rio didn’t panic. She absorbed.

The lights weren’t just lights. They were patterns. Timing. Rhythm. The advertisements blinked in a sequence that her brain immediately began calculating. The way pedestrians surged forward with the changing walk signs resembled waveforms, filed in her brain under ‘crowd fluid dynamics.’ The buildings rose up around her like a canyon of geometry, hard lines, glowing edges, and shapes.

Rio tilted her head slightly and began to move, slow but purposeful, weaving through the crowd not by instinct but by prediction. She watched the gait of the man ahead of her, too wide, avoid. The woman with a rolling suitcase, left side, overtaking speed 1.3x average. Child with balloon radius three feet, path erratic. She adjusted, compensated, and roamed.

Rio stared up. The LED billboards were stunning. Not because they were loud, or bright, or cinematic, but because they were mechanical. Predictable in their timing, even if their content wasn’t. One switched every thirty seconds. Another cycled images in a loop, 7 images total, 12 seconds each. She counted. She catalogued. She noticed a single ad glitched for 0.4 seconds on its loop, something no one else would have seen. Her brain stored it anyway.

Rio turned in a slow circle, head tilted back, scanning the curve of each building, the digital ribbons winding around their faces, the symmetry and asymmetry of the skyline. Every angle was like a new level. A new board.

“Times Square…” she muttered to herself. “Tetris, but 3D.”

She took a few steps back and bumped into someone. “Sorry..." she mumbled, stepping aside, already shifting her path. The man didn’t acknowledge her as he kept walking. Her eyes followed him for half a second, then moved on, adjusting, recalibrating. She didn’t take it personally. She wasn’t here to be seen. She was here to observe.

A tourist family took a selfie near red steps. A man in a Spiderman suit struck a pose beside a child who was crying far too loud. A woman with glitter on her face twirled on roller skates while holding a sign about love and free hugs. It was all noise. All clutter.

And yet…it had rhythm. If she squinted, and tilted her head a little, she could almost see the logic hidden underneath the chaos. Almost.

She realized, that Agatha was right: you can’t optimize New York.

But you could understand it. You could listen to it in a way most people never thought to. You could see the logic beneath the mess, the order behind the chaos. You just had to look differently.

As she wandered, Rio found herself following the small markers no one else seemed to notice, the rhythm of traffic lights, the placement of street signs, the subtle curve of each intersection. With every block, her internal map refined itself, locking into place like the grid that New York was. Surprisingly, she found out quickly, she wasn’t that far from the apartment. She had looped farther than needed in the cab, by a long shot, yes, but it wasn’t all for no reason. It was all data collection.

She didn’t need her phone. She didn’t ask for directions. She already knew which way the buildings were pulling her, which street she had to cross next.

She paused at the next crosswalk, shifting her weight and twitching her fingers. This wasn’t just about getting home.

She thought of Agatha then. Her voice. The smirk she wore like armor. The lavender in her perfume, the green in her eyes when the light hit them just right. She imagined Agatha watching her now, arms crossed, muttering something like, ‘I swear to God, she’s reverse engineering the entire borough.’ And Rio was. Block by block. Step by step.

And somewhere beneath all that calculation, all that structure and shape and motion, there was something warm and alive buzzing inside Rio’s chest.

“I need to stay here for a while..." she said quietly to herself. Going home too soon didn’t feel right. ‘That wasn’t the point of the challenge,’ she thought. Agatha hadn’t just wanted her to return. She’d wanted her to learn something. To listen.

And Rio intended to pass this test with a perfect score.

– –

– –

Rio was gone for hours. Which meant, miraculously, for once, Agatha actually won.

Agatha had never been messy, at least, not by her standards, but now that she was technically living with someone, she found using the time to herself to clean up the apartment. The piles of half read books on every surface suddenly looked less like charming intellectual chaos and more like a fire hazard. She’d cleared off the kitchen counter, and unearthed parts that hadn’t seen daylight in months, relocated three rogue coffee mugs from the windowsill, and even reorganized space so Rio would have a proper place for her absurdly minimal belongings. It wasn’t about presentation, well, ok, maybe a little, it was about making the space feel less like hers and more like theirs. And that felt weirdly huge.

She curled up on the couch with a new book she picked up on the way home, ‘Look Me in the Eye’ by John Elder Robison. It was supposed to be casual reading, mild curiosity, nothing serious, but within a few pages, Agatha was treating the book as if it held all the cheat codes to Rio. “Goddamn it..." she muttered. “I’m starting to understand her.” She kept petting Scratchy on her lap. “She’s never allowed to find out I’m learning things about her like this, buddy.”

After a bit, she put the book on her bookshelf with all the others and started lazily scrolling through her phone, half expecting to hear the front door burst open and a triumphant Rio stride in like she'd conquered the city.

Instead, there was a knock. Soft. Hesitant. Agatha frowned and got up. Scratchy hopping away and sitting in his chair in the corner.

Agatha padded barefoot to the door, expecting, at most, a slightly ruffled, mildly irritated, perhaps sweat dampened Rio. Disheveled, flustered, maybe even clinging to the last threads of dignity.

She opened the door. And promptly forgot how to breathe.

There, standing dead center in the doorway, framed by the late golden spill of sunset behind her, was Rio Vidal in full tourist mode.

She wore an oversized ‘I <3 NY’ t-shirt that reached nearly to her knees, a Yankees baseball cap cocked just slightly sideways like she didn’t understand how hats worked, and smears of bright red dye staining her hands, her shirt, and somehow the edge of her lips, chin, and jaw like she’d narrowly escaped a vampire attacking her. She looked like someone had dared her to camouflage as a Times Square souvenir stand, and she’d said, ‘challenge accepted.’

Agatha froze. Her mouth opened. Closed. Opened again. Then she cackled. Bent doubled over, hand over her stomach, laughter cracking out of her in gasping, delighted waves.

“RIO…” she managed between wheezes “...what the actual fuck are you wearing?!”

Rio blinked, serene as ever. She looked down at herself. Then back up. “I thought it was...appropriate attire..." she said evenly, as if she were defending a Ph.D. thesis.

Rio walked in and Agatha closed the door behind her.

Agatha wiped at her eyes, breathless from laughing. “You…you look like you lost a bet with a gift shop.”

“I did not…” Rio said, taking off her Docs. “...however, that cab driver was terrible.”

Agatha winked at her. “Cab driver? Terrible? Oh…I wonder why.”

Rio stared gravely. “You didn’t.”

Agatha grinned, shaking her head. “I did. Welcome to New York, dumbass.”

Rio tilted her head. “You…you sent me into an uncontrolled urban chaos zone telling someone on purpose to not have route optimization????”

Agatha raised an eyebrow. “That’s how you learn, Vidal. You want real world XP, you gotta do the random side quests.”

Rio didn’t argue. She just sighed through her nose like someone accepting a reality that would’ve happened whether she liked it or not.

Then Agatha asked, “So, ok…what was the hardest part about getting home?”

Rio paused, eyes narrowing slightly, and answered in complete seriousness. “…the pigeons.”

Agatha lost it again. “The pigeons???”

Rio nodded solemnly, arms crossed over the gaudy red heart on her shirt. “They don’t follow logical movement patterns. I tried predicting their flight arcs. It was...inconsistent to say the least.”

“They’re rats with wings, Rio…Of course they’re chaotic.”

“They are aerial anomalies and I don’t like them.” Rio said matter of factly.

Agatha was laughing so hard now she had to sit down, collapsing onto the couch with a breathless wheeze. “God, you’re hopeless.”

Rio raised one eyebrow. “Am I?”

Agatha glanced over at her, still catching her breath, the grin tugging stubbornly at her lips. “…No, but you are insane.”

Rio made her way to the couch and sat beside Agatha, still in her ridiculous tourist getup, knees a little too close together, posture still too stiff for someone covered in red stains.

Agatha leaned back, stretching her legs out, watching her with fond disbelief. “Alright, Vidal. You survived your first solo New York trial. A full blown initiation. How do you feel?”

Rio was quiet for a moment. Then she looked over, eyes sharp and clear beneath the brim of her hat, and said, “...sticky.”

Agatha groaned, dropping her head back against the cushions, laughing. “What even is all that???”

Rio fidgeted with her hands, and murmured low “...Spiderman ice cream.”

Agatha turned her head slowly, staring at Rio like she had just confessed to a war crime. “Spiderman ice cream???”

Rio nodded solemnly. “From a cart. It was melting. I tried to eat it efficiently.”

Agatha blinked. “And by efficiently, you mean...?”

Rio looked down at herself, at the faint red smears on her shirt, the sticky sheen still clinging to her fingers. “I didn’t anticipate the structural instability of the gumball eyes.”

Agatha snorted. “Oh my God. You lost to a popsicle.”

“I didn’t lose..." Rio said quickly. “I simply failed to optimize.”

Agatha was wheezing now. “You are covered in sugar and regret.”

“Who you’re dating is slightly radioactive now..." Rio added, completely deadpan. “If the coloring was accurate.”

“Oh great. So I’m dating a sticky, mutant tourist.”

Rio tilted her head. “You didn’t deny the dating part.”

Agatha’s mouth opened. Then shut. Then opened again.

“I hate you..." she muttered, face flushed.

Rio smiled faintly. “I’m sticky, not deaf.”

Agatha groaned, standing up and grabbing Rio by the wrist. “Ok, come on. You’re showering. Immediately. Before you become permanently bonded to my couch like some kind of souvenir slime.”

Rio followed obediently, then paused. “Do I get another Spiderman ice cream after?”

“No. You get soap. Lots of it.”

“Ice cream and soap would be a balanced outcome.”

Agatha looked over her shoulder, smirking. “You really are out here trying to negotiate with me while looking like a melted cartoon character.”

Rio blinked. “It’s called multitasking.”

Agatha dragged her toward the bathroom. “It’s called disgusting. Come on, mutant.”

Rio sighed dramatically. “Sticky, not evil.”

And with that, the bathroom door shut behind them with Agatha muttering something about “spandex trauma” and Rio already calculating the optimal soap to stickiness removal ratio.

– –

– –

The bathroom quickly filled with steam, the kind that clung to the walls and softened the edges of everything, like the whole room had been dropped in warm fog. The shower hissed steadily behind the curtain, and Agatha, fully dressed, perched on the countertop. She was scrolling her phone aimlessly, but not really reading anything. Just listening. Occasionally glancing at the outline of Rio’s silhouette behind the curtain. She could hear the way she moved, quiet, methodical, like even rinsing conditioner was something Rio approached with quiet intention.

“You’ve never had a real relationship, have you?” Agatha asked suddenly, as if she were asking about the weather.

Behind the curtain, there was a pause. Then, Rio’s voice, calm and unaffected “Define real.”

Agatha smirked. “That answers that.”

“I’ve dated..." Rio said, the sound of water shifting as she turned slightly. “Three people, technically. None lasted long. It felt like I…was...being tested. Like I had to perform to be the right kind of human.”

Agatha hummed, dropping her phone into her lap. “So, what, they bailed when you didn’t emote on cue?”

“One of them made flashcards..." Rio said flatly. “With drawings.”

Agatha blinked. “Flashcards?”

“For facial expressions. She meant well.”

Agatha laughed. “That’s either endearing or wildly condescending, and I can’t decide which.”

Rio didn’t respond immediately. Then, with a faint shrug in her voice “Probably both.”

There was silence for a few beats, the kind that hung heavy but not uncomfortable.

Agatha let out a breath, picked at her nail polish, then said, “Alright. My turn.”

The water kept running, but Rio was listening. Agatha could feel it.

“I’ve had a handful of relationships. Usually with women who talked a lot and wanted me to be less…me.” She shrugged, even though no one could see it. “They’d say things like ‘soften up’ or ‘just be a little less much.’ One girl told me I was like dating a knife in heels.”

“Is that…a compliment?”

Agatha snorted. “I took it as one.”

Rio was quiet again and Agatha could practically feel her thinking.

“I always thought I wanted someone bold..." Agatha went on. “Loud. Confident. Someone who’d match me in energy and never get drowned out. But they always left me feeling like I had to play a version of myself to keep things going. Eventually, I got tired of the performance.”

Agatha looked up, toward the shower curtain, steam curling off the top like the room itself was trying to listen in.

“Turns out..." Agatha said slowly, “I like quiet people. People who aren’t trying to win the room. Who say weird shit and mean it. Who forget they’re hot and then act surprised when you tell them.”

There was another beat of silence behind the curtain. Then Rio peeked her head out of the shower, then saying almost too soft to hear “You think I’m hot?”

Agatha groaned and rolled her eyes. “That is not the part you’re supposed to focus on.”

“It’s the part that stood out.”

“Because you’re a menace.”

“Because I like data..." Rio said smiling at her then returning to the shower.

Agatha chuckled, rolling her eyes. “You’re lucky you’re cute.”

Rio turned off the water, and the bathroom suddenly felt louder without the constant sound of water in the background. Agatha moved to the doorway of the bathroom to give Rio some space, handing Rio a towel.

“Are you worried?” Rio asked, her voice quieter now, wrapping herself in the towel.

Agatha blinked, trying to not keep her mind on the flash of Rio’s naked body she saw only briefly. “About what?”

“That this’ll go the same way as the others.”

Agatha hesitated. She hadn’t expected that. “Yea..." she admitted. “Kind of. Different people, same ending, that’s how it usually goes.” She picked her fingers. “But this doesn’t feel like the others..." she added. “Which, frankly, is terrifying.”

Rio looked around on the countertops, found Agatha’s brush and started using it to comb out her hair. “Then don’t run..." Rio said, staring Agatha dead in the eyes.

Agatha sighed. “You make it sound so easy.”

“It’s not…” Rio replied. “...but we can choose easy.”

“We can choose easy..." Agatha repeated, her voice low. “What does that even mean?”

“It means we don’t have to make it hard just because we’re used to hard.”

Agatha huffed, but it wasn’t dismissive, more like she was trying to exhale the tension in her shoulders. “I don’t know how to not make things difficult. I’ve spent so much time trying to prove I’m not soft that I don’t think I know how to be soft without it turning into a liability.”

Rio turned to face her, brush still in hand, her expression calm and completely unflinching.

“You were soft the second you let me stay.”

Agatha opened her mouth. Closed it. She blushed.

“I was high on endorphins and exhausted…” she mumbled.

Rio shrugged. “Still counts.”

Agatha let out a breath of something that wasn’t quite a laugh. “You always do that. Say something devastating in the calmest possible tone.”

“I’m not trying to be devastating..." Rio said simply. “I just don’t think feelings should be that complicated. People make them complicated.”

Agatha arched a brow. “People like me?”

Rio held her gaze. “Yes.”

Agatha let that hang in the air for a moment, then smirked faintly, like it almost stung, but didn’t.

“Ok…fine..." Agatha muttered. “But just for the record? You don’t make it easy, either. You're like dating a walking logic puzzle that occasionally says something weirdly poetic and then goes right back to acting like a human calculator.”

Rio blinked. “That sounds accurate.”

Agatha snorted, shaking her head. “Of course you think it does.”

They were quiet for a beat.

Agatha turned and with a little hop, hoisted herself up onto the counter again. She rested her hands on either side, legs swinging slightly as she watched Rio put up her hair in her classic messy bun. “C’mere..." she said, soft and half smirking.

Rio obeyed, still in the towel, her skin damp and flushed from the heat of the shower. She stepped between Agatha’s legs, resting her hands gently on her thighs like she didn’t want to assume too much. Like she still wasn’t quite used to being allowed to touch this much of Agatha without being burned.

Agatha’s hands came up, brushing her hands across Rio’s collar bones, and settling them by her shoulders, holding her in an embrace. She stayed there, tracing small shapes into her skin with her fingertips. There was a silence, thick but not heavy, just quiet.

“I’m trying not to run..." Agatha said softly. “So don’t…get all optimized and perfect and unreadable on me, ok? Let me try and keep up.”

Rio looked up at her, earnest. “I’m not trying to be unreadable. I just don’t always know what people want from me.” She hesitated, then added, quieter, “But you keep up better than anyone else I’ve ever known.”

Agatha’s expression softened, not quite a smile, but something warmer, quieter, settling just behind her eyes. Agatha exhaled. “I don’t want anything from you.” A beat. “I just want you as you are right now.”

That hit something in Rio, something deep, wordless. Her hands slid up to Agatha’s waist, tentative but wanting.

She rested her cheek lightly against Agatha’s chest, listening to her heartbeat for a moment.

Then, quiet and sincere, Rio looked up at Agatha and muttered slightly “You know I’d do anything for you, right?”

Agatha looked down, one eyebrow raising with a slow, amused curve. “God..." she said, voice low and wicked, “you’re so pathetic, Rio.”

Rio blinked, totally smitten. “You say that like it’s a bad thing.”

Agatha smirked and leaned kissing her, soft, slow, lazy. The kind of kiss that made time stretch and lose meaning. Rio returning the kiss and moving in closer to Agatha.

They stayed like that for a moment, bodies in sync, all the noise outside the bathroom gone.

Then…knock knock knock. They both froze.

Agatha groaned. “God, if that’s the universe trying to make a point, I swear I’ll hex it.”

Rio didn’t move, still close. “Do you…have that ability?”

Agatha gave her a flat look. “Honestly? I might.”

They exchanged a glance, quiet, amused, and very much not done with this conversation.

But the door waited.

So Agatha slipped off the counter, one last quick kiss pressed to Rio’s lips, and padded out to answer it.

And the steam behind them lingered, like the moment had only paused. Not ended.

– –

– –

Agatha cracked open the door open an inch and groaned. “Of course it’s you.”

Jen stood in the hall, arms crossed, eyebrow arched so high it might have filed a flight plan. She wore a cropped black leather jacket over a pink cotton turtleneck and sleek black trousers that flared just enough to let her heels say ‘I dare you.’ Gold geometric earrings framed her face.

“Well, well, well..." Jen drawled. “Look who’s alive.”

Agatha tried to close the door. “Not a good time.”

Jen stuck her foot between the door and the frame. “So you are alive. Good. I came to confirm you weren’t being eaten by your rabbit or slowly decomposing under a pile of laundry.”

“I’m fine..." Agatha said quickly, glancing over her shoulder. “Really. Thriving. You can go now.”

Jen narrowed her eyes. “Why do you look like you’re hiding a one night stand?”

“I’m not..." Agatha said, voice high and defensive in a way that absolutely did not help her case.

Jen tried to push past her. Agatha pushed back. “One minute…” she hissed, slamming the door shut in Jen’s face.

From the other room, Rio called out, “Should I…stay hidden? This seems…time sensitive.”

Agatha rushed in. “She can’t see you like this.”

Rio was standing awkwardly near the bedroom door, wrapped in just a towel, holding one of her own shirts out in front of her. It was bright green with the words ‘I paused my mental breakdown to be here’ written in Comic Sans.

Agatha grabbed it and threw it across the room. “Absolutely not.”

“I have this one..." Rio offered, pulling up another: ‘I’m not antisocial, I’m antistupid.’

Rio was annihilating everyone who dared to play Tetris against her.

One by one, editors, junior dev reviewers, layout artists, even one of ‘the Kyles,’ each stepped up, cocky and grinning, only to get ruthlessly outpaced, outscored, and psychologically broken in under two minutes.

“Yo, is she cheating?” one of them yelled as the screen blinked ‘GAME OVER’ in increasingly humiliating fashion.

“She’s not even looking at the screen..." groaned another.

“I think I just saw my soul leave my body..." a third muttered, clutching the controller like it had betrayed him.

Rio smiled politely and shook hands with each of them as she defeated them.

The room buzzed with a quirky, almost fun energy, which was weird for the workplace. Agatha, still sitting with her laptop balanced on her knees, watched Rio with the faintest smirk. Her little Tetris savant. So terrifying. So calm, and so hers, dismantling grown men like they were confused shapes in Rio’s favorite grid. It was perfect.

The crowd began to thin as the office wound down for the day. Eventually, the last few staffers trickled toward the elevators, muttering plans for drinks or DoorDash. Agatha packed up her laptop, stretching her neck side to side, when one of ‘the Kyles,’ possibly Kyle #2, came up next to Rio, who was now delicately putting away her rig with exact precision.

“Hey..." Kyle #2 said. “You ever done the Tetris tournament in Chinatown?”

Rio paused. Her head tilted. “What?” she asked, slowly. “The what?”

Agatha looked up immediately. The tone in Rio’s voice had changed, like someone had just mentioned there was free money being given out, or told her of a hidden final level she hadn’t unlocked yet.

Kyle #2 smirked, clearly enjoying the moment. “It’s kind of a word of mouth thing. Invite only. You show up to a bodega with a yellow awning in Doyers Street, go to the freezer door, and give them password, ‘T-Spin Double.’”

Rio’s eyes went wide. Her mouth opened. Then closed. Then opened again.

Agatha raised an eyebrow. Uh oh.

“The people there are all into retro games, but the people for Tetris are in the back." Kyle #2 continued. “You might give them a run for their money.”

Rio nodded once, sharp and mechanical, the way she did when inputting something crucial into her mental log.

“Thank you...Kyle." she said with deadly sincerity.

Kyle #2 smirked. “I’ll see you there? 8 pm sharp.”

Agatha stood, closing her laptop. “Alright, champ. That’s enough for one day. Finish packing it up, we’ve got errands.”

Rio was already closing the case. “Yes. Errands.”

Agatha slung her bag over one shoulder. “First, we’ll get you your key. Then, we’re going clothes shopping, on me.”

Rio blinked. “Clothes? Shopping????”

“Yes. If I’m dragging you to Japan, you’re not showing up looking like you’re trying to win a LAN party in 2006.”

Rio looked down at what she was wearing. She frowned slightly. “But this outfit’s optimal for Japan.”

Agatha rolled her eyes. “We’re not optimizing. We’re accessorizing.”

Rio brightened. “Can I…at least pick the color palette?”

“You can try. But just so we’re clear, if you insist on another meme shirt, I’m leaving you at the register.”

Rio sighed. “Fine.”

Agatha paused in the doorway, watching Rio, sharp eyed and excited, texting Mike and Billy about the tournament with more exclamation points than Agatha thought possible for her.

– –

– –

The moment Rio spotted Jen across the store, a deep pit of dread unfurled in her stomach.

She tugged at the hem of her shirt like it might become a shield. No such luck.

“This is going to kill me..." Rio whispered to Agatha.

Agatha, standing beside her, gave her a small pat on the back. “You’ll survive.”

“Will I?” Rio said nervously.

“I mean, probably..." Agatha said. “Unless you offend Jen’s aesthetic sensibilities. Then, no. That’s fatal.”

They were standing just inside the wide, glass entrance of Citrine, a high end boutique nestled between two art galleries in SoHo. The interior was white marble and warm lighting, all subtle gold accents and hand curated racks. It smelled like old money and bergamot. The kind of place that had one of a kind items and non-existent price tags that whispered, ‘You can't afford me, but try anyway.’

Jen turned at the sound of the door. She was dressed like she’d just stepped off a Paris runway. Wide leg black trousers, a pink sleeveless turtleneck, gold bangles that clinked when she moved, and a pair of white pointed toe heels that defied physics. Her expression was unreadable, save for the sharp arch of one eyebrow that spelled trouble.

“Oh good..." Jen said, striding over. “You brought her.”

Rio blinked. “You say that like I wouldn’t be here.”

“Oh, I’ve been waiting for this since Agatha asked me to help…" Jen replied, smiling sweetly. “And you do need it…this is going to be fun.”

Agatha snorted. “Jen, please. Be nice. She’s a fashion baby.”

Jen circled Rio once like a shark in Balenciaga, hands on her hips. “What’s the goal here?”

Agatha folded her arms. “She’s going to Japan with me.”

“She looks like she’s going to reboot a server..." Jen replied flatly.

Rio, to her credit, stood completely still and just said, “This is my optimal outfit for…everything.”

Jen blinked. “Of course it is.” And then, terrifyingly, Jen smiled.

5 minutes later, Rio was deep in fashion hell. Or heaven. She couldn’t decide.

She’d been ushered into a private fitting area, one of those sun drenched alcoves lined with plush chairs and a gilded mirror that probably cost more than her entire wardrobe. Agatha had sprawled across a velvet loveseat with a glass of complimentary champagne in one hand and a smirk on her face. Jen was directing the staff of the store like a general mid campaign.

“This feels like a boss level..." Rio muttered from behind the curtain.

“You’re doing amazing, sweetie..." Agatha called back.

“I haven’t even stepped out yet.” Rio said flatly.

Jen pulled open the curtain and Rio stood there, frozen, in a crisp green suit with sharp lapels and a high collared white blouse that hugged her frame in all the right ways. She looked like a genius AI programmer who’d casually take over the world.

Agatha dropped her phone. Jen clapped as Rio stepped out into the space.

“Oh, yes…” Jen said, walking a slow circle around her. “This is giving tactical CEO who never blinks and only dates museum curators.”

Rio glanced in the mirror, brow furrowed. “It doesn’t have pockets.”

“Fashion doesn’t care about your functionality..." Jen said. “Suffer with the rest of us.”

Agatha laughed, tilting her head. “You look…SO hot.”

Rio blinked and smiled. “I do?”

“Like, alarmingly more so.” Jen admitted.

Rio stood there for a long moment, studying herself in the mirror. She smoothed the front of the jacket. Her hands twitched, wanting to adjust it, optimize it, but…it didn’t need fixing.

“This is weird..." Rio said quietly.

“What is?” Agatha asked, gentler.

Rio looked at her own reflection like she was a character she hadn’t unlocked before. “Feeling like I…fit.”

Agatha stood and crossed the room, resting a hand on Rio’s shoulder and looking at her in the mirror. “You already do.”

Jen cleared her throat. “Ok, enough romcom tension. We’ve got more looks to try on.”

The second outfit was a flowing deep green shirt dress with a cinched waist and buttons down the front. Jen paired it with soft black loafers and a gold pendant that Rio could not stop touching. Rio touched the pendant again, fingers lingering. “This necklace is...oddly soothing.”

Agatha leaned forward from her seat. “You look like you’re about to casually ruin someone’s life at brunch.”

“She looks chic,” Jen said. “It’s giving mysterious literary professor who’s definitely hiding something.”

“Statistical probability says I am,” Rio replied.

The third was a wide leg jumpsuit in light green with a plunging neckline that made Agatha’s eyes glue to Rio’s chest. Rio blinked at her reflection. “There’s...a lot of clavicle.”

“That’s not clavicle,” Agatha said, eyes going wide. “That’s foreplay.”

Jen laughed. “Honey, you look like a Bond girl who beat Bond at baccarat and walked out richer.”

“Is it... too much?” Rio asked.

“It’s exactly enough,” Agatha said quicky. “And if anyone says otherwise, I’ll fight them.”

The fourth was an ivory linen shirt tucked into high waisted green tailored pants that made Agatha genuinely speechless for a solid fifteen seconds.

Jen gave an approving nod. “This one says, ‘I own a vineyard and a lesbian publishing house.’”

Agatha tilted her head. “Yea, it says ‘I know my wine and your secrets.’”

Rio looked at her reflection and frowned thoughtfully. “I feel like I should be holding a wine glass.”

“You can hold me,” Agatha said without missing a beat.

Jen groaned. “Gross. Adorable. But gross.”

The fifth was a moss green pleated mini skirt with a tucked in dark green turtleneck and a cropped blazer in emerald.

“This feels...structured,” Rio said. “Contained. I like it.”

Agatha gave her a slow once over. “You look like you overhauled someone’s entire company during a coffee break.”

Jen raised a brow. “It’s very CEO of a zero emissions law firm. In the best way.”

“Can I keep this one?” Rio asked quietly.

“Only if you wear it the next time we go out to dinner,” Agatha said.

The last was a forest green satin camisole tucked into high waisted black paperbag trousers, with a sheer green shawl layered over it.

Rio turned slightly and watched the shawl swish. “This one moves when I walk.”

Agatha pressed a hand to her chest. “God help me, I’m gonna short circuit.”

“This is the ‘accidentally turns heads and pretends not to notice’ look,” Jen said. “It’s dangerous.”

“But I do notice,” Rio said. “I just don’t know what to do with the noticing.”

Agatha stepped closer and murmured, “You let me deal with that part.”

By the end of the spree, there were six carefully packed bags labeled and folded by the staff of the store, a very smug Jen, and a still dazed Rio back in her meme shirt, standing by the door like she’d just emerged from a dimension where people knew what colors worked with their skin tone.

Agatha walked over, “Well?”

Rio blinked. “I think I blacked out in there. Somewhere.”

“You looked like you were about to smile.” Agatha giggled.

“I feel like I just solved a new kind of puzzle,” Rio admitted.

Agatha nudged her gently with an elbow. “You did good, champ.”

Jen raised a brow. “You did great. Honestly, if I didn’t know you were the world Tetris champion, I’d think you were just a very sexy mannequin we dressed up for fun.”

“I think that was a compliment?” Rio said, glancing between them.

Agatha shrugged. “It was…kind of.”

And with that, they pushed out through the boutique doors, the soft ring of the bell fading behind them.

Agatha, Rio, and Jen exited onto the street with the shopping bags swinging between them, the afternoon sun glinting off the storefront glass.

Rio glanced over at Jen. “That…wasn’t as painful as I thought it would be.”

“You crushed it.” Jen laughed.

Rio grinned. “Maybe we’ll do stuff together for New York fashion week?”

Agatha shook her head. “Oh fuck me, you’d optimize us to be able to see everything I’d want to.”

“No promises, but I could try.” Rio winked.

– –

– –

The group made their way back uptown, designer tags peeking from the folds of crisp tissue paper in the bags Jen and Agatha carried, while Rio still had her rig case in hand. The sidewalk buzzed with late afternoon noise, car horns, someone yelling at a taxi, a dog wearing sunglasses barking at a pigeon, and for once, Agatha didn’t feel like growling at the entire city.

Then they passed it.

A tiny, grimy shop wedged between a vape store and a psychic, “Sunshine Too” emblazoned in graffiti on the glass. The window was full of poorly screen printed shirts like “I’m Not Arguing, I’m Just Explaining Why I’m Right” and “MILF: Man, I Love Frogs.”

Rio’s steps faltered. Her eyes went wide like she was witnessing holy relics. She stopped in front of the window and stared at a particularly heinous lime green tee that said “Math Is Hard But So Am I.”

Agatha followed Rio’s gaze and broke into a wicked grin. “Oh, absolutely not.”

Rio looked at her. “But…”

“No..." Agatha said. “You just spent two hours in the Citadel of Couture. You are not rebooting your wardrobe with…whatever this is.”

Rio blinked. “But it’s perfectly on brand for me.”

“Fine….I’ll allow one thing..." Agatha said, pointing sternly at her. “But I get to pick it.”

Rio nodded solemnly, and Agatha handed over her bags to Jen. “Supervise her. Make sure she doesn’t wander in and buy something with a Minion on it.”

“I make no promises..." Jen muttered, already amused.

Agatha ducked inside, stared at the wall of shirts, all of them absolute crimes against cotton, and returned just three minutes later with a single bag in hand.

She held up the bag proudly. “I found you a gift, Smashboy.”

Rio took the bag, peeked inside, then pulled out a maroon t-shirt. Blocky white letters that said on the front “DON’T BULLY ME I’LL CUM” in all caps.

Jen spat her gum out mid laugh. Rio stared at it for a long moment, horrified, but also, something else glinting in her eyes.

“Very funny..." Rio deadpanned, then handed Agatha her case. “My turn.”

Agatha blinked. “Wait, what?”

Rio darted inside before either of them realized.

Agatha and Jen watched through the smudged window as Rio browsed with all the intensity of someone trying to disarm a bomb.

“Oh no..." Agatha said.

“OH YEAH..." Jen replied.

Rio inspected the wall, tapped her chin, picked something up, paused…then went to the register, paid, then emerged from the store with a deeply pleased expression.

Rio came up to Agatha and Jen, then pulled the shirt from the bag and held it in both hands like a proud game show host.

White cotton. A picture of two photorealistic dolphins mid jump, black outlined white text overlaid the image “DADDY’S LITTLE SQUIRTER.”

Agatha screeched and her face fully flushed. Jen doubled over, gasping for air as she burst out laughing.

Rio just smiled, entirely too pleased with herself. “What? It’s thematic.”

“You are a monster,” Agatha wheezed grabbing the shirt and stuffing it back in the bag.

Jen wiped tears from her eyes. “I take back everything I ever said about your fashion instincts. You’re a chaotic genius, Rio.”

Agatha handed Rio back her case as they continued walking on down the street, laughing so hard at themselves that time seemed to pass in a blink.

As they reached Agatha’s building, still giggling, Jen slowed down and gestured at Rio. “Ok, but isn’t she going home now?”

Rio, deadpan replied, “I live here now.”

Jen stopped walking. “Wait. You’re serious? You’re actually shacking up with Miss Ice Queen ‘Don’t Touch Me While I’m Editing?’”

Rio produced a gleaming gold key from her pocket and held it up like it was a badge of honor.

“Got a key..." Rio said.

Jen stared. “Holy shit.”

Agatha smirked and unlocked the door. “Behold, domesticity.”

“Do you cook for her too?” Jen asked, mock horror creeping in.

“She makes the coffee..." Agatha said. “Really well. It’s very upsetting.”

“I optimize the ratio of the beans to water. It’s all about timing." Rio added.

Jen shook her head. “God help me, this is actually cute.”

As they climbed the stairs, Scratchy thumped somewhere above them like he could sense their return.

“You hear that?” Agatha said, sighing.

Rio nodded. “The judgment never stops.”

Jen chuckled. “I don’t know if I’m more surprised you let her move in or that she survived you long enough to want to.”

Agatha shrugged. “She’s persistent.”

“I’m a Tetris player..." Rio replied. “...I know how to wait for the right piece.”

Jen blinked. “Ok, that was actually smooth. What the hell.”

Agatha just muttered, “Kill me now..." and stepped into the apartment.

– –

– –

They barely had time to drop the shopping bags inside the apartment before Jen was already calling a Lyft. “Because this secret tournament is in a seedy bodega, I’m bringing mace..." she muttered.

Agatha and Jen waited for Rio as she changed her shirt, coming out of the bedroom in one that read “Only Fans” with a pile of line art of various types of cooling fans. Her hair was pulled back into a ponytail with quiet precision.

Agatha rolled her eyes.

“What?” Rio asked.

Jen huffed. “You’ll fit in better than us, that’s for sure.”

The Lyft dropped them off on Doyers Street at exactly 7:50 pm.

The street looked like it had no business hosting a video game anything. Narrow and strange, curved like a question mark, it was packed with unmarked doors, cracked brick, glowing neon from nearby dumpling spots, and one sad looking bodega tucked between a bakery and a shuttered tailor. A yellow awning drooped above the windows, which were cluttered with faded chip bags, energy drinks, and handwritten signs in three languages, none of them English. They went inside.

Agatha stared blankly at the place. “This is where hope comes to die.”

Jen squinted. “I’m 70% sure this place only sells cigarettes and bleach.”

Rio said nothing. Her body had gone taut, posture sharp as a blade, adjusting her glove to fidget slightly, energy humming under her skin. She walked ahead of them with purpose. Straight to the back of the bodega.

Rio paused at a solid metal freezer door in the back. She then knocked once, twice, pause. A small door opened, the dorky guy on the other end muttered, “Password?” Rio clearly said “T Spin Double.”

The door clunked as it unlocked and creaked open. The group stepped inside and the world changed.

They stepped into what looked like a basement club colliding with an arcade, with low LED lights in blue and pink, exposed brick walls, and the sound of clacking keys of game buttons, joystick controllers, and people buzzing with the intensity of competition. The floor was sticky, the crowd smelled like burning Cheetos, and 8-bit retro video themes trilled with every footstep.

Rio was alive. It was chaos…glorious…glorious chaos.

And right in the back of it, a cluster of Tetris rigs. Rows of TVs. Controllers glistening and custom wired. People playing in gloves. Someone using a keyboard to play. Metronomes ticking at 120 BPM. Tetris, was alive here.

Rio vibrated like someone had flipped a switch inside her. She inhaled sharp and grabbed for Agatha’s hand without thinking. Agatha didn’t hesitate. She took it, laced their fingers together, and let Rio lead. The way Rio kept her grip tight, Agatha knew Rio needed her as a way to stay tethered to something real in a place like this.

The moment they entered the Tetris zone, someone gasped. Loudly. A voice somewhere in the crowd rang out, shocked and starstruck.

“Holy shit. Is that Rio Vidal?” Suddenly, all eyes were on them. A wave of people swarmed forward, young men, women, and nonbinary teens in bucket hats and oversized hoodies, everyone talking at once, their phones out, faces lit up with phone screens and awe.

“Oh my god, it’s really her.”

“Can I get a selfie?”

“She’s here, she’s really here.”

“Are you playing tonight?”

Rio smiled. A little shy, a little stunned. But she nodded. “Yes.”

She posed for photos, answered questions about input delay on old monitors versus modern LCDs, even signed someone’s controller with a borrowed Sharpie. Her face was composed, calm, but Agatha could see the slight flush in her cheeks.

And then someone new stepped up. Younger than the rest. Maybe sixteen, seventeen. Skinny, sharp eyed, all attitude. He didn’t smile. Didn’t ask for a picture.

“You’re the one who uses the double stack method, right?” he spoke sternly.

Rio blinked. “Yes.”

“Hmmm. Overrated.” He mocked her.

Agatha tensed instantly, but Rio, bless her, stayed calm.

“Depends on the build..." she added, politely. “But it’s efficient in matches that require mid game adaptability.”

The kid snorted. “Guess we’ll see.”

Jen leaned in, whispering to Agatha, “This little gremlin’s gonna get smoked.”

Agatha grinned. “I might pay to see it happen.”

Kyle #2, Agatha thought it was #2, but honestly, she’d stopped keeping track after the third one joined the office, appeared beside them, looking mildly overwhelmed.

“Yo..." Kyle #2 said. “This place is wild. I got called ‘normie trash’ then given her phone number by a hot girl with holographic eyeliner and a tail.”

“Good grief man..." Jen said, scoffing as she looked him over. “Of course you’d be into that.”

Kyle #2 squinted at Jen, then noticed the kid talking to Rio. “Oh no. That’s Tate. He’s been coming here for only a month, but he’s super annoying.”

“Perfect..." Agatha muttered. “Can’t wait for Rio to annihialte his ass.”

“Mind if we play a game before we start?” Rio said to Tate. He nodded and grinned a little too big.

Rio moved toward the rigs, adjusting her glove, stepping into the open seat with a fluid grace that had people pulling out phones again. Her opponent, the kid, Tate, sat next to her with a smirk and cracked his knuckles. A crowd formed.

Jen nudged Agatha. “You’re about to watch your girlfriend psychologically dismantle a child in front of like, fifty people in a Red Bull cooler.”

Agatha exhaled and crossed her arms, pride swelling hot in her chest. “Damn right I am.”

Rio reached out her hand to have Tate shake it. He just looked the other way.

“Ready to lose?” he smirked.

Rio gave a small, polite nod. “We haven’t played yet.”

“Oh, right.” His grin spread. “Didn’t wanna assume. Y’know, women in Tetris, it’s like seeing cats do calculus. Novel. But sad.”

Agatha’s jaw clenched so hard she heard it click. Jen held Agatha back just in case she was going to lunge at the kid. Kyle #2 joined in the death glares from the sidelines.

The match started. Rio was fast, but not perfect. Her fingers faltered on the T-spin. She missed an L-piece rotation. Her stack started to look disorganized.

Tate kept going.

“Hey, does she make those little whimpering noises when she gets fucked too?”

Agatha lunged, and thankfully Jen was holding her back. “Oh no you don’t.” Jen held her tighter.

Rio didn’t reply, just sniffed and adjusted herself in her chair.

Tate smirked. “That’s right…dykes don’t belong in this game anyway.”

The air dropped out of the room. Rio froze. For the first time in her career, she fumbled a hard drop. Her board locked up. GAME OVER.

The crowd didn’t cheer. They just stared, quiet, tense, and uncomfortable.

Agatha’s hand balled into a fist. “I’m gonna punch him.”

Jen didn’t blink and held Agatha back. “No, you’re not assaulting some teenager on my watch, no matter how punchable he might be.”

Rio didn’t speak. She didn’t even look at them. She got up, offered a hand to Tate, who didn’t shake it, and walked away to a chair in the corner of the room. She got out her headphones, sat down, closed her eyes, and pressed play.

The group ran up to Rio, making sure she was ok, and like she could sense their presence, she held up her hand and said calmly, “Give me a minute.”

Rio took a second and breathed deep.

She pressed her palms together in front of her, thumbs resting lightly against her sternum, a gesture she did slowly, like she wasn’t even thinking, one of focus, of control. She let the noise blur, the crowd soften, the adrenaline settle into something sharper, cleaner. Her mind clicked into place finding a peace in a place of madness.

For Rio, this was home. Tetris was her safest space, the one arena where the world made sense in clean, precise movements and perfect fits. Some smug, backwards ass teenager with a superiority complex and the emotional intelligence of a thumbtack wasn’t going to unravel her. Not now.

She had a job to do. Revenge.

Rio got up, took her headphones off, nodded at the group, and cracked her knuckles. She dug into her pocket and pulled out a solid dark green glove, with light green fingertips on the thumb and index. She yanked off her white glove on her right hand in one fast motion and slid the new one on. It was skin tight, like it was spandex.

Agatha’s eyes went wide looking at Rio, ‘why is that green glove so fucking sexy’ she thought to herself. She shook her head in hopes to erase the thought.

Rio walked up to Agatha, eyes full of something she had never seen out of Rio before. She slithered up to her and stuffed the white glove in Agatha’s pocket. Rio kissed her on the cheek, then turned around swiftly.

“Let’s do this.” Rio breathed out slowly.

– –

– –

The tournament roster wasn’t huge, sixteen players total, 3 games to win. But the energy in the room was loud, sweaty, and competitive. A dozen tv’s buzzed in tandem. Wires snaked across the floor like trip hazards. Controllers clacked. On lookers cheered and groaned along with how the games were going. Round 1: Rio Vidal vs. Chad Muse

The game started and Rio’s fingers danced across the controller, quick, deliberate, exact. Tetriminoes dropped like they were being summoned by instinct instead of input, each one landing with satisfying finality, slotting perfectly into her board. Double. Clean. T-spin. Clear. Another Tetris. Rio barely blinked.

Across from her, Chad Muse, an eager college sophomore in a hoodie and too much Axe, was already flailing. His stack was jagged, uneven, panicked. Rio didn’t even look at his screen. She didn’t need to.

All she heard was rhythm. The soft tick of placement. The click of rotation. The internal hum of her own pattern recognition kicking in at full force. It wasn’t just playing. It was choreography. She moved faster. Smoother.

Chad cursed. He wasn’t just losing, he was getting dismantled.

Two minutes in, Chad’s board was done for, GAME OVER.

Rio hit the kill switch. Her eyes narrowed just slightly, one last perfect L-piece rotated mid fall to slide into the exact pocket it was made for.

Winner: Rio Vidal

Rio stood, extended her hand with polite neutrality, and shook Chad’s sweaty, stunned grip. He blinked at her like she might’ve hypnotized him.

“Good game,” she said quietly, already walking away.

Her hands still twitched with residual motion, kinetic memory firing like aftershocks in her fingertips. She returned to the group where Agatha stood with her arms crossed, already smirking.

Jen leaned over to Agatha and whispered, “She’s terrifying.”

Agatha smiled. “Isn’t she just?”

Jen gave Rio a high five, then one to Agatha.

“Nice..." Agatha murmured, pride thick in her voice. “You didn’t even break a sweat.”

Jen leaned in. “I think that kid shit his pants.”

Before Rio could speak, from the fringe of the gathered players, Mike waved both arms like he was trying to land a plane. “Yo! Rio!”

Billy followed close behind, already grinning. “That was ridiculous. You didn’t even blink.”

Rio clasped hands Billy, Mike and Rio exchanging some kind of secret handshake.

Rio laughed. “Should I have gone easy on him?”

“Absolutely not,” Billy said. “I just didn’t know I was watching John Wick but for blocks.”

Mike laughed. “You weren’t kidding about your skills. You’re scary good.”

Agatha raised her eyebrows. “She’s the most dangerous quiet person you’ll ever meet.”

Jen rolled her eyes. “Wait, are we collecting ‘Kyles’ now?”

Kyle #2, who had somehow ended up within earshot again, perked up. “I’m not…"

Jen waved him off. “It’s ok, honey, we’re all background characters in this storyline right now.”

Rio turned to Mike and Billy. “It’s good to see you two.”

Billy beamed. “We came to see the hype. And you very loudly crushed someone’s self esteem, so…success?”

Mike elbowed Agatha. “You must be proud.”

Agatha shrugged, hiding a smirk. “She’s just warming up.”

Rio glanced at Agatha. “Is that...a compliment?”

“It’s more like a threat.” Jen muttered.

Kyle #2, from the corner, “A big threat.”

Agatha groaned. “Ok, pack it up everyone, let her breathe.”

Rio nodded. “I still have work to do.”

Billy leaned over to Mike. “She says that like she’s solving world hunger, not playing Tetris.”

Agatha just watched Rio with that little glint in her eye, the one that said she knew exactly what was coming, and that the rest of them had no idea what they were in for.

Round 2: Rio Vidal vs. Aidan Jerdee

The crowd buzzed. More people were gathering now, louder than before. The energy had shifted. Word was spreading.

Rio stepped up to the chair and rolled her shoulders. She extended a hand out and shook hands with her opponent, a kid in his mid-20s. Jittery. Hoodie half zipped, cheap sunglasses on the brim of his nose like he thought this was a rap battle instead of Tetris.

The match began. Aidan opened aggressively. He was fast, she’d give him that. His board was tight, early pressure high. He dumped garbage pieces like her wanted to prove something, slamming pieces down with unnecessary flair.

But Rio didn’t flinch. She shifted gears. Slowed her breathing. Her fingers tapped a rhythm against the controller that didn’t match his speed, but outclassed it in precision. She was already inside the game. Inside the code.

Jen leaned over and said quietly to Agatha, “She’s not playing Tetris anymore. She’s reprogramming it for revenge.”

Aidan misdropped a piece.

Rio pounced. She was cleaning lines faster now, operating with clinical efficiency. Not hot with rage, but cold. Focused. She tucked pieces in places that were nearly impossible to tuck them. She flipped a Z-piece and slammed down a perfect T-spin triple.

Aidan swore.

Rio didn’t react. There was no hesitation, no wasted movement. She dropped a back to back Tetris. The crowd cheered loudly.

Aiden blinked and looked at Rio’s screen, wondering what just happened. The hesitation led him to be unfocused for just the right amount of time. His board locked up. GAME OVER.

Winner: Rio Vidal

The crowd let out a collective cheer in a hushed awe that comes from witnessing something deeply impressive.

Rio set her controller down and exhaled through her nose. Not triumphant. Just centered.

Aidan stood. He looked disappointed, but not bitter. “You’re amazing,” he said, offering his hand again. She shook it, firm and sure. “You’re fast,” she said. “You’ll be very dangerous soon.”

Aidan grinned, nodded, flushed from the compliment.

Rio got up and walked over to the group. Agatha reached for her hand. Rio took it, tightly.

Billy leaned toward Mike and whispered, “Was that…was that murder?”

Mike nodded, wide eyed. “It was art…but also murder.”

PART 1: SOUND REMOVED

– –

Cold.

That was the first thing Rio noticed.

Not the quiet. Not the faint sound of rain outside, or the low hum of the city stretching somewhere beyond the walls. It was the cold. The absence.

She stirred slowly, her brain rebooting in fragments, piecing together the strange hollowness of the room.

The sky outside was gray, rain smearing down the windows in soft lines. Morning, maybe. Or late afternoon. The light was diffused, sleepy, and directionless.

Rio reached across the bed instinctively, her fingers splaying across the sheets before she even opened her eyes. But the space beside her was empty…and cold. Her eyes snapped open.

She sat up quickly, heart racing. Not fear. Not panic. But the feeling of something missing, sudden and sharp, like a skipped beat in a song she knew by heart.

The apartment was still. The kind of motionless that made her feel like the air itself had been holding its breath.

She got up without thinking, bare feet brushing the floor as she stepped out into the living room. Her whole body felt tight, like it hadn't finished processing the shift in the atmosphere. It felt like waking up in the wrong level of a dream.

Scratchy sat by the front door, staring at it with intensity. He sensed Rio, looked back, and thumped his foot on the floor lightly. He hopped over to her and stood up on his hind legs, pawing up at her. Rio bent down and scooped him into her arms. She held him the way she’d seen Agatha do a dozen times, close and careful.

They made the circuit together: the bathroom, empty. The kitchen, untouched. The coffee pot hadn’t been used. No mug in the sink. Bedroom, still rumpled from the night before, but devoid of her.

No lavender perfume in the air. No sarcasm coming from within the walls. No smirk.

Rio stood still in the center of the living room, holding Scratchy like he was the only steady thing left.

Agatha was gone.

– –

– –

– –

Hot.

The tears were coming down, but so was the rain, and Agatha couldn’t tell which was which anymore.

She sat rigid on a damp bench along the East River Esplanade, hunched under the unyielding gray sky. The umbrella in her hand caught the rain above, but it still traced down her cheeks and into her collar. Her gray sweatsuit was soaked, sticking to her skin in a way that made her feel both real and removed from her body. Her white running shoes were ruined, mud streaked and squelching faintly whenever she shifted. She didn’t care.

The wind whipped up off the river in sharp little bursts, slicing sideways across her face, tugging at her wet hair, making an umbrella all but useless. Across the water, the skyline blurred in the mist. Ferry horns wailed in the distance. Water lapped against the rocks below like a pulse. But in her mind, everything was still looping, stuck, skipping like a scratched vinyl.

She didn’t even know what time it was. Didn’t check her phone. She couldn’t. The words kept replaying, over and over. Rio’s voice, a little slurred, a lot sincere. Mumbled against her neck after a night of too many drinks and dancing so close their atoms practically fused. A confession dropped like a glass on tile.

It hadn’t been dramatic. Not some cinematic crescendo with swelling violins. It had just been. Quiet, honest, and so Rio. Which somehow made it worse.

She could still sense the weight of Rio’s forehead against her chest. She could still hear the way she said it, like it wasn’t even a question. Like she knew. Like she had already said it a thousand times before.

And Agatha? She hadn’t said it back. She’d gone stiff. Frozen. Staring into Rio’s soft, drunk, utterly trusting face like she didn’t know what language was anymore.

Because who says that after four days? Four days of being with Rio in New York, flirting and fucking and falling asleep tangled together like it was normal? Who could ever mean it that fast?

Apparently, Rio did…and that was the thing. That was the part she couldn’t stop circling back to.
