Rio knew which mug was her favorite. Rio knew which side of the bed she hated. Rio knew how to thread her fingers just right between Agatha’s when she needed her close.

Four days and Rio had memorized every part of Agatha.

And maybe, just maybe, that’s what terrified her most. Not the speed. But the depth.

Words said, simple and unarmored, and now Agatha was soaked to the bone on a bench in the rain because she had no idea what to do with that.

It wasn’t that she didn’t feel something. She felt everything. It was all crowding her lungs all at once. But love? Love required a surrender Agatha didn’t know how to give.

Agatha didn’t know how long she sat there. Minutes. Maybe hours. The city moved around her like a carousel she’d jumped off.

Eventually, she blinked up through her lashes at the sky, and whispered, barely audible,

“...I think I love her too.”

– –

– –

The train rattled east, deep into the dark.

Agatha sat by the window, her head resting against the glass, watching her own reflection blur and reappear in the glass as they passed through long stretches of nothing. Trees. Suburbs. Distant orange porch lights like flickering fireflies. She wasn’t sure how long she’d been on the train. She hadn’t tracked the stops. Hadn’t checked the time. Just bought a ticket and sat down.

Her phone buzzed in her pocket, but she didn’t check it. She already knew it was Rio. Or Jen. Or maybe even one of ‘the Kyles,’ if work had gone nuclear without her. But tonight she couldn’t be the editor. Couldn’t be the ex. Couldn’t be the sharp tongued, remark having, armor wearing version of herself that everyone expected her to be. Tonight, she was someone who just had to run.

The train hummed and rocked beneath her. The car was mostly empty, just a few late commuters and one guy passed out in his seat with a backpack for a pillow. Agatha’s eyes burned. She felt like blinking, but she couldn’t. Every time she closed her eyes, even for a moment, she saw that moment again. Heard Rio’s voice. Soft. Drunk. But real. The kind of real Agatha hadn’t known what to do with. So she’d done the only thing she knew how, leave.

And now she was here. Somewhere between Manhattan and the middle of nowhere. Headed to Westview.

Her aunt had always lived on the edge of things. A quiet house tucked into a sleepy street, all wind chimes and mismatched mugs and books stacked in the hallway. Lilia Calderu, her mother’s younger sister. A woman who brewed loose leaf tea by instinct, made spells out of grocery lists, and never once asked Agatha when she planned to settle down. Maybe because Lilia never had, either.

Agatha closed her eyes, listening to the metal on metal rhythm of the train on the tracks. She remembered the way her aunt used to talk about her “best friend..." Maria, with a reverence that didn’t match the term. How she’d always made two of everything. Two mugs. Two bowls. Two garden chairs. How Agatha didn’t realize until she was sixteen that Maria wasn’t a roommate or a friend or a quirky housemate, but a partner. A forever someone.

And maybe that was why Agatha was going there now. Not because she needed advice. But because she needed proof. That what Rio had said wasn’t insane. That maybe four days could mean something. That maybe this wasn’t a disaster waiting to happen. That maybe…just maybe…people like her could be loved like that too.

The train slowed. “Westview..." the automated voice crackled overhead.

Agatha sat up straighter, heart knocking uncomfortably against her ribs. She wiped her eyes in hope it would mask the feelings that were too deeply written on her face.

She stood as the train hissed to a stop. She stepped onto the platform, the air colder than she’d expected. It smelled like wet leaves and damp concrete. The little station was empty, just a single flickering light bulb and a vending machine that buzzed faintly.

She walked down the steps into the parking lot. She half expected her aunt to be waiting, arms crossed, gaze all knowing, but there was no one. Just the distant hum of a streetlight and the rustle of trees in the dark.

Agatha tapped her screen with a sigh, opening the Lyft app with fingers that felt too cold, too clumsy. Her thumb hovered for a second, half of her still waiting for a reason to back out, then she hit “Confirm Pickup” and watched the little car icon inch closer down the map.

As she stood waiting, she looked at her messages for the first time all day after ignoring them all.

RIO: are you ok?

JEN: where are you???

LARRY: I’d appreciate a text if you’re were going to be a no show today.

A short, sharp laugh pushed up through her throat. Of course Larry’s message was the one that made her laugh. Of all the things spiraling, his concern about meeting etiquette was almost comforting in how painfully predictable it was.

She stared at the screen. She didn’t answer any of them. She locked the phone and slid it into the pocket of her soaked sweatpants, leaving them all on read. That…somehow…felt easier. Not yes. Not no. Just...quiet.

The Lyft pulled up, headlights flaring in the dark like a portal to somewhere else. She climbed into the back seat, nodding politely to the driver, as they pulled away from the station.

The town slid past slowly, inky and small, one sleepy block at a time. Lights twinkled on porches. A dog barked behind a fence. Everything looked like damp wood and old memories.

And then the houses grew further apart. Streetlights more infrequent. The car turned left onto a narrower road, nearly a lane, and she recognized it instantly. That twist at the mailbox with the sun bleached flamingo. That lopsided fence that never quite got fixed.

The car slowed to a stop in front of a low, shingled cottage, windows aglow with soft amber light. Ivy curled around the porch railings. A wind chime clinked once. There were mismatched pots on the steps, some herbs, some dead plants, one empty teacup tucked in like a secret hidden. A carved wooden plaque hung above the door, faded but legible: “The Calderu House.”

Agatha hesitated, her hand hovered above the door to knock. She didn’t know what she was going to say. She didn’t even know why she was really here. She just knew she needed to be. That something in her had cracked open, and she needed someone who had once known what it was to be split wide and still keep breathing.

The porch light flicked on before her knuckles touched the wood.

And there stood Lilia, barefoot in a knit cardigan, mug in hand, her hair wrapped in a silk scarf like always, eyes sharp and steady behind gold rimmed glasses.

“Well..." Lilia said, not unkindly. “Look what the cat dragged in.”

Agatha opened her mouth. Nothing came out.

Lilia stepped aside. “Come in, sweetheart..." she said gently. “Let’s get you warm.”

The warmth hit Agatha before the door even closed behind her.

Not just the heat from the radiators clanging softly in the walls, but something thicker, deeper. The kind of warmth that comes from lived in comfort, wooden floors and soft rugs, low light and mismatched picture frames, the faint scent of rosemary and garlic and paint.

Lilia stepped out of the doorway and into the kitchen like Agatha showing up out of the blue was the most expected thing in the world.

“Don’t say it..." Agatha said, voice scratchy.

“I wasn’t going to..." Lilia replied, setting her mug down. “But now that you mention it, you look like shit.”

Agatha let out a tired breath that was maybe a laugh. “Charming.”

“Truthful.” Lilia didn’t soften it. “Whatever…this is, emotional breakdown, existential crisis, nuclear fallout, you’re not saying a word about it until you eat something, take a shower, and stop looking like a drowned puppy. In that order.”

Before Agatha could respond, another voice came from deeper in the house.

“Is that her?”

A second later, Maria emerged from around the corner, wiping her hands on a dish towel. She was still in her apron, sleeves rolled to her elbows, grey curls loose and wild. Her eyes lit up when she saw Agatha.

“There’s my girl..." Maria said, and Agatha was already being pulled into a soft, firm hug before she could brace herself. It didn’t break her. Not quite. But something in her chest unclenched.

“You’re just in time..." Maria said, pulling back with a gentle squeeze. “I made lentil stew. There’s bread. Sit.”

“I…” Agatha started.

“Nope.” Lilia pointed to the table. “Sit. Eat. Talk later.”

Agatha blinked at the two of them. The team of it. The quiet choreography of thirty years together. And she obeyed.

Dinner was hearty and hot and smelled like childhood. The table was cluttered with mismatched plates, cloth napkins, a chipped candleholder. It was imperfect and human and more grounding than anything she’d felt in days.

They ate in mostly silence at first, Agatha spooning stew into her mouth with a distant kind of gratitude. Maria passed her a slice of warm bread, then nudged the butter closer without a word. Lilia poured a splash of red wine into all three glasses.

Eventually, Maria spoke up. “So…is New York still as awful as we remember?”

“Worse..." Agatha said, mouth full.

Lilia raised her glass. “To surviving it anyway.”

Maria clinked hers gently. “To making it through another day.”

“Here, here..." they all echoed, as they drank.

Conversation eased as dinner went on. Maria talked about her latest painting series, portraits of birds on stormy branches, “not metaphorical..." she claimed with a wink. Lilia mentioned their neighbor's son Tommy had joined a queer hiking group and now kept gifting them trail mix he made himself. Agatha listened, occasionally joining in, but mostly letting the sound of their voices smooth out the edges inside her.

By the time the bowls were scraped clean and the wine was gone, Agatha felt that it was easier to smile, just a little.

They cleaned up together, passing plates and towels and crumbs. Lilia hummed as she rinsed. Maria made some passing joke about Agatha’s lack of dishwasher skills. Agatha groaned deeply.

It wasn’t home. But it was something close.

Eventually, Maria disappeared down the hall and came back with an armful of soft things.

“Guest room’s made up..." she said, handing Agatha a stack…folded flannel pajamas, a towel that smelled like lavender, thick socks. “There’s a heating pad under the quilt. You still run cold, don’t you?”

Agatha took them, arms suddenly too full, throat suddenly too tight. “Thank you.”

Lilia kissed her cheek on the way past. “Shower. Sleep. Tomorrow we’ll talk.”

“Only if I feel like it..." Agatha mumbled.

“Of course..." Lilia said, already halfway to the living room. “But you will.”

Maria pointed down the hall. “Second door on the left.”

Agatha made her way there, the hallway softly lit, paintings lining the walls, rugs soft beneath her tired feet.

She didn’t cry in the shower. Not exactly. But she let the water run too long, and when she climbed into bed, clean, warm, wrapped in old pajamas that smelled faintly of cedar, she felt something like safe.

The house creaked once. Rain tapped gently on the windows and Agatha finally slept.

– –

– –

It had been hours.

Not that Rio was counting.

Except, she was.

She stared down at her phone. Again. The message still sat there, on read. “are you ok?” Just three words. Too little and too much.

The apartment was quiet. The kind of quiet that buzzed. Static silence. The kind that settled into her skin and refused to leave. She stood in the middle of the living room, the space that, until now, had felt weirdly like home. But without Agatha in it? It felt off. Tilted. Like a T-spin set up one block too early. Almost right. But not.

Scratchy thumped from his chair with a kind of dramatic flair that made Rio flinch. She turned toward him slowly.

"Oh…” she glanced at her watch, “...you must be hungry."

He thumped again.

"Ok..." she said, slowly. “...I can handle that.”

Rio opened the fridge. Nothing remotely rabbit friendly. A few take out boxes, a questionable tomato, three kinds of mustard. No greens. No pellets. No hay. No, whatever it was Agatha always reached for in the mornings when she fed Scratchy like it was muscle memory.

“Do you eat…lettuce?” she asked him. He blinked.

“....Cil…antro?” A pause.

“Do you have a preference for leafy or crunchy textures?” Another thump, annoyed.

“Cool…” she muttered. “...you’re just like her.”

Rio scratched her head, scanned the counters, the cabinets. Nothing. But then, like a flash of divine providence, she spotted a receipt crumpled next to the toaster. Rio flattened it out on the counter.

TAKE A PAWS – 515 W 52nd St, New York, NY 10019

Timothy hay

Purina Complete Rabbit Feed

Stick balls (x3)

The list might as well have been in Russian to Rio.

"Stick ball…is…a food?" she asked Scratchy.

He gave her a long, judgmental stare.

“Right. Noted.”

Rio got dressed. Gray wash Levi’s, broken in at the knees. Her trusty black Doc Martens, and she threw on a black t-shirt that read “My Existential Crisis is Out of This World” complete with a distressed UFO graphic. She squinted at the low light outside, dim, still raining. She might need her glasses, and grabbed them from the bathroom drawer, slipping them on with a sigh. Oversized and thick black framed. A ponytail. Clean and sharp.

She stood by the door, one hand on the knob, and turned back toward Scratchy.

“I’ll be back..." she said. “Promise.”

A firm thump.

“Ok, fine. Soon. Geez.”

“Take a Paws” looked like it hadn’t been dusted since the early 2000s.

She stepped inside, the smell of kibble and pee thick in the air. Posters of cats and dogs lined the wall, faded and curling. Rio spotted a teenage employee walking around, elbows deep in a bag of sour gummy worms.

“Hi..." Rio said, stepping up to him with her receipt. “I’m babysitting a rabbit and I have no idea what I’m doing. Can you help me find these things?”

The kid blinked at her. "You mean like…now?"

“Yes. Now.” Rio said plainly.

“Uhhh…” He stared at the receipt. “Timothy hay’s over there...” He pointed vaguely toward the back. “...I…think?”

“Thanks..." she said flatly, already walking in that direction.

She found the small pets aisle at the back and squinted at a wall of options that felt absurd. Flavored sticks. Unflavored sticks. Tunnels. Balls. Hay in four different cuts. Seventeen different types of food. She Googled furiously. Compared textures. She read reviews. She located tossed in the hay, the food, and the 3 “stick balls.” Apparently, it was an enrichment toy and also edible. Weird. But ok, Scratchy.

Then she saw it.

A tiny baby carrier, like the ones you carry on the front your chest. Pet sized. Black canvas. Ventilated.

"You're coming with me..." she whispered.

Two aisles over. Halloween costumes. One triceratops, sized ‘small fluff’. Into the cart.

She moved like she was shopping in a video game. Swipe. Toss. Collect item. Add bonus point.

Once she was done wandering, Rio headed to the register.

The woman working it was maybe in her twenties, with a nose ring and hair the color of watermelon candy. She scanned Rio’s items, pausing when she reached the triceratops costume.

“Big night in?” she asked, a teasing lilt in her voice. “...or…big night out?”

Rio frowned slightly, not understanding the inflection. “I’m emotionally supporting a rabbit..." she answered seriously, placing the bag of timothy hay on the counter like it was backup for her claim. “It’s mutual.”

The woman blinked. “...Hot.”

Rio blinked back. “Thank…you?”

There was a beat. A long one. The woman opened her mouth like she might say something else, but Rio had already tapped her card and was busy calculating how much weight she could carry comfortably per arm without cutting circulation. She left with three bags held in her hands.

Outside, the air had cooled, the clouds hanging low and heavy over the city like the sky itself was thinking too hard. The bags rustled at her sides and she paused at the corner. There was one more thing she needed to do.

Scratchy liked dandelion greens. She remembered that. And the raspberries, Agatha always said they made him look like he’d wandered off a horror movie set, juice staining his little mouth like he'd bitten someone.

Rio smiled faintly. “Of course he gets the good stuff.” And if Agatha wasn’t here to get it, then she would. That was the agreement, wasn’t it? Silent, unspoken, and suddenly very real.

She moved from the produce to the aisles of boxed food with the familiar rhythm of someone trying to be efficient, but already fighting the creeping temptation of her comfort zone. Her cart filled fast.

Hot Pockets. Fruit Roll-Ups. Purple Doritos, the best kind. Classic comforts. Safety snacks. Predictable, low prep, sensory friendly food.

Her hands hovered over a box of instant mac and cheese before Agatha’s voice filled her mind. ‘Processed crap, Rio? Your body is a temple, not a trash fire.’ The voice, perfectly preserved, cut through the aisle like a sharp edge. Rio groaned out loud. She looked down at the snacks in her cart. Her ‘temple,’ apparently, was made of neon powder and microwaveable sodium.

“Betrayal..." she muttered, and began pulling items back out with reluctant precision. Back went the Doritos.

She turned back to the shelves and started over, more carefully this time. She added eggs. Pasta. Chicken breast. Garlic, not pre-minced. Leafy greens that felt like human rabbit food. A lemon, because Agatha once said she liked it in her water, and that somehow counted as reason enough.

Rio stood in front of a display of avocados. They stared at her. She stared back. Small, knobbly. Greenish black and smug. She picked one up, turned it in her hand, and squinted.

“Are you a fruit or a vegetable?” she whispered. “Are you a lie? Are you just…millennial propaganda?” She pulled out her phone and Googled.

“Technically a berry.” Rio’s eye twitched. “That’s worse. That’s so much worse.”

She added two to the cart anyway. Out of protest, or obligation, or in case Agatha came back and opened the fridge and needed a reason to roll her eyes.

She stood back, looked at her cart, and nodded once. This? This was survival mode. Upgraded. She was doing it. For Agatha, for herself, and for a rabbit who liked horror movie raspberries.

The apartment door clicked open.

Rio shuffled in sideways, balancing bags on both arms and using her foot to kick the door shut behind her. She looked like a sitcom punchline, glasses slipping, ponytail unraveling. She dropped everything with a grunt.

From his perch in the corner, Scratchy thumped with theatrical irritation, nose twitching like he was judging her life choices.

“I know, I know..." Rio said, throwing her arms in the air like she was surrendering to a tiny, furry general. “You’re starving. You’ve been abandoned. I’m a monster.”

Scratchy thumped again, louder this time.

Rio peeled open one of the grocery bags with the urgency of a bomb tech. She yanked out the dandelion greens, tore off a few leaves, and dropped them gently in front of him like a peace offering.

Scratchy attacked them with alarming speed.

“You eat like you're on a deadline..." Rio murmured.

She watched him eat for a long moment. That rhythmic, satisfied chewing. That tail twitch she had only started to notice meant contentment. That, she understood.

“You’re not the only one struggling with transitions..." she added, more to herself than to him.

She took off her glasses and wiped the rain from them with the hem of her shirt. The apartment was still too quiet. She could feel it in her teeth.

Then she got to work. First: the fridge. Unloading groceries was her favorite part, sorting, organizing, aligning. It was one of the few activities where the task was the reward. Where the outcome was visual and satisfying.

Cartons first. Left to right. Tallest to shortest. Food grouped not by traditional category, but by function. “Breakfast.” “Fast.” “Protein.” “Agatha.” The lemon went under “Agatha,” obviously.

She took her time aligning labels so everything faced forward, making sure everything was spaced evenly. No overlap. Nothing touching. Every item had a place. Order. That, she could control.

Then came the pet store bag.

She reached in and pulled out the tiny carrier. It looked even sillier now than it had in the store, but she clipped it on over her chest with total seriousness, adjusting the straps like she was prepping for a backpacking trip and not rabbit parenting.

She turned to Scratchy, now mid groom and totally unimpressed.

“Ok…” she said, looking over at Scratchy. “...field test.”

He looked at her. She looked at him. He hesitated. She waited.

Then, slowly, he hopped over. She knelt down, scooped him up, gently, carefully, like he was made of glass and fur, and nestled him into the pouch.

He blinked, adjusted, then settled in like he’d always belonged there. One paw curled against her chest. His head poked out just enough to survey his domain. He thumped, once, against her stomach.

She stood. Scratchy was warm against her. Still. Content.

“I think that means we’re good?” Rio questioned.

He didn’t thump. His eyes were half lidded. Relaxed. She walked across the room in a slow circle, testing the fit, watching his nose twitch every time she moved.

She caught their reflection in the mirror and tilted her head.

“We look…ridiculous..." she whispered.

Scratchy twitched his nose in agreement…or dismissal. Hard to tell.

“Alright…” Rio sighed. “...you and me Scratchy, domestic edition.”

And with that, she started cleaning.

She stripped the bed and did the laundry. Remaking the bed with crisp clean sheets, smoothing each layer with deliberate, even strokes, folding the corners with hospital precision until there wasn’t a wrinkle in sight. The pillows were plumped and arranged symmetrically, two against the headboard, two smaller ones angled at just the right tilt. She adjusted them twice more, then stepped back and stared, just to be sure.

Then she moved to the windowsills, dusting them carefully, running her fingers along the grooves to catch anything she might have missed. She dusted the curtain rods too, and the picture frames, and the tiny ledge above the doorframe, because even if no one ever looked there, she would know.

Baseboards were next. She crouched and cleaned them in slow, steady lines, her breath coming in a quiet rhythm as she moved from room to room. She wiped down every light switch and doorknob, eyes narrowing at every smudge, every trace of touch or chaos. Order mattered. It helped.

The bathroom sparkled by the time she was done. She cleared the counter, lined up the toothbrushes, replaced the towels, folded the way Agatha always folded them, one third in, then in half. She scrubbed the sink basin until it gleamed, then the tub, then the tile grout, brushing between each square until it looked new.

Rio moved through the kitchen with quiet precision. She cleared the counters first, wiping them down in layers, first with disinfectant, then again with Windex to remove the chemical residue. Every mug, stray fork, and misplaced item found its proper home. She reorganized the spice rack alphabetically, then by frequency of use, labeling a few jars that had long since lost their markings. The fridge got a second pass, labels straightened, leftovers rotated, produce re aligned. She scrubbed the sink with baking soda and vinegar until it gleamed, then took apart the stovetop cleaning around each burner with delicate precision.

She vacuumed last, slow, calculated grids in her mind from wall to wall. No shortcuts. When the imaginary vacuum lines didn’t match up, she went over the space again. She even checked the floorboards for dust bunnies. Then she mopped. Then waxed. The wood floors caught the light like glass.

Even Scratchy’s cage got the deluxe treatment. A refilled bowl of pellets, a pile of hay larger than him, hopefully considered a peace offering, the stick ball hung perfectly centered in his cage.

By the time she stood back, the entire space was reset, streamlined, sparkling, and finally still. Like a model apartment in a catalog, but one that had been lived in gently, and by people who cared.

She sat down at the edge of the couch, Scratchy still snug against her chest, his breathing soft and rhythmic. She reached up and scratched behind his ears.

"I don’t know what I’m doing..." she whispered to him. “...but I think this counts as trying.”

The room was quiet. But not empty.

She picked up her phone. Still nothing from Agatha.

Just the echo of her own message, sent hours ago. “are you ok?”

Rio stared at the screen. Then glanced at Scratchy a few inches away from her own face.

"...She’ll come back."

Scratchy blinked.

"She has to..." Rio whispered.

Scratchy let out a soft snuffle. He’d been dozing in and out for the last hour, more content than Rio had ever seen him. He didn’t twitch, didn’t thump. He just rested, his body rising and falling with hers, like being carted around like a spoiled prince in a personal palanquin was his birthright.

“Officially spoiled..." Rio murmured. “...you’re not even pretending to be self sufficient anymore.”

Scratchy blinked at her, nose twitching. Absolutely unbothered.

She stood in front of Agatha’s bookcases, which loomed against every spare wall, each one proudly disheveled like an overworked librarian on the brink of giving up. The bookcases had become her final frontier. The one zone she hadn’t dared to touch all day.

Shelves sagged under the weight of Agatha’s obsession with “physical media," as she called it, like tables were a personal betrayal. Paperbacks were crammed sideways. Hardcovers perched precariously on top of one another. A French book of poetry leaned diagonally against a battered copy of “Murder on the Orient Express” like it had seen too much and wasn’t talking.

“You see this, right?” she asked Scratchy.

Scratchy wiggled an ear.

“Architecturally unsound…” Rio said.

Agatha read nonfiction like it was a religion. Biographies, essays, journalism collections, history, social theory, queer studies. More than one title had “fuck” in it. Rio scanned the titles, tugging them out of their place on the shelf one at a time. She found dog eared pages, underlined sentences, fragments of thoughts scribbled into margins.

Then, something caught her attention. Not because it was flashy, but because it wasn’t. A small, trade paperback, it was new, not beat up like the rest. “Look Me in the Eye” by John Elder Robison.

Rio froze. She knew this book as one on the experience of someone living with autism. Her fingers hovered just above it, like the book might collapse under its own weight if she touched it too fast. She flipped it open and scanned the inside.

Underlines. Questions. Circles.

A section about misreading facial expressions had been underlined twice, with a faint scribble in the margin ‘Rio does this sometimes when she’s overwhelmed, but she’s not wrong, she’s just figuring it out in her own way.’

There was a note stuck into the back, folded in half and hastily torn from a legal pad. ‘I should ask her about the way she organizes things. There’s logic, but it’s not mine. That’s not bad, it’s just different.’

Rio blinked hard. Her throat tightened.

She looked down at Scratchy. “She read this..." Rio said quietly. “For me.”

Scratchy wiggled his nose.

She turned back to the shelf put it back. She kept looking. She spotted another oddity. A single book “Great Expectations.” The only one in the row that looked too pristine. Too fake.

Rio narrowed her eyes. “Well that’s suspicious.”

She reached out and pulled it free. It was light. Wrong. She shook it once, softly, and heard a faint rattle. The kind of sound you didn’t associate with Dickens. It opened like a hinge.

Inside, joints. A neat little stash. Some clearly store bought, uniform in shape and size. Others a little more...artistic. Uneven. Slightly loose at the base. One twisted with a comical amount of filter sticking out.

“I feel like this explains…a lot.” Rio said sighing.

She looked into it further. Papers, a grinder, a purple lighter.

Scratchy thumped once against her.

Rio looked down. “Don’t narc.”

He blinked.

Rio kneeled down and unbuckled the carrier straps gently, and eased Scratchy out into his chair. He flopped dramatically, clearly devastated at the loss of his luxury transport.

Rio sat cross legged on the floor in front of the coffee table, and started re-rolling Agatha’s joints. The tactile precision of the paper, the symmetry of rolling. She twisted them with slow care, correcting their form. Then she rolled two new ones from scratch, lining them up on the table like little soldiers.

Rio picked up one of the joints, the better one she’d rolled, and held it between her fingers for a moment, thoughtful. Then looked up at Scratchy, who was watching her like a tiny, furry chaperone.

“You think she’ll be mad I messed with her stash?”

Scratchy gave a half hearted thump and turned around.

“Exactly..." Rio said. “That’s basically a ‘yes.’”

Rio took the lighter, opened one of the windows, and stepped out onto the fire escape.

The night was cool and close. The city hummed below in fractured pieces, passing cars, a distant siren, the low thump of bass from a party across the street. She didn’t sit. She leaned against the railing, let the cool metal bite gently into her skin, and lit up.

The joint crackled softly in the quiet. She took a slow drag, and then another, letting the smoke curl up and out into the darkness like a ribbon. It didn’t taste particularly good, but she liked the weight of it. The pause. The way the edges of her thoughts softened around the burn.

She stared out at the buildings across the way. The lights in their windows. The lives happening inside them.

Somewhere out there, Agatha was still awake. Or maybe not. Maybe she was asleep. Rio didn’t know, and that, more than anything, was what made her chest feel heavy.

By the time the joint burned down to her fingers, she’d forgotten time existed, lost in thought. She came back inside, where Scratchy was waiting.

He sat on his little throne of a chair, ears twitching, glaring at her like she’d just committed a war crime in his name.

Rio sighed. “You’re worse than my parents, Señor.”

Scratchy thumped. Just once. Deliberate.

Rio pulled the window shut behind her, turned off the living room light, and moved through the dim apartment. Her steps were soft. She didn’t need to turn on a lamp. She knew where everything was now.

She headed to bed. She got changed into one of Agatha’s shirts, the Fleetwood Mac one she wore last night. She climbed into bed, pulled the covers up to her chin, and rolled to her side.

She looked at her phone again, no response from Agatha, but now it did say “Read.”

Rio tapped her phone and started texting with Jen.

RIO: hey. do you know where agatha is?

JEN: she ghosting you?

RIO: yes. she read my message but didn’t answer.

There was a pause for a minute or two.

JEN: she’s alive, I assume. she’s not answering me either. which means she’s spiraling. or brooding. or both. probably boht…in sweatpants.

RIO: should I be worried?

JEN: honestly? no…but she is probably being dramatic somewhere with bad lighting and a tragic playlist.

RIO: ok.

JEN: you ok?

RIO: i cleaned and i’m hanging out with the rabbit.

Jen: good. she’ll come back. she always does.

RIO: i’ll be here.

Rio clicked her phone off and set it on the nightstand.

Rio grabbed and hugged Agatha’s pillow. She closed her eyes and took a deep breath in, the faint scent of Agatha’s shampoo clung to the pillow. It wasn’t much. But it was something. And for now, that was enough.

– –

– –

The smell of fresh coffee reached Agatha before anything else. She stirred slowly in the soft flannel pajamas Maria had given her the night before, blinking up at the ceiling of the guest room. The light through the curtains were soft and golden, filtered by the trees outside. It was quiet. Still. She didn’t feel rested, but she felt...safe.

Agatha swung her legs over the side of the bed and padded out into the hallway. The house creaked familiarly under her steps, every sound a memory. A warm clatter of pans came from the kitchen, voices low and overlapping.

When she stepped into the doorway, Maria turned from the stove with a knowing smile. “Morning, honey.”

Agatha rubbed at her eyes. “What time is it?”

“Almost ten..." Lilia called from the kitchen table, where she was reading the paper and sipping coffee like it was a ritual. “We let you sleep. You looked like someone dragged you backwards through a wind tunnel last night.”

Agatha squinted. “Thanks...”

“Sit..." Maria said. “We’re feeding you.”

Agatha did as she was told, slumping into a chair with all the grace of a housecat. Maria slid a plate of eggs and toast in front of her, followed by a mug of coffee that was strong enough to restart a car.

For a while, no one talked. Maria sat with her own breakfast, dipping toast into her eggs, humming something that didn’t seem to have a melody. Lilia read quietly, turning pages with the tip of her finger.

Agatha didn’t realize how hungry she was until the food was half gone. She looked up, wiping her mouth with the back of her hand.

“I didn’t mean to show up unannounced…” Agatha said.

Lilia shrugged. “You’ve done worse.”

Maria gave her a gentle look. “We’re glad you’re here.”

Agatha set her fork down. “I don’t really know what I’m doing.”

“Shocking..." Lilia deadpanned. “A Harkness woman lost in a storm of her own creation.”

Agatha gave her a look. “Do you want me to open up or are you just doing your usual bitchy savant thing?”

Lilia grinned. “Bit of both.”

Maria touched Agatha’s arm lightly. “We can talk when you’re ready. No pressure.”

Agatha stared into her coffee. “She said she loved me.”

There was a long pause. The only sound was the clink of Maria setting her fork down gently on her plate.

Lilia looked over the rim of her glasses. “That’s what made you run?”

Agatha didn’t answer at first. Then said “It wasn’t even the word. It was how easy it was for her to say. Like it wasn’t terrifying. Like she just...meant it.”

Maria nodded slowly. “And you?”

“I didn’t say it back. I froze. Like a fucking idiot.”

Lilia folded the paper and set it aside. “Sweetheart, sometimes your body knows when you’re not ready before your mind catches up.”

Agatha hesitated. “But I think I do. I think I feel it. I just…I don’t know how to let myself.”

“Let yourself what?” Maria asked.

Agatha’s voice was quieter than she meant. “Be that loved.”

The silence that followed wasn’t awkward. It was reverent.

Lilia leaned forward, resting her forearms on the table. “You’ve spent your whole life being sharp. Cutting through people before they get too close. And then someone comes along who doesn’t flinch when you cut. Who actually leans in, and what, that’s not allowed?”

“She’s….different..." Agatha said. “She doesn’t play games. She just...tells the truth.”

“And that’s terrifying?” Maria asked.

“Yes!” Agatha shouted, then immediately winced. “Sorry. Yes. It is. Because what if I break it? What if I screw it up? What if she’s the one good thing I let in and I destroy it?”

Maria reached across the table and took Agatha’s hand. “Then you don’t run. You stay. You let her see the messy parts. The old wounds. The jagged edges.”

Lilia nodded. “You let her love you there, not just in the cleaned up parts.”

There was a brief silence.

Agatha’s throat was tight. “I think I’m in love with her.”

Lilia raised her mug. “Congratulations. Welcome to the club.”

Agatha laughed. It cracked out of her, surprised and shaky and real.

They sat there for a moment longer, the three of them, in that small house at the edge of Westview, as the light filtered through the trees.

Agatha took a deep breath. Then she stood.

“I need to shower…and then I think...I need to go home.”

Maria smiled. “There are clean towels in the bathroom. Take your time.”

As Agatha turned to leave the room, Lilia called after her.

“Tell her. Whatever you’re thinking, whatever you didn’t say, just tell her.”

Agatha paused in the hallway. Nodded once. And kept walking.

And when the door to the bathroom closed behind her, something inside her finally…finally let go.

– –

– –

The air inside Tilt was a blend of nostalgia and neon, half arcade, half dive bar, with sticky floors, cracked leather stools, and the sharp ding! of a jackpot echoing somewhere in the back. Overhead, a Goo Goo Dolls song filtered through low speakers, competing with the chirps and electronic trills of dozens and dozens of machines.

Rio stood in front of a classic ‘Twilight Zone’ pinball cabinet, her lips pressed into a line, her entire body angled like she was calculating the physics of the universe through flippers.

“She’s not blinking again..." Billy whispered behind her, elbowing Mike.

Mike crossed his arms. “She only blinks when she’s about to tilt the machine on purpose.”

“I heard that..." Rio said flatly, eyes still locked on the game.

Billy leaned against the neighboring cabinet, arms folded dramatically. “How are you this intense with pinball? You literally melt entire Tetris boards in your sleep. This should be fun.”

Rio finally missed a shot, the ball spiraling down the drain with a death ring of sad lights.

She stepped back from the machine. “This is fun.”

Mike raised an eyebrow. “This is what fun looks like?”

Rio stared at the screen a beat longer. “...it used to.”

Rio sighed deeply.

Mike exchanged a glance with Billy, then said gently, “Still no word from Agatha?”

Rio didn’t answer right away. She adjusted her glasses and gave the score one final glance before walking away from the machine.

“She read my text..." she said, making her way toward the back of the arcade, where a red lit booth sat abandoned. “But she didn’t respond.”

Mike and Billy followed, sliding into the booth across from her like this had become their ritual.

Billy frowned. “You think she’s ok?”

Rio picked up a menu off the table and stared at it like it might give her the answers instead of a list of novelty hot dogs and overpriced milkshakes.

“She’s so smart and sharp as a razor..." Rio said. “But she spirals, and she’s quiet when she spirals.”

Mike tapped his fingers against the table. “You want us to hunt her down? Pull a Liam Neisson from Taken on her?”

Rio gave a small, dry smile. “I don’t think she’d appreciate even John Cusack right now.”

Billy leaned forward. “We could set Scratchy on her. Like a tiny, judgmental therapy missile.”

“I don’t know..." Rio said, voice quieter. “I think she needs space. I think…I…I think scared her.”

Mike looked at her carefully. “Because you said you loved her?”

Rio nodded.

Billy shrugged. “You were drunk. Maybe she knows that.”

“I wasn’t lying.” Rio admitted.

That made both of them stop. Rio’s voice was soft, but there was no hesitation in it. Just a quiet kind of certainty. Like she’d built the logic tree in her mind and followed it to its inevitable conclusion.

Mike was the first to break the silence. “Ok. So what now?”

Rio took off her glasses, folded them neatly, and rested her head on her arms against the table.

“Now I wait..." she said. “…and maybe play some pinball.”

Mike reached across the table and gave her hand a squeeze. “You want a milkshake?”

Rio blinked. “Only if it comes with rainbow sprinkles.”

“Obviously..." Mike said, standing up. “I’m gay, not tasteless.”

Billy leaned back in the booth, watching Rio with something close to big brother sympathy. “You know, for someone who usually works in formulas, you’re doing pretty good at the waiting game.”

Rio stared at the blinking arcade lights across the room.

“It’s not a game..." she said. “It’s just…hope…and a variable I can’t control.”

Rio stood up and walked back toward the machines.

Billy turned to Mike. “Is she getting more poetic?”

“She’s in love..." Mike replied, dragging out the word like it was a punchline.

Billy sighed, watching Rio line up her shot at a ‘Star Wars’ cabinet.

“God help the next pinball machine.”

Lights flashed. Bells rang. Rio grinned for the first time all night. And the machine? Stood no chance.

– –

– –

The train pulled into Penn Station with a screech and a hiss, all fluorescent light and too many announcements. Agatha stepped off, prepared to disappear into the anonymous hum of the city, until she saw her.

Jen. Leaning against a pillar in that effortlessly stylish way she had, arms crossed, sunglasses perched on her head despite the lack of sun, and that same smug ass look on her face. The one that said: ‘You thought I wouldn’t know, huh?’

Agatha stopped short. “What the hell?”

“Welcome home..." Jen said, pushing off the column and walking toward her.

Agatha blinked. “How…how did…did you track my phone?”

Jen grinned. “Please. I know you. Big difference. You’re a creature of habit, babe. You run to Lilia, then you come back the day after, once you’re ready to start telling the truth.”

Agatha frowned. “I wasn’t ready to be ambushed.”

“This isn’t an ambush..." Jen said, looping her arm through Agatha’s and steering her out of the crowd. “This is an intervention.”

“I’m not spiraling.”

“You’re wearing the sweatsuit you swore you’d burn in 2019. You’re spiraling.”

Agatha groaned. “I hate you.”

Jen smiled sweetly. “I hate you too bitch.”

They exited the station into the thick, damp evening air. The city was its usual chaos, taxis honking, people yelling into phones, and someone on the corner trying to hand out coupons for a comedy club no one had ever heard of.

Jen flagged down a cab with ease, as if it had been preordained.

“Where are we going?” Agatha asked warily, sliding into the backseat.

Jen gave the driver an address in the West Village. “Dinner. Somewhere that serves wine by the bottle and won’t judge you if you cry into the breadbasket.”

Agatha slumped against the door. “You’re insufferable.”

Jen reached over and flicked a piece of lint off Agatha’s sleeve. “And yet, here I am. Your emotional first responder.”

The restaurant was small and dimly lit, tucked into a side street with string lights overhead and soft jazz playing somewhere in the walls. They sat in a corner booth, a bottle of red between them, a basket of warm focaccia rapidly disappearing.

“So..." Jen said, topping off Agatha’s glass. “Tell me what happened.”

Agatha swirled her wine. “She said it.”

Jen raised an eyebrow. “The it?”

Agatha nodded. “I froze.”

Jen didn’t say anything at first. Just leaned back, watching her.

“She said it like it was nothing. Like it wasn’t…like it didn’t change everything.” Agatha said.

“And did it?” Jen asked quietly.

Agatha stared at her glass. “Yea...it did.”

Jen leaned forward, resting her chin in her hand. “Ok…so what now?”

“I ran..." Agatha said flatly. “…I just…left.”

“Classic you..." Jen muttered, taking a sip.

“I didn’t know what to do..." Agatha said. “It felt...too fast, too much, too soon.”

Jen nodded slowly. “Love doesn’t care about pacing. Sometimes it shows up mid sentence and demands a key to your apartment.”

Agatha snorted. “She already has one.”

Jen looked proud. “Oh wow. Character growth.”

Agatha rolled her eyes, then softened and sighed deeply. “I’m scared, Jen.”

“I know..." Jen said. “…but Agatha...if you’re scared because it’s real, that means it’s worth staying for.”

They sat in silence for a moment, letting that settle between them.

Agatha reached for a piece of bread. “She deserves better than me freaking out and going radio silent.”

“She deserves you, when you’re honest..." Jen said. “…the sharp parts, the soft parts…all of it.”

Agatha let out a slow breath. “I don’t know what to say to her.”

“Start with ‘I’m sorry.’ Then ‘I missed you.’ Then maybe, ‘Do you still want me?’”

Agatha looked down. “God, I sure hope she does.”

Agatha leaned back in the booth, watching the red swirl in the bottom of her glass like it might spell out instructions.

“She’s probably pissed..." Agatha said, more to the wine than to Jen.

“She probably is..." Jen replied, unbothered. “…you did pull a vanishing act and left her alone with a rabbit who’s emotionally closer to a grumpy Victorian librarian.”

“Scratchy hates when I leave..." Agatha muttered.

“Everyone hates when you leave, Agatha..." Jen said. “...especially when it’s just before they’re about to love you a little harder.”

That landed. Agatha blinked once. Twice.

“She’s...different..." Agatha said. “...she doesn’t do things the way other people do. It’s not about romance for her, it’s all about data and pattern recognition. She said it because she meant it. She said it because she calculated it and the right time to say it…and I…”

“You panicked..." Jen finished. “...because for once, someone told you the truth before you were ready to handle it.”

Agatha didn’t argue.

Jen refilled their glasses and leaned her elbows on the table. “You’ve spent your whole life with one foot out the door. She’s the first person I’ve seen you hold the door open for.”

Agatha smiled. “You’re disgustingly insightful tonight.”

“I’m always insightful..." Jen replied. “You just never listen until you’re emotionally compromised.”

There was a beat of silence. She stared over the rim of her glass at Jen. “I left her in that apartment. I didn’t even leave her a note.”

“You know what she probably did the second you were gone?” Jen asked.

Agatha blinked. “What?”

“Fed the rabbit. Cleaned the entire apartment. Reorganized your bookshelf. Probably found your weed stash.”

Agatha groaned. “God. She definitely did.”

“She’s not mad..." Jen said, gentler now. “...she’s waiting.”

“Yea?” Agatha sighed.

“Yea. She’s waiting for you to do the one thing you’ve never done before.” Jen admitted.

Agatha raised an eyebrow. “Which is?”

“Let someone love you without running.” Jen nodded.

Agatha looked away, out the window. The street outside buzzed with low lit nightlife, people laughing too loud, taxis honking at nothing, the ghost of a saxophone from somewhere underground.

“I don’t know if I know how.” Agatha admitted.

Jen shrugged. “Learn to...or lose her.”

Agatha sighed. “You’re such a good friend it’s actually annoying.”

Jen grinned. “Oh….I know.”

They sat in silence a few beats longer, letting the weight of everything hang in the air between them.

Like a fog needing time to clear, everything suddenly became brighter. Agatha stood.

Jen raised an eyebrow. “Where are you going?”

Agatha drained the rest of her wine in one gulp. “Home.”

“To her?” Jen scoffed.

Agatha smirked. “Where else?”

Jen watched her go with a satisfied exhale, then leaned back in her seat and flagged the waiter.

“Bring me the tiramisu..." she said. “I earned that shit.”

– –

– –

Agatha ran.

Through the moonlight streets of New York, past red lights and honking taxis and a man screaming about lizard people. Her feet slammed against the pavement, the light seemingly flying behind her like a cape in a storm. She cut across intersections with zero grace and even less caution, side stepping around food carts and pigeons like the city was daring her to earn this moment. If someone hit her? So be it.

She had to get home. Not to the apartment. Not to the building. Not even to Scratchy. Home to Rio.

Her lungs burned as she tore up the block, practically sliding on the concrete as she reached her building and looked up.

And there she was. Her Rio. Perched on the fire escape like it was her throne. A black hoodie over her head, her glasses pushed up her nose, arms rested on the railing. A lit joint dangled lazily between her fingers. Smoke curled around her face like punctuation. Her expression was unreadable. Not sad, not angry, just still.

Agatha’s heart was going to explode.

She cupped both hands around her mouth and shouted up, breathless and raw, “Rio!”

Rio looked down.

Agatha’s voice cracked. “I love you, you fucking idiot!”

Rio blinked.

Agatha wiped at her face, breathing hard as she moved to be right under Rio. Lowering her voice now that Rio could hear her, “I left because I was scared. I ran because it felt too big. And then I realized…I want the big. I want the terrifying. I want the version of my life where you’re in it.”

Rio took one slow drag, exhaled, and said calmly, “You’re yelling.”

Agatha laughed, her voice shaking. “I know. Shut up.”

Rio took another drag. “You’re not subtle.”

“Never claimed to be.” Agatha said teary eyed.

“Also..." Rio added, adjusting her glasses, “...you’re ruining the vibe.”

Agatha barked out a laugh that nearly turned into a sob. “Jesus Christ, just come down here.”

Rio didn’t move at first. Then, after one last inhale, she pinched the joint out, tucked it behind her ear, and climbed down the fire escape.

Agatha took a step forward, then another. And then Rio’s feet hit the pavement, and the distance between them evaporated.

They collided in the middle of that sidewalk outside of their apartment like a force pulling together two magnets.

Agatha wrapped her arms around Rio, resting her head on Rio's shoulder. An arm slung around her shoulders, the other bracing Rio's head lightly to pull her into the hug tighter, sighing deeply as she held on to her like a lifeline. Rio blinked then closed her eyes, allowing herself to nuzzle her face in Agatha’s shoulder, hands holding her as tenderly as she could, melting into the embrace, the world blurring away until there was nothing left but the shape of each other’s heartbeat.

Neither of them spoke. Not for a long moment.

Then Rio said, muffled into Agatha’s neck, “You love me?”

Agatha pulled back enough to look her in the eyes. “Yea...I do.”

Rio blinked. “Still?”

“Yes.”

Rio nodded once, slow and stunned. “That’s…statistically surprising.”

Agatha cupped Rio’s face in both hands. “You’re scarily good at sex, and you probably alphabetized my spice rack. I’m basically doomed.”

Rio’s mouth twitched into a crooked smile. “Say it again..." she whispered.

Agatha smiled through her tears. “I love you.”

Rio kissed a tear away, “I love you.”

They just kissed right there. Quiet, deep, and full of the kind of certainty that didn’t need words. In a city that had seen it all, 49th had never borne witness to something like this…so vulnerable, so open, so unmistakably real.

From the window above them, they could hear Scratchy thump loudly against the window.

Agatha broke the kiss just long enough to look up and mutter, “Yea, yea, we’re coming.”

Rio didn’t let go. She just whispered, “Took you long enough.”

Agatha smiled. “I know…but I’m here now.”

And for the first time in her whole messy, complicated, over caffeinated life, Agatha meant the words she said with all that she was.

– –

– –

The apartment door clicked open, and Agatha stepped inside first, bracing herself for whatever emotional wreckage she might have left behind.

What she didn’t expect was…a tiny, furious triceratops standing in the middle of the living room.

Scratchy was dressed in a fuzzy green dinosaur costume, complete with three soft horns and a frill around his little face. He thumped violently the moment he saw Agatha.

“Oh my god..." Agatha whispered. “...is…is that…a costume?”

“He’s mad at you..." Rio said simply, stepping in behind her and shutting the door. “...and yes.”

Agatha blinked. “You dressed him like a dinosaur.”

Rio shrugged, unbothered. “Triceratops. Herbivore. Figured it matched the vibes.”

Scratchy thumped again.

Agatha crouched down slowly, careful not to startle him, sinking until she was eye level with the tiny dinosaur glaring at her with all the righteous fury of a bunny wronged. Scratchy thumped hard once, his little body quivering with indignation inside his felt triceratops costume, the horns flopping slightly with the force.

“Look, I know..." Agatha said softly, both hands raised in surrender. “I left…and you had to survive alone…with her.”

Thump. Louder this time.

Agatha winced theatrically. “Ok...that was fair. Deserved that one.”

Scratchy took a slow hop forward, still glaring, the green fabric riding up around his sides. The sight of him in that ridiculous dinosaur suit, tiny tail dragging, soft horns askew, was so absurd it made Agatha’s chest ache.

“I don’t have an excuse..." she admitted, quieter now. “...but I did cry in the rain, in public, like a very emotionally repressed protagonist in a French film. That has to count for something…right?”

Scratchy flicked one ear. Unconvinced.

“Like, full on sad girl on a bench energy..." she added. “It was very cinematic. I think even the squirrels felt bad for me.”

Scratchy took another step forward, then thumped once more.

Agatha sighed. “You’re impossible.”

Still, she reached out and gently and began undoing the Velcro at his belly. He twitched once like he was going to bolt, but didn’t. She slipped the costume off, folding it neatly beside her like it was a ceremonial robe instead of one of Rio’s chaotic decisions.

“Better?” she asked, lifting him into her arms.

Scratchy didn’t thump. That was progress.

Agatha straightened up and turned around just in time to see Rio adjusting a bizarre contraption across her chest, a soft, fleece lined baby carrier, of all things, already unbuckled and ready to receive its royal occupant.

Agatha blinked. “What the hell are you wearing?”

Rio didn’t even look up as she secured the waist strap. “Baby sling. For rabbits.”

“That’s…not a thing.” Agatha said.

“It is now.” Rio admitted.

Agatha gawked as Rio calmly stepped forward and held her arms out like a very serious valet. “Your Highness..." she said solemnly, addressing the rabbit. “...your throne awaits.”

And then, somehow, Scratchy allowed it.

Rio tucked him into the carrier like she’d done it a hundred times before, his tiny feet kicking once in protest before curling up against her chest. She adjusted the fabric over his little shoulders, careful with the fit, and secured the top buckle. Scratchy shifted…then melted. Head tucked, eyes half closed, ears drooping in the exact posture of smug, satisfied royalty.

Agatha stared. Words failed. Rio looked up at her, deadpan.

“You turned him into a grumpy old man..." Agatha said eventually, voice flat.

“He was already a grumpy old man..." Rio replied, giving the buckle a final click. “Now he just rides around like a grumpy old prince.”

Agatha was frozen, brows furrowed in complete disbelief. “Wait…you actually carried him around in that?”

Rio tilted her head. “Of course. He loves it. He thumped every time I tried to set him down. I figured it was a bonding thing.”

Agatha doubled over in laughter, hands on her knees. “Oh my god. You are unhinged. This is…this is the funniest thing I’ve ever seen.”

Rio blinked, her expression perfectly serious. “This is practical.”

“That is a rabbit in a baby carrier, Rio.” Agatha chuckled.

“Exactly.” Rio nodded.

Agatha covered her face with her hands, still laughing. “I leave for one day and this is what happens to the two of you. You both lose your minds.”

“He’s very particular about his movement patterns..." Rio explained. “Plus, I figured he could supervise while I cleaned.”

Agatha looked around, blinking again. It was then she really noticed the apartment. Not just clean. Transformed.

The air felt crisper. The windows sparkled. The floors gleamed. The scent of citrus cleaner and something faintly herbal, maybe eucalyptus, hung in the air. Every surface had been wiped down. The bookshelf wasn’t sagging anymore, and the coffee table cleared, centered by one very familiar book.

Her voice dropped to a whisper. “Did you clean the entire apartment?”

Rio looked faintly sheepish. “I didn’t know what else to do. So I figured...if I couldn’t find you, at least I could make this place feel like yours again…or...ours.”

Agatha stared at Rio. Then at Scratchy. Then back to Rio. And just like that, her throat tightened, her eyes burned, and her laughter gave way to something softer, deeper, and far more terrifying. She didn’t say anything.

She just reached forward, cupped Rio’s face with both hands, and kissed her. Long. Quiet. Full of a kind of gratitude that had no words yet.

Scratchy, nestled between them, thumped once against Rio’s ribs.

“I know..." Agatha muttered against Rio’s lips. “I missed you too, you demanding little tyrant.”

Rio pulled back with the faintest, drowsy smile. “He was surprisingly good at comforting me. Like a judgmental therapist who doesn't take insurance.”

Agatha wiped her eyes, then glanced down again at the carrier. “You’re really just gonna…wear him around now?”

Rio nodded solemnly. “It’s his kingdom. I merely serve.”

Agatha sighed. “God help me, I live with two emotional support cryptids.”

Scratchy blinked slowly.

Rio leaned forward, whispering conspiratorially. “I think he forgives you.”

Agatha looked at the rabbit’s deeply unimpressed face. “He’s considering forgiving me.”

“Progress..." Rio said.

Rio walked over to Scratchy’s chair and took him out of the carrier. Rio folded the carrier and put it next to Scratchy’s cage. Scratchy dramatically huffed as he flopped over in his chair in a perfect display of bunny contempt, settling into his chair with a deep sigh.

Agatha sat on the couch. Glancing over and sitting proudly in the center of the coffee table, “Great Expectations.” She laughed. “You found my favorite book.” She took it off the table.

Rio sat on the arm of the couch beside her. Agatha opened it and found only three joints left tucked inside. Agatha raised an eyebrow. “And you smoked all my weed.”

Rio shrugged. “Scratchy was judging me. I had to cope.”

Agatha laughed, the sound full and real, as she closed it and put the “book” back on the table.

Agatha sank further into the couch and she patted the cushion beside her, and Rio sat. She rested her head on Agatha’s shoulder, letting out a long, quiet breath.

For a moment, neither spoke.

Then Rio said softly, “I thought you ran because of me.”

Agatha turned slightly toward her. “Because of you?”

Rio kept her eyes forward, voice steady but small. “Because of how I am. I thought I overwhelmed you…or you realized you didn’t want to deal with someone who you know…is a little more complicated…and autistic…and has to think out entire conversation scripts sometimes just to avoid saying the wrong thing.”

Agatha didn’t answer right away. She watched Rio, really watched her, the slight tremor in her fingers, the way her them tapped against the hem of her shirt, like a metronome she couldn’t silence. She reached out, slowly, letting her hand hover for a moment before gently wrapping her fingers around Rio’s. She felt the hesitation in Rio’s grip, tense at first, then gradually relaxing.

“You haven’t ever overwhelmed me…” Agatha said. “...not once.”

Agatha sat up slightly, leaning back against the couch, still holding Rio’s hand. She reached gently and turned Rio’s face toward hers. “No.” she said, firm. “No, I didn’t run because of that. I didn’t run from you. I ran from me. Because for the first time in a long time, maybe ever, someone saw through all my bullshit and didn’t flinch…and that scared the hell out of me.”

Rio looked over at her, eyes soft and wide. “You didn’t scare me.”

“I know...” Agatha’s voice broke just a little. “...and that made it even scarier.”

Rio went quiet, nodding slowly. “Sometimes I forget…that other people can be afraid too.”

Agatha offered a crooked smile. “I’m not invincible. I fake it really well, nut I’ve been hurt. I’ve built an entire personality around making sure I’m the one who leaves first.”

They sat in the hush between words for a moment. Just breathing.

“I thought..." Rio said slowly, “...that you would realize how much I rely on structure, and decide it made me rigid, or that you’d get annoyed with the way I hyperfixate, or get lost in my own head, or…”

“Rio..." Agatha interrupted gently. “I love that your brain is its own galaxy. I love that when something matters to you, you dive into it like there’s no end. I love that you notice patterns most people don’t even dare to see. And I love that you probably reorganized my entire fridge because the mustard wasn’t with its ‘like items.’”

Rio blinked. “It was next to the oat milk.”

“Exactly..." Agatha said, mock horror in her voice. “...and I kind of love that too.”

Rio smiled, bashful and small. “You’re different than I expected.”

Rio looked down, their hands still tangled together. “You didn’t think it was…too much?”

“I think it was perfect..." Agatha said. “...I think you’re perfect. In your own way, and I want more of it. I want you.”

Rio smiled, her whole face lighting up. “You’re kind of gross when you’re sweet.”

“I know..." Agatha groaned. “It’s horrible.”

Rio laughed again, shoulders finally relaxing.

Agatha kissed her. Gently. No rush. No panic. Just a steady, certain connection.

When they pulled back, Rio’s voice was quiet again. “I was scared you ran because I did everything right and it still wasn’t enough.”

Agatha reached up, brushing a hand through Rio’s hair, tucking a loose strand behind her ear. “You are enough, Rio. You’ve always been enough. I just didn’t know I could be.”

Rio’s lips trembled. “I don’t need you to be perfect.”

Agatha nodded. “And I don’t need you to mask. Not with me.”

Rio rested her head on Agatha’s shoulder. and she wrapped an arm around her, pulling her close. And they just sat there like that, quiet, entangled, holding space for everything between them.

After a while, Agatha whispered, “So…do you still want to live with me?”

Rio didn’t even pause. “Yes.”

Agatha kissed her hair. “Good…because there’s no way I’m wearing that ridiculous carrier…”

Rio nodded against her. “Ok.”

And for the first time in a long time, Agatha didn’t feel like she had to prepare for an exit. For once, the only thing ahead of her was forward.

– –

– –

The apartment had gone still. That deep kind of still, where their breathing felt louder than anything spoken.

Agatha got up off the couch and knelt in front of Rio. She gently reached for Rio’s hand and held it in hers, thumb tracing the lines of Rio’s palm like she was memorizing them.

“I don’t want to be scared of this anymore..." Agatha said. “...of us.”

Rio looked down at their hands. “Then don’t be.”

Rio’s answer was simple. It was everything.

Agatha stood and tugged Rio gently up with her. They walked back to the bedroom in silence, fingers still loosely laced, like something sacred. There was no urgency in the way they touched each other, only intention.

Agatha took off her clothes, down to nothing, slowly, while staring directly into Rio’s soul, not for effect but for reverence. Rio watched her, barely breathing, copying her the same, eventually standing there in nothing a glove and boxer briefs with tacos in sunglasses. She slipped off the glove, and flexed her fingers, staring down at them as they fidgeted slightly, then took off her boxers, staring into each others eyes with a new kind of intensity neither one of them could name.

When Agatha moved to hold Rio, she kissed her with a softness that wasn’t new, but felt deeper now. Earned. Anchored. The first touch of their lips was featherlight, uncertain but aching, like they were translating something neither could ever speak aloud. A kind of reverence, but deeper than that. A longing that had lived in the marrow of their bones, finally being given breath.

Agatha exhaled into the kiss like a prayer answered, her head tilting just enough to open the door. That was all Rio needed. Her lips followed the invitation like a secret she’d been waiting to tell.

The sound Rio made was small, involuntary, and wonderstruck. A sharp inhale, as she ran her hands over her, as if Agatha’s skin held the map to something Rio didn’t know she’d been trying to find her entire life.

She always kissed like she played, focused, present, precise, but there was something new beneath it. Something soft. Something afraid to be too rough with the most delicate thing she’d ever held. She didn’t kiss to win. She kissed to worship.

Agatha’s heart stuttered as one of Rio’s hands brushed her cheek, barely there. As if Rio was still unsure she was allowed to touch, but the other hand, steady at her waist, said otherwise.

Agatha answered her in full, threading fingers through Rio’s hair and pulling her closer, until the warmth of her body folded into Agatha’s, until every breath felt shared.

Rio melted into her. A full bodied surrender. As if she’d been waiting for this all her life and hadn’t known it until now.

Agatha shivered at the press of Rio’s fingers pressing back at her hip. There was no demand in the touch, just intent. A question. A promise.

She smiled against Rio’s lips and pulled them back onto the bed. Rio looked into Agatha’s eyes as she laid beneath her. Rio smiled softly and nodded. With a smile like that, Agatha was undone.

Rio nestled her head into Agatha’s shoulder, nipping at it lightly, then more intensely, until marks bloomed with the sight of Agatha being undoubtedly Rio’s.

Rio’s hand finally touched through Agatha's folds, it wasn’t just arousal that surged from both of them, it was trust. Trust so profound it felt like falling into open arms.

Agatha felt it before she could name it. That dizzying sense of being wanted. Not consumed. Not conquered. But understood. With every touch, every shift of Rio’s hand, she felt something new being created between them.

Agatha fingers tugged lightly at Rio’s hair and the response was instant. A soft gasp. A stuttering breath. Rio trembled like the moment had caught fire under her skin.

Agatha bit her lip, overwhelmed by the sight of her, Rio, this brilliant, razor edged woman, coming apart not from pressure or heat, but from connection.

Agatha whispered, lips curved into a wicked grin, “Come on, champ. Touch me the way you play Tetris, like you’ve already planned to five moves ahead.”

Rio huffed a breath, her smile sharp. “Hope you’re ready for a combo chain.” and in return, Rio gave her everything.

Rio moved with a grace Agatha hadn’t expected, slow and sure and impossibly gentle. Every motion was laced with care. Not possession. Not need. But Love.

Agatha’s breath hitched. The touch was exquisite. Not because of pressure, but because of presence. Rio was there. Heart and mind, eyes and breath.

When their eyes met, Agatha saw it, the stunned, dazed wonder in Rio’s face. The sheer depth of what she was feeling. And she knew, without words, that this was the first time Rio had ever touched anyone like this. Not with hands. With soul.

“Tell me how it feels..." Agatha whispered.

Rio whimpered. Honest. Unrestrained. Hers. “Like I’m winning the only game that ever mattered..." she breathed back, voice cracking.

Agatha kissed her, lost in it, lost in her, in the fullness of what they were becoming with every shared breath. Nothing about this was rushed. Every kiss, every touch was deliberate, like they were crafting a memory that would outlast them both.

Agatha’s head tipped back, lips parting around a gasp that felt too big for her chest. Her breath caught on the way out, shattered by the rising wave cresting inside her, pleasure blooming so quickly it almost hurt. Every gasp, every tremor, every part of herself she’d never let anyone touch.

Her hands reached blindly, grasping for Rio’s back, her shoulders, anything solid. Anything real. And Rio was there. Their eyes met, Rio’s gaze deep and steady. Unwavering. Agatha saw the love in her eyes for her, raw, radiant, and terrifying in its purity. The kind that stripped everything else away.

And Agatha broke.

She stopped thinking. Stopped holding back. And she let it happen, let it take her. It wasn’t just release. It was surrender. The kind that pulled a cry from the deepest part of her chest, her body bowing into Rio’s touch like it was the only anchor in the universe. She held on, breathless, undone, as her climax surged through her like a storm and left her trembling in its wake.

But Rio didn’t let go. She stayed with her, touching gently now, coaxing every last ripple, every shudder. Her hand never left Agatha’s skin. Just softened. Slowed. Shifted into something warm and grounding.

And Agatha, boneless and blinking up at the ceiling, could barely breathe for how full her chest felt. Like her heart had outgrown her ribs.

The room had gone quiet, but not empty. The space between them pulsed and Agatha didn’t dare move. Didn’t want to. Not when her whole body was still echoing with the imprint of Rio’s hands and the unbearable beauty of being seen like this…known and cherished.

The glow of the streetlamp outside slipped through the blinds. There were no jokes afterwards this time, no teasing. Just quiet breath. Trembling, not from fear, but from the unfamiliar sensation of being wanted without hesitation. Of being known and still chosen.

They lay tangled in the aftermath, Agatha laying on her stomach, Rio’s hand resting gently over her back, tracing lazy shapes into her skin.

“Still scared?” Rio murmured.

Agatha sighed. “A little.” She pulled Rio closer, “But I think I’m more in love than I’m afraid.”

Rio blinked up at the ceiling, her heart thudding so loud she was sure Agatha could feel it.

“Me too.” Rio said softly rolling onto her side to face Agatha.

Rio reached for Agatha’s hand, gentle and deliberate, and laced their fingers together. Then, without a word, she guided it to rest over her chest, just above her heart. She held it there, hand over hand, as if anchoring something fragile.

Agatha leaned in closer, her lips brushing Rio’s temple. “Your heart’s still going fast..." she whispered, voice soft as a secret.

Rio looked up at her, eyes glassy in the low light. “Yours?” Agatha’s breath caught. Just for a second. The question was simple, but the weight of it landed squarely in her ribs. She could feel the edges of her own vulnerability pressing in, sharp, and unrelenting. How had this woman, this brilliant, baffling, soft eyed woman, could crack her open with a single word.

“Mine..." Agatha murmured. She tightened her hold, thumb sweeping slow circles over Rio’s knuckles.

Then, with a crooked smile, Agatha mirrored Rio, taking her hand, threading their fingers again, and pressed it flat to her own chest. Her heartbeat was just as fast. Just as steady. Just as scared.

“Yours?” Agatha asked, smirking faintly.

Rio nodded, her fingertips tapping softly to the rhythm beneath her hand. “Mine.”

They both laughed, wet and quiet, the kind of laugh that leaks out when you’ve been holding too much in for too long. It blurred into tears neither of them minded wiping away.

Agatha sniffed. “You’re such a fucking dork.”

Rio smiled, eyes still shiny. “But you love me anyway.”

Agatha nodded, her forehead resting against Rio’s. “Yea…” there was a long pause, “I do.”

A hush settled between them, thick with everything they didn’t need to say, every promise folded into touch, into breath, into the steady rhythm of their hearts, as they drifted off to sleep, wrapped in each other and something that felt a lot like home in each other’s arms.

Chapter 14: where the pieces land

Chapter Notes

agathario in japan. agatha harkness in akihabara is about as funny as it gets y'all.

-- --

this chapter is the end of the main story arc i had planned.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

– –

– –

– –

Agatha almost made it out of the office…almost.

“This is huge, Harkness..." Larry gushed, nearly tripping over his own feet as he rushed out of his office to meet Agatha. “A behind the scenes look at the Tetris world champion in Japan? You do realize how much traffic this piece is gonna get, right?”

Agatha arched an eyebrow. “Relax, chief. You’re acting like I just scored an exclusive with Miyamoto himself.”

“I wish..." Larry muttered, already tapping aggressively on his phone, probably firing off an email about expanding the feature. “Nintendo’s personally inviting you. You’re gonna be in the heart of Akihabara, right next to all the top tier players, getting a front row seat to everything. This could be your biggest article yet.”

Agatha sighed, feigning reluctance. “If I must suffer through an all expenses paid trip to Japan…well…I’ll endure.”

Her boss stopped typing just long enough to shoot a firm glance at her. “Don’t fuck this up.”

Agatha smirked. “When have I ever?”

He opened his mouth, then closed it.

Agatha entered the elevator, rolled her shoulders, and huffed. “Ok, don’t answer that.”

– –

– –

– –

14 hours and 25 minutes.

Agatha found herself exactly where she never thought she’d be, boarding a flight to Japan, compliments of Nintendo. She was tucked into a buttery leather seat, sipping whiskey that cost more than her rent in college, wrapped in a plush blanket, while Rio sat beside her like this was just another Tuesday.

Agatha tilted her head slightly, watching Rio. She was next to her, earbuds in, reading a thick well worn book on game theory cracked open on the tray table in front of her. She was so still, so focused, that it was almost absurd. They were at thirty thousand feet, on the way to one of the most overstimulating places on the planet, and Rio was acting like they were on a public bus headed downtown.

Agatha smirked, swirling the whiskey in her glass. “You excited?”

Rio didn’t even blink. “Yes.” She flipped a page of her book.

Agatha waited, expecting more. When nothing else came, she nudged Rio.

“That’s it?” Agatha prompted, eyebrows raised.

Rio turned another page with the same calm, mechanical efficiency she applied to everything else in her life. “Excitement is a subjective measurement…” she said with a sigh “...and I’ve been to Japan 14 times before.”

Agatha let her head fall back against the seat with a theatrical groan. “Jesus Christ. You’re about to have a paid week long gaming vacation, full VIP treatment, and this is your reaction?”

Rio finally glanced sideways at her, lifting one eyebrow in what could only be described as polite confusion. “Would you prefer I jumped up and down?”

Agatha grinned. “Honestly? I would pay good money to see you do that.”

Rio considered this seriously, as if weighing the logistics of it. After a pause, she said, “Noted.”

Agatha laughed, the kind of loose, easy laugh that only came when she forgot to be defensive. She reached over and plucked the book from in front of Rio, ignoring the startled look that flashed across her face.

“You’re banned from intellectual pursuits for the next fourteen hours..." Agatha declared, tucking the book into her seat pocket triumphantly. “This is supposed to be fun. Lighten up.”

Rio blinked at her, processing. Then, slowly, she tilted her head, studying Agatha with a small, fond smile. “You’re nervous.”

Agatha made a sputtering sound. “I am not.”

“You’re drinking whiskey at noon on an international flight.”

“It’s not noon, it’s…” she checked her watch and swore under her breath. “Ok…technicality.”

Rio reached over with absolutely zero shame and plucked the whiskey glass right out of Agatha’s hand.

“Excuse me…” Agatha started.

But it was too late. Rio tossed back the rest like it was a cheap shot at a dive bar.

“I thrive on technicalities, babe..." Rio said with a wink.

Agatha blinked at her. “That was a $28 whiskey.”

“Yea…and it tasted like fire and hubris…” Rio declared, smacking her lips. “Five out of ten. Would steal again.”

And then, without even a moment’s hesitation, Rio turned and unceremoniously slung both legs over Agatha’s lap like they were on a couch at home instead of thirty thousand feet in the air. She nestled in, folding herself against Agatha’s side.

Agatha froze. Like, full mental blue screen. She blinked once. Twice.

Rio yawned and stretched her arms over her head, the motion somehow elegant and absurd at the same time. “If we’re doing fun, you need to stop catastrophizing…” she said, as if she hadn’t just turned Agatha into a human armchair in public airspace.

Agatha stared at the completely unwarranted tangle of limbs across her lap. “You can’t just…what even is…you can’t do this on a plane, Rio.”

“I can…” Rio murmured, already tucking her head into the curve of Agatha’s shoulder. “I am. Adapt or perish.”

“I’m perishing…people are looking.” Agatha hissed under her breath.

“Dramatic…as usual.” Rio mumbled, smug and already half asleep.

Agatha sighed, then settled a hand on Rio’s knee. Her thumb started tracing slow, thoughtless circles against denim. She could feel her pulse hammering in places that had absolutely no business being affected by a casual lap invasion.

“I don’t understand how you just…can do that…” Agatha whispered into Rio’s hair.

“I’m a limited edition…” Rio replied, barely audible. “...serious collectors only.”

Agatha made a quiet, strangled noise, half laugh, half sigh, and let her head fall back against the seat. This woman was going to kill her in midair. Slowly. With banter and sleep cuddles.

“I’m screwed, aren’t I…” Agatha muttered.

“Extensively…” Rio replied with sleep heavy in her voice.

The hum of the plane filled the quiet that followed, and Agatha let her eyes fall shut, heart still beating entirely out of order. The warmth of Rio’s body, the ridiculous comfort of her weight against her, the dumb little satisfied sound Rio made as she nuzzled in closer, it all wrapped around her like a weighted blanket dipped in charm and nonsense.

Maybe this trip would be a disaster…or maybe it would be another chapter of something impossible and perfect. Either way, Agatha wasn’t going anywhere.

And that…was new.
