– –

The moment they landed at Haneda Airport, Rio was already in mission mode. They had barely stepped off the plane when their welcoming committee arrived, three sharply dressed people in matching navy jackets embroidered with the red Nintendo logo on the lapel.

They were met at the terminal by the three figures: a soft spoken female translator with wire rimmed glasses and a precise bow. Next a calm, middle aged man as the driver who looked unfazed by chaos in general. Finally a silent male bodyguard in a fitted suit whose sharp eyes and discreet earpiece screamed “don’t even try it.”

Rio greeted them with a single, precise bow. It was like flipping a switch, one minute, sleepy, the next, full on operational focus. Agatha who was bleary eyed and slightly disoriented from the flight, trailed after Rio.

The drive into Tokyo was surreal. Agatha watched through the window of the white van, neon lights blurring past, katakana signage, endless blinking pachinko parlors, a 30-foot LED screen of a cartoon cat winking above a ramen shop. Tokyo thrummed with a kind of manic precision that somehow felt even louder than New York, even though the streets were physically quieter.

Rio, meanwhile, sat perfectly straight, hands folded in her lap, eyes tracking the skyline like she was mapping it in real time. Like every building was another Tetris piece she could slot into place.

Agatha snuck a glance at her and felt her stomach do flips. ‘She’s not even trying…’ she thought. ‘...and she’s already the most fascinating thing in this whole goddamn city.’

They arrived at Nintendo Headquarters just before noon. The building was crisp and imposing, all minimalist white architecture and glossy security gates. A giant silver “Nintendo” logo gleamed at the entrance like a benediction.

The bodyguard ushered them through the lobby with a few clipped words to the security staff. Their translator stayed a step behind, following.

The day unfolded like a symphony Agatha didn’t know the notes to. First up, a full glamor treatment for Rio. Getting dressed in her grey Levi’s with the holes at the knees, her Doc Martens, and a green and white button up shirt, making her look like a mechanic, with a green “Rio” patch over the right pocket.

Agatha classy as ever, opted for a purple silk blouse and black wide bottomed trousers, and classic black heels that gave a sharp clack against the marble floors.

The translator led the way, with the bodyguard at the rear. Rio and Agatha followed closely behind as they were led into a conference room with high, tinted glass walls, long white tables, spotless and gleaming.

Inside, the media handlers were hungry for Rio.

They wanted soundbites about her strategies, her inspirations, her "journey" to the top. They wanted that clean, curated, polished narrative they could put on a press release, and they wanted it now.

And Rio, beautiful, brilliant, slightly terrifying Rio, gave them exactly what they wanted. No more, no less. Rio handled it like she'd been built for something like this, when Agatha knew she wasn’t.

Her face was a perfect mask of calm intensity. Not bored. Not stiff. Just...Rio. Focused. Composed. Hands twitching only slightly at her sides, like her brain was running background calculations even as she answered yet another question about "the psychological impact of high speed puzzle solving on modern youth."

Agatha watched from the edges seated off to the side, scribbling notes with her pencil in a battered tiny notebook that had taken up residency in her bag.

Rio answered everything directly, politely, often with an almost surgical brevity that made Agatha want to snicker into her hand.

“How do you maintain focus under pressure?” one eager journalist asked.

Rio tilted her head slightly. “I don’t consider it pressure. Only input.”

“How did you develop your personal style of play?” another asked.

“Optimization. Pattern recognition. Practice.” Rio said robotically.

“What do you eat before a match?” a third piped up.

Rio blinked once. “A banana.”

Agatha almost bit through her tongue trying not to laugh.

Through it all though, Rio kept an almost constant gaze on Agatha. Knowing somewhere nearby was a safe space in all the chaos. Whenever Rio felt like she needed a grounding breath, she’d do it, then stare at Agatha shooting her a wink and smile, before continuing on.

After the press, they were led to another room for an official photoshoot. The photographer had Rio do a few staged shots against a blank white backdrop. Rio standing. Rio smiling or pretending to. Rio posing with her custom controller. Rio seated with a fake Tetris game on a monitor behind her. Rio pretending like she was mid match.

The one that had Rio standing with her arms folded across her chest, chin tilted up, weight on one hip, that single green glove peeking out like a challenge, made Agatha mutter under her breath, “God, she’s so fucking hot…” It wasn’t fair. Rio looked like a poster for danger you wanted to kiss.

After the third interview block, it was finally over. Rio had shaken every hand, signed every promotional poster, and politely bowed about two hundred times. They were escorted to a waiting lounge with low leather sofas and more complimentary drinks than anyone could possibly need. Rio collapsed into one of the seats with a graceless sigh, finally letting herself slouch.

The translator approached with another polite bow. "Miss Vidal you are free for the remainder of the day. If you need anything, we are available."

"Thank you…" Rio said simply.

Agatha nodded, throwing the translator a quick smile. "You’ve all been saints."

The translator looked faintly puzzled by the phrase, but nodded and retreated with a small bow.

Agatha turned to Rio once they were alone. "I don’t know about you, champ, but you deserve a medal."

Rio shook her head. "Not interested in more attention right now."

Agatha groaned and sprawled across the couch dramatically, draping her arm over her eyes like a Victorian heroine about to faint. “You’re so right it physically pains me sometimes.”

Rio smirked. “Come on..." Rio said, standing. “Let’s go. You’ll feel better after some sleep.”

Agatha peeked at her from under her arm. “Did you just say ‘sleep’?”

Rio nodded once.

Agatha stood up. “Lead the way, Smashboy.”

Their entourage stepped into the van, and sped off in to the late Toyko night.

Agatha yawned so deeply her jaw popped. “Thank God we are not staying at a fucking internet cafe.”

Rio hesitated. Just a second. Just long enough.

Agatha caught it. She whipped her head to face Rio, staring her down. “Rio.”

Rio blinked once. Considered her phrasing. “...What if it was a very nice internet cafe?”

Agatha’s entire body stiffened like she’d seen a ghost. “Rio.”

Rio paused. “...A…capsule hotel?”

“Rio.” Agatha said with deep annoyance.

“What about a very nice capsule hotel?” Rio said with a wicked grin.

“OH. MY. FUCKING. GOD.” Agatha shouted throwing her hands up.

Rio, utterly unbothered, just tucked her hands behind her head and offered a small, slow smile. The kind that meant she knew exactly how much she was pushing it.

Before Agatha could launch into a full New York grade tirade, Rio calmly added, “They booked us a private onsen.”

Agatha blinked. Paused. Her soul returned to her body.She sagged with relief. “Thank Christ, Vidal. For a second, I thought you were about to make me spend a week in a glorified vending machine with a bunch of unwashed nerds living off Red Bull and crushed hopes and dreams.”

Rio tilted her head. “What? You don’t want the Mike’s or Kyle’s breathing inches away from you?”

Agatha barked out a short, sharp laugh, pressing a hand to her heart. “Please no. Men? That close to me? That would be fucking disgusting.”

The translator, who had up to this point maintained a strict, stoic silence, quirked an eyebrow. She looked at Rio, then at Agatha, then back again, her face neutral, but her eyes very faintly amused. “You speak fast..." she said in clear English to Agatha.

Agatha flashed a grin, completely unbothered. “Occupational hazard.”

“She is American..." Rio said with a faint shrug, like that explained everything.

The translator considered this with all the solemnity of a courtroom judge. Then, after a long beat, she nodded. “Explains the...volume.”

Agatha placed a hand over her heart in mock offense. “Ma’am, I’ll have you know this volume is genetically engineered. My ancestors screamed at British people for fun.”

The woman’s mouth twitched, the closest thing to a smile either of them had seen from her so far.

Rio chimed in, deadpan, “Efficiency of noise projection: 97%.”

Agatha elbowed Rio lightly. “Don’t encourage her.”

“I wasn’t encouraging…” Rio said, looking almost mischievous. “...I was just…explaining the data accuracy.” The translator gave a tiny giggle before regaining full control of her expression.

Their driver chimed in with broken English “Private onsen five minutes away. Shall I keep drive...or will Miss Harkness run there on foot?”

Agatha narrowed her eyes playfully. “I’m thinking about it.”

Rio shook her head. “Statistical risk of you getting hit by traffic: 46%.”

Agatha threw up her hands and scoffed. “I jaywalk……one time…”

“Seven times…” Rio corrected without missing a beat.

“...and suddenly I’m the menace to society???” Agatha said with a huff.

The translator nodded. “It’s good to have hobbies.”

Agatha turned to Rio elbowing her. “I like her.”

“She doesn’t like you back…" Rio observed as she raised her eyebrows.

Agatha flashed a grin. “Story of my life, baby.”

“So…" the translator said, casual but precise, "Miss Vidal…is Miss Harkness your….assistant?”

Agatha choked on air.

Rio blinked. “Assistant?”

The translator nodded, deadly serious. “She takes your notes. Carries your things. Follows you everywhere.”

Agatha wiped her mouth, still coughing. “No. No, no….fuck, no.”

“She does follow me…” Rio offered thoughtfully, as if genuinely considering it “...and sometimes she does carry my things.”

Agatha glared at her. “One time. I carried your Tetris rig one time, because you needed that blasted llama plushie.”

“Alpaca…alpacachino is an alpaca Agatha. How many times do I have to tell you?” Rio said blankly.

The translator nodded. “So…Assistant?”

Agatha leaned forward between the seats, her voice full of dry sarcasm. “Girlfriend, actually.”

She blinked once. “...This is a joke?”

Rio shook her head. “Not a joke.”

“You chose…this?” the translator asked Rio, so deadpan it nearly sent Agatha into orbit.

Agatha sighed. “Believe me, I’m just as confused as you are.”

Rio, utterly unbothered, added, “Statistically, it was improbable. But not impossible.”

The translator stared ahead “I see.” A pause. “Congratulations on your…impressive tolerance, Miss Vidal.”

Agatha burst out laughing, smacking Rio lightly on the knee. “You hear that, champ? You’re tolerant.”

Rio tilted her head in faint amusement. “...and it’s not dairy based tolerance for once.”

The translator shook her head once, like she had seen too much and it was simply beyond her pay grade now.

The driver stopped the van. “We’re here.”

“I will help check you in.” The translator said exiting the van.

At the onsen, Agatha’s first impression was silence. Not just silence, real, pure, unbothered silence. The air was soft and thick with the scent of cedar and steam. The buildings were built from polished wood and stone, natural and clean lined, blending perfectly into the sloping landscape. Lanterns glowed along the footpaths like sleepy fireflies.

Agatha stopped walking and just breathed deep.

Everything about New York was loud, demanding, and constant. But this? This was peace in a way she hadn’t realized she was starving for.

Steam curled up from the open air baths, the mist carrying the faint smell of minerals and something earthier and older. The sound of water trickling somewhere nearby was the only thing that broke the stillness.

Beside her, Rio just nodded once, approvingly. “This is optimal.”

Agatha huffed a laugh, shaking her head. “Just say it’s nice, dude.”

Rio hesitated and then gave a small, awkward smile. “It’s nice…dude.”

Agatha snorted and looped her arm through Rio’s, tugging her gently down the path toward the small, private space they'd been given.

The room was traditional, tatami mats, sliding shoji doors, a low futon bed made up in crisp, fluffy white linens. There was a little balcony outside that overlooked the steaming hot springs, the mist drifting like ghost stories in the moonlight.

Agatha dropped her things at the door, kicked off her shoes, and collapsed face first onto the futon with a heavy splat.

Rio stood for a moment, surveying everything, making small mental notes, the location of the outlets, the arrangement of the room, the fact that the doors latched differently than western ones, and Agatha watched her enjoy every part of this place, not a thing out of place.

"Come on, lover boy..." Agatha murmured, her voice muffled against the futon. "...let's get some sleep. I'm beat."

Rio took off her Docs and organized her and Agatha’s shoes by the door with an automated alignment next to each others, before walking over and dropping onto the futon beside Agatha.

Agatha rolled onto her side, eyes half lidded, reaching out lazily to tug Rio closer. Rio went without resistance, settling beside her, body warm and solid against Agatha.

For a long moment, they just laid there. Listening to the distant, soothing gurgle of the hot spring. Breathing in sync.

Agatha exhaled, feeling a knot she hadn't even realized she'd been carrying start to unwind somewhere deep in her chest.

This trip, this insane, impossible trip with this insane, impossible woman, it was already more than she could have asked for…and they were just getting started.

– –

Two days later, after some much needed rest they returned to Nintendo headquarters. Today wasn’t meetings or press. Today was...something else, that neither of them knew about. The van pulled up to the polished front steps of Nintendo headquarters, and the translator gestured them inside a large conference room with a small, knowing smile.

Rio sat stiffly at the table, Agatha took a seat on Rio’s right, the translator to her left.

Rio looked down at her shirt, thinking she should’ve probably worn something that wasn’t her “Ask me about my T-spin strategy” t-shirt.

Agatha, dressed in simple purple slacks and a cream colored blouse, could sense Rio was tense, she leaned over and muttered to Rio, “Don’t worry, you look terrifying…in a good way.”

Rio blinked at her. “I’m nervous.”

Agatha smirked. “You don’t look nervous.”

Rio didn’t respond. She just fidgeted slightly and focused on the empty chairs like they were a boss level she hadn’t quite leveled up enough for.

After waiting for only a few moments, three people in sharp black suits entered. They moved with the kind of choreographed precision that only came from either extreme professionalism or the quiet terror of working corporate jobs in Japan.

Agatha instinctively straightened in her chair, eyeing them with mild suspicion. Rio, in contrast, looked like she was calculating which one would fall first in a high speed Tetris match.

There was a polite bow from each of the three, formal but not unfriendly.

Then the air shifted. The door opened again, and in stepped a man who didn’t need to be announced. Shuntaro Furukawa, President of Nintendo.

There was a murmur of recognition even from the handlers. The man had a calm gravity to him, the kind of understated authority that didn’t need raising a voice to fill a room. Crisp black suit, black tie, expression composed yet somehow kind.

Agatha instinctively nudged Rio under the table. Rio, of course, didn’t flinch. She merely stood up, respectful, offering a bow that was short but precise.

Furukawa smiled warmly, bowing back with an almost fatherly grace.

“You must be Rio Vidal..." he said, his English lightly accented but clear, his voice calm.

Rio straightened. “Yes, sir.”

"And you..." Furukawa turned to Agatha with a small, knowing smile, "...must be the journalist Miss Harkness. I've heard…stories."

Agatha smirked, lifting an eyebrow. "All lies, I’m sure."

There was the faintest glimmer of amusement in Furukawa’s eyes. Then he motioned for everyone to sit.

"We are honored to have you both here…" he said. "It is not often we have a world champion in our house."

Agatha leaned back in her chair, watching Rio out of the corner of her eye. For once, Rio actually looked...a little overwhelmed. Not badly. Just the faintest flicker of ‘oh god it’s real’ behind her steady exterior.

Agatha smiled to herself. For Rio Vidal, world champion Tetris player, the real boss fight had just begun.

“Thank you for having us…” Rio said, evenly.

The conversation that followed was not what Agatha expected. Furukawa didn’t talk about Tetris at first. Or press junkets. Or brand endorsements. He asked Rio about how she approached the game. What patterns she saw. How she mentally arranged not just the falling pieces, but the spaces they hadn’t yet touched.

“How do you anticipate an empty space?” Furukawa asked, curious.

Rio thought about it for a long moment, fingers tapping lightly against the table. “I don’t anticipate…” she said finally. “...I calculate."

Furukawa nodded slowly, a small smile tugging at the corners of his mouth. "You see possibilities where others see problems."

Rio flushed faintly.

Agatha felt a strange pride unfurl in her chest, warm and rooted. Watching Rio come alive like this, watching her be seen right was almost enough to make her want to cry. Almost. Instead, she smirked to herself, kept listening, and taking notes.

The conversation drifted gently, from philosophy of games to the future of competitive e-sports to how Rio trained her reflexes, “Muscle memory. Not caffeine…” Rio had said, very seriously, earning the faintest chuckle from Furukawa.

“You play with precision. But you also play with joy. Now that is rare."

Rio blinked. “It’s…pure joy, but most of all, fun…” she admitted, as if it was a secret.

He smiled fully now. “That’s the heart of Nintendo. Fun, above all.”

They stood to shake hands after the conversation was over. Furukawa’s grip was firm yet respectful.

“Miss Vidal. It has been an honor and…if you ever wish to design puzzles instead of solving them…" he said, “...let me know.”

Rio’s eyes widened slightly. Agatha, nearly, fell out of her chair. ‘Holy shit’ Agatha said to herself. ‘I think she just got a job offer to work at Nintendo?’

Rio tried valiantly to play it cool but failed miserably. Her foot tapped uncontrollably against the floor, her mouth dry. Furukawa smiled warmly, clearly amused by Rio’s silent reaction.

“But we can discuss the details of that later…" he said, voice steady, "...you should know, everyone at Nintendo has been watching you for a long time, Miss Vidal. Your understanding of pattern recognition and spatial optimization…is…uncommon."

Agatha fixed her mouth into a tight, stunned smile and nodded like, yes, ‘of course, no big deal, just my girlfriend being a world dominating Tetris god, totally normal, carry on.’

“And…” Furukawa continued, moving to the doorway “...I have one more thing for you. Miss Vidal, Miss Harkness. If you'll follow me?”

– –

– –

Furukawa motioned them forward and led the way down a narrow private corridor, polished wood floors muted beneath their steps. The lighting shifted as they moved, soft and low, and Agatha's stomach twisted with anticipation.

They reached a simple door at the end of the hallway. No fancy signage. No press. Just a quiet, heavy moment suspended between all of them.

Furukawa opened it.

Inside was a small room, sparse except for a few chairs, a low table, and a Tetirs rig set up in the corner, the beloved interface glowing on the screen.

Seated casually in front of it, adjusting a controller in his hands, was a man, older, graying hair, warm, and intelligent eyes.

Agatha squinted, confused, “Who is that?” she said in a low hushed voice.

Rio stopped dead. Like she'd hit a wall. Her mouth opened, then closed. Her hands fluttered once, twitchy against her sides. Because she knew that face.

“Alexey Pajitnov.” Rio muttered softly to Agatha. “The man who invented Tetris.”

Furukawa smiled gently. "We flew him in last night. Now if you excuse me, I have to other matters to attend." He gave a small bow, and made his exit, entourage in tow.

Rio stood frozen in the entrance, muscles locked. She couldn’t seem to move forward. Couldn’t breathe.

Pajitnov stood slowly, smiling wider, moving slowly, not imposing, not formal, and offered his hand. Rio shook it with both of hers, clutching it like she might never let go.

"You must be Rio Vidal..." he said, his slight Slovic accent warm and familiar.

Rio opened her mouth. Closed it. Then managed, faintly, "Yes."

Agatha, bless her, covered her laugh with a cough. She stepped up to Rio’s side, “She’s just a little...stunned…" Agatha said. “Agatha Harkness, the girlfriend” she said shaking hands.

Pajitnov chuckled warmly. “Understandable. Please, both of you, come, sit.” He pulled a chair directly across from him and gestured toward it.

Somehow, Rio’s legs carried her forward. She sat stiffly, hands clenched in her lap, knuckles pale with tension. Agatha moved a seat beside her, watching quietly.

For a full three seconds, Rio didn’t move. Didn’t breathe.

"I have been watching your matches…" Pajitnov said, his voice like a soft rumble. "...you are...remarkable."

Rio made a small noise in her throat, somewhere between a gasp and a broken squeak.

Agatha pressed her knuckles to her mouth to keep from audibly laughing at how utterly starstruck Rio looked.

"I don’t..." Rio began, but the words got tangled somewhere in her throat.

Pajitnov just chuckled, reaching out and squeezing Rio’s hand. "There are no right words. Only...understanding."

And somehow, Agatha realized, they did understand each other already. Without Rio needing to say a thing.

Rio opened her mouth. Nothing came out. She cleared her throat, tried again. “I..." she croaked. “Thank you for making your game, Sir.”

Pajitnov chuckled again. “No need for ‘sir’. We are...colleagues, no?”

Rio stared at him like he had just handed her the keys to the universe. ‘Colleagues? With him???’ she screamed in her head. She swallowed hard, heart thundering in her chest.

Pajitnov leaned back, smiling in a way that softened all the edges of his face. “Tell me, when did you first fall in love with the game?”

Rio blinked. She hesitated, then said, quietly “When I was 8 my dad showed me the game...I don’t know if it was love. Not at first. It was...order. When nothing else made sense.”

Pajitnov nodded thoughtfully, like he understood completely. “Order in chaos..." he said softly. “...it is the foundation. Even in falling blocks.”

Rio felt something tight loosen in her chest.

Agatha watched all of it silently, her heart cracking wide open.

Rio finally found her voice, raspy but steady. “You changed everything for me. When I was younger, I didn’t understand people. But I understood patterns. I understood...strategy.”

Pajitnov smiled wider. “We all need something to catch us, yes? Even when everything else fails.”

There was a long, heavy silence between them. Not awkward. Sacred.

Pajitnov studied Rio for a moment longer, then his smile tilted a little mischievously. "The game belongs to everyone who loves it."

Rio blinked hard, looking away like she might actually cry if she looked him in the eye too long.

Pajitnov gestured toward the Tetris rig. "I thought...perhaps...you would allow me the honor of a game?"

Rio made a tiny, shattered noise, a mix between a laugh and a sob, and nodded frantically. "Against you?" she whispered, eyes huge.

Agatha leaned back in her chair, grinning. ‘Oh my god. She’s about to ascend to another plane of existence.’

Rio plugged in her controller and they played.

For twenty glorious minutes, Rio Vidal and Alexey Pajitnov sat across from each other, battling slowly through a casual match of Tetris.

It wasn’t about winning. Rio was far, far faster. Pajitnov knew it. Rio knew it. Everyone knew it. But that wasn’t the point.

Rio slowed herself down without being asked, adjusting her play to match his. Meeting him where he was. Sharing the game, not dominating it.

Agatha saw it immediately. She wasn’t here to win. She was here to honor him. And he to honor her.

Rio’s fingers flew over the controller, but her eyes stayed soft. Focused. Patient.

And when Pajitnov finally filled his board, the game over flashing up, he laughed, warm and easy, like he hadn’t had that much fun in years.

He extended his hand to Rio again.

Rio immediately shook it, both hands covering his, his head ducked in silent respect.

“You are better than me..." Pajitnov said warmly. "But better yet...you have heart."

Rio, blinking fast, croaked, "Thank…you."

Pajitnov leaned in closer and whispered, like a secret, "The world does not need more fast players. It needs more players who love, and you have honored my game..." Pajitnov said simply. "That is all a creator could ever ask for."

Rio nodded, face flushed, eyes wide and glassy.

Agatha swiped fiercely at her eyes, pretending it was dust or whatever bullshit excuse her brain could conjure, because, if she started sobbing now, she was never going to stop.

“Oh…before I forget..." he pulled out a silver marker from his jacket pocket. “May I?” he asked, gesturing to her controller.

Rio nodded so fast she thought she might snap her own neck and handed him the controller. He signed the back of the controller slowly, carefully. “A. Pajitnov. Play Well. Always.”

Then he turned it, holding it out, giving it to Rio with both hands like an offering. Rio took it with a reverence she didn’t know she was capable of. Her fingers closed around it. It felt heavier now. Warmer.

Rio blinked rapidly. The world shimmered.

Pajitnov smiled. “The world is yours. It always was.”

Rio opened her mouth, but again, no words came, so she did the only thing she could think of. She stood, set the controller carefully down on her chair and bowed. Deep. From the waist. A full, formal, soulful bow.

Pajitnov chuckled again, something proud and amused in his eyes. He stood too, and bowed lightly in return.

Agatha rose too, watching Rio with something dangerously close to tears prickling the backs of her eyes. God, she loved this fucking weirdo.

When Rio straightened, she was breathing a little hard, but her hands were steady.

Pajitnov clapped her lightly on the shoulder.

"Play well, Miss Vidal..." he said kindly. "But more importantly...enjoy life."

Rio nodded, once, sharp and certain. Then, in a voice rough with feeling, she said, “I will. Thank you.”

And then he departed.

Rio looked at Agatha and broke down in tears. Overwhelmed, she couldn’t believe what had just happened. She had actually met the man whose work had quietly stitched itself into the fabric of her life, her career, her identity, her soul.

Her hands were still shaking. The signed controller on her chair felt almost too sacred to pick up.

Agatha wrapped her arms around Rio without hesitation, squeezing her close.

"Hey…" Agatha murmured against her temple, voice low and steady. "Breathe. It's real. You did it."

Rio squeezed her eyes shut, pressing her face into Agatha’s shoulder. Her whole body was trembling with adrenaline and disbelief and pure, helpless joy.

"I can’t believe it..." Rio choked out, muffled by Agatha’s blouse. "I can't believe he knew who I was. He shook my hand. He talked to me. He said my name."

Agatha smiled, smoothing a hand down Rio’s back. "Of course he did..." she said easily, like it was the most obvious thing in the world. "You're you. You’re the best in the world at what you do. You think he wasn’t going to know that?"

Rio pulled back just enough to see Agatha’s face, eyes shining, mouth wobbling between a laugh and another sob.

Agatha just kissed her forehead, firm and sure. "You deserve all of it. Every second of this. So just…let yourself have it, ok?"

Rio nodded fiercely, blinking rapidly, picking up the controller and clutching it to her chest like a holy relic.

And for once, Rio let herself believe it. “Ok.”

– –

– –

The next evening, Agatha walked out of their room and down the pathway towards the lobby of the onsen. Rio was only a few feet ahead, engaged in what was unmistakably a conversation in fluid, flawless Japanese with the translator.

By the time Agatha approached closer, Rio had dipped her head slightly and exchanged a few final words as they got into the van together.

Agatha settled in and smirked at Rio, nudging her slightly. "You didn’t tell me you spoke the local language."

Rio turned to her, completely unfazed. "I didn’t think it was vital information to share."

Agatha threw her hands up. "Vidal, you’re like a sleeper agent. What else are you hiding? Can you fly a helicopter too?"

Rio’s mouth twitched in the faintest, guiltiest, almost smile. "Not yet."

Agatha groaned, dragging a hand down her face. "Ok. Now what?"

Rio tilted her head slightly, the suggestion casual but somehow still exacting. "Dinner."

Agatha sighed, loud and theatrical. "I thought you'd never ask."

– –

– –

– –

Sukiyabashi Jiro wasn’t just a sushi restaurant. It was practically a shrine. Small. Impossibly clean. Minimalistic to the point of obscene. Every seat at the long, gleaming wooden counter faced the master at work, Jiro’s son tonight, but the tradition remained, no menu, no choices, just what the chef prepared, when he prepared it.

Agatha sat beside Rio and glanced around the restaurant, taking in the polished wood, the gleaming counters, the air of sophistication that hung over the place. It was a far cry from the tiny, bustling sushi joint in Pasadena where she and Rio had shared their first dinner together. She looked over at Rio, who sat with her hands folded neatly in her lap, the picture of calm, and smiled warmly at her.

The first piece of sushi arrived. Perfectly shaped tuna nigiri, glistening with a delicate sheen.

Agatha looked at it, then at Rio. "Is it weird if I cry over raw fish?"

Rio blinked once. "Only if you cry before eating it."

Agatha snorted and delicately picked up the piece, popping it into her mouth. Immediate, perfect bliss.

She exhaled like she’d been holding her breath for years. "Holy shit."

Rio followed suit, chewing thoughtfully, her face unreadable in that very Rio way. She swallowed and offered her verdict, "...Acceptable."

Agatha nearly choked. "Acceptable? Are you kidding me? I think my soul just ascended and is looking for my body."

Rio tapped the back of her hand lightly, a small giggle escaping her. "Subjective measurements."

They fell into a rhythm, pieces arriving one by one, small conversations filling the spaces between courses.

Rio glanced sideways at her after a while, voice softer. "Do you think...Scratchy is angry?"

Agatha barked out a laugh, almost dropping her next piece of sushi. "Oh, absolutely. That rabbit’s probably mounted a coup by now."

Rio’s mouth twitched again. "Jen promised to feed him raspberries."

Agatha sighed dramatically. "She’s going to turn him into a spoiled, pink mouthed monster. We'll come home and he’ll be wearing a tiny crown made of dandelions."

Rio tilted her head. "He already has a throne, what more is a crown…" making gestures to reference the baby carrier back at home.

Agatha grinned. "His reign of terror is nearly complete."

Rio sipped her green tea calmly. "...with optimal pre-planning."

Another piece of sushi arrived, this time uni, sea urchin, a silky, briny taste that melted the moment it touched the tongue. They both took a bite in near unison, and for a moment, there was just silence. The good kind.

Agatha watched Rio out of the corner of her eye, her serene posture, the slight furrow in her brow when she was focused, even on eating, and smiled to herself.

"You know…" Agatha murmured after a minute, "...I can’t believe it’s only been a few weeks."

Rio blinked over at her, like she was calculating something. "...since we met."

Agatha nodded, swirling the last sip of her sake in the small porcelain cup. "Feels longer. In a good way. Like...like I should’ve met you years ago."

Rio set her tea down carefully. "You weren't ready."

Agatha barked a sharp laugh, almost surprised at how accurate that felt. "Oh come on…you don't have to call me out like that at a Michelin star restaurant."

Rio smirked faintly. "...but I was ready."

Agatha stared at Rio for a long moment. There was no flinch in her voice, no hesitation in her certainty.

Agatha reached under the counter and squeezed Rio’s hand, small, quick, like a secret.

"Yea…" Agatha said quietly. "...and you helped me be ready too."

Another course arrived, salmon so soft it almost dissolved on contact.

Through it all, through the hum of the restaurant and the crisp chatter of the chef and servers, Agatha kept glancing sideways at Rio, at the easy way her shoulders had dropped, at the faint light in her usually guarded eyes.

Home wasn’t a place. It was her. Wherever they went next? It didn’t really matter.

Rio finished her tea, wiped her mouth with the corner of her napkin, and stood, offering her hand out like it was the most natural thing in the world.

Agatha grinned, stood, and took it without hesitation.

"Let’s go..." Rio said softly. "Before I spend all our yen on tuna sashimi."

Agatha laughed. "God forbid. Scratchy needs his future millions."

And together, they slipped out into the cool Tokyo night, the world sprawling before them, and for once, it all felt manageable, because they were doing it together.

– –

– –

The next day, Agatha was overwhelmed.

And that was putting it mildly.

She wasn’t surprised, Rio had warned her. She'd smiled in that quiet, cryptic way of hers and said, “Akihabara’s a little intense. Just follow me ok?” But no amount of warning could’ve prepared Agatha for this. The place was chaos made flesh.

A neon fever dream, bursting at the seams. Loud, fast, relentless. The buildings all blazed with electric colors, towering billboards flashing anime girls with oversized eyes and unreadable expressions. Giant LCD screens screamed advertisements for gacha games, idol concerts, obscure virtual YouTubers. Electronic store banners flapped furiously in the wind, shouting discounts into the street with blinding bold type. Music clashed with music. Mascots danced on the corners. There was a man in a full Pikachu suit breakdancing without shame. Claw machines filled with plushies spilled out of storefronts and onto the sidewalk like offerings to some benevolent, sugar eyed god.

Agatha blinked hard and reached for Rio’s hand like a tether to reality. “This place is...Jesus Christ, Rio…”

Rio, naturally, looked unbothered. Serene, even. Holding Agatha’s hand tighter, “Don’t worry. I’ve got you.”

The sea of noise and color didn’t touch Rio. She moved through the crowd like she belonged to it, like it recognized her. One foot in front of the other, avoiding collisions with impossible precision. People made space for her without realizing it. Agatha had to consciously remind herself not to stare around like a grandma seeing the internet for the first time.

“Are you sure we didn’t accidentally teleport to the inside of your brain?” she muttered, dodging a teenage boy in a full Gundam costume.

Eventually, they ducked down a side alley, and Agatha thought maybe the chaos would relent, but no. It only condensed. The space grew tighter, more vertical. Ads screamed louder, and then, at last, they stopped in front of a towering building with glass doors and six full floors of pure gaming madness. Its signage “PORTAL” pulsed with color like a heartbeat.

“I think you’ll like this place..." Rio said simply.

Agatha raised an eyebrow, skeptical. “I doubt that.”

Rio held the door open. They stepped inside. Instantly, Agatha was hit with another wave of sensory confusion. This time, it was focused and razor sharp.

Cabinets lined the walls, glowing, pulsing, moving in tandem. Screens flashed rapid fire visuals, notes flying down the track like missiles, hands darting across controls faster than the eye could track. Music pounded, fast and precise and otherworldly. Lights moved in sync. Everything felt mathematical. Measured. A thousand patterns at once. And all around them, players, just like Rio.

Not casuals. Not button mashers. Professionals. They stood like athletes in front of their machines, gloved hands in motion, eyes unblinking, posture impossibly straight. They were still, until they weren’t, and when they moved, it was with impossible speed and fluidity. Like dance. Like combat.

Agatha turned slowly, deadpan. “You dragged me to a cult.”

Rio pulled something from her pocket without looking, another pair of gloves, and offered them to Agatha, she held them out, as casual as could be.

Agatha recoiled, “Oh, no champ. I’m here to spectate. Admire from a safe and respectful distance. Plus, I’d feel like a kid playing on easy mode in a place like this.”

Rio hummed, unfazed, stuffed them back in her pocket, and fishing out her green gloves from the other, putting them on like a surgeon prepping for a procedure. “Suit yourself.”

And with that, they turned and headed deeper into the arcade.

Agatha followed, unable to help herself. Even now, especially now, Rio was a magnet. She moved with purpose, as if every turn of her head had been planned.

They wove past flickering lights and pounding bass, past a group of players gathered around a DDR cabinet like fans at a championship match, and then, they stopped.

At the very back of the floor, in a dim, narrow alcove, sat a single, unassuming machine.

It looked older than everything else, worn edges, green buttons, a screen that flickered slightly like it was barely holding on. The marquee at the top had faded, the lettering cracked and peeling.

Agatha squinted at the words. TETRIS: THE GRAND MASTER. She folded her arms across her chest. “Seriously? More Tetris Rio?”

Rio didn’t even flinch. “This not just Tetris. This one is one of a kind, and this one’s the only one in the world.”

Without further explanation, Rio stepped forward, gave a small bow to the machine, and fed a coin into the slot. The machine whirred, the screen coming to life.

– –

– –

Agatha looked on skeptically but intrigued. At first, it was familiar. The old school visuals of Tetris. The charming, bloopy sound effects. The classic tetrominoes falling into place.

Then it sped up. Not gradually. Not fairly. Like the machine had decided it was done playing nice and wanted to embarrass anyone who dared to play it.

The pieces slammed down instantly, leaving barely a breath between their arrival on the screen and their being locked into place. Agatha gasped. “What the…what the fuck?”

But Rio didn’t flinch. Her fingers danced over the controls like water, the joystick stuttered in her hand, and the sudden button taps for rotations were lightning. Precise. Controlled. Not just reacting, but anticipating.

Agatha couldn’t look away.

Faster. Faster still the pieces came and were placed. The pixels blurred. Agatha’s brain couldn’t process the shapes fast enough to track them, but Rio’s body could. Agatha’s heartbeat kicked up. Her skin prickled.

This was no longer a game. This was art. Performance. Muscle memory carved into bone.

And then, the pieces vanished. Literally vanished. The invisible stage. Every block placed disappeared the second it landed. The board blanked out. No ghost trail. No visible stack. Just empty, unknowable space.

Agatha’s hand went to Rio’s shoulder as she watched.

Rio kept playing. Not a flicker of hesitation. No pause. Her fingers continued moving with supernatural confidence, slotting invisible pieces into place like she could see what no one else could. Like the machine didn’t matter.

The final block landed. A pause.

Then the screen flashed in massive, glowing letters: “EXCELLENT. MASTER MODE. ALL CLEAR.” Then made way for more words “GRAND MASTER.”

Rio stepped back and breathed out slowly, the sound of her own breath returning to her ears.

Agatha was silent. Wide eyed. Her body was coiled like a spring, breath caught in her throat. She hadn’t blinked in minutes. Finally, she exhaled. “That was terrifying..." she muttered.

Rio turned, cocking her head. “You didn’t like it?”

Agatha blinked at her. “Are you kidding me? That was the most absurd thing I’ve ever seen. You play like you’re being possessed.”

Rio’s lips tugged into a modest smile, head tilted, eyes sharp and just a touch wicked.

“You know..." she said, brushing her fingers through her hair, “...there is something here harder to play.”

Agatha squinted. “No way? What???”

Rio leaned in just enough for it to count. “You.”

Agatha blinked, then barked a single laugh, loud and unexpected. “Oh my god, you absolute menace.”

Rio just smiled, all quiet mischief and catlike stillness, watching her reaction like she was proud of herself.

Agatha gave her a long, suspicious look as they began to drift away from the Tetris cabinet, back into the arcade’s organized chaos. She folded her arms loosely, eyes darting from screen to screen as they passed players lost in their own private battlegrounds.

“I cannot believe you just flirted with me again using game logic..." Agatha muttered, but her mouth twitched, betraying her amusement.

“I’m adapting to my environment..." Rio said, hands in her pockets now, cool as ever. “You should appreciate the effort.”

Agatha rolled her eyes. “You’re insufferable.”

Rio smirked, “...but charming.”

Agatha huffed, “No.”

Rio tilted her head. “You’re smiling.”

Agatha rolled her shoulders, “That’s just…muscle fatigue from watching you violate the laws of physics.”

Rio sighed, “Sure, Agatha.”

They passed a row of claw machines, the kind Agatha used to hate on principle. Stacks of adorable, overpriced plushies inside, chubby bears, blushing frogs, sleepy boba cups with felt straws.

Agatha sighed softly and rubbed the back of her neck. Then, she glanced sideways at Rio. “Alright…fine…hand me the gloves.”

Rio raised a brow. “Oh?”

“I might as well play something..." Agatha said, snatching the gloves from Rio’s hand with mock irritation. She fumbled them on awkwardly. “Since apparently I’ve been absorbed into the Church of the Holy High Score.”

Rio suppressed a laugh. “I didn’t think you’d convert so fast.”

“I didn’t say I believed..." Agatha said, flexing her fingers, “...just that I’m willing to sin.”

That got a real smile out of Rio, broad and slightly stunned, the kind that only came when Agatha said something that short circuited her for just a second. It was one of Agatha’s favorite side quests, shocking Rio into actually looking flustered.

Agatha slung her hair back into a ponytail, growing more serious. “Is there anything we can play together?” she asked. Her voice had changed, lower now, intentional. A kind of vulnerability disguised as casual curiosity.

Rio’s eyes softened. “Yea..." she said quietly. “...come on.”

– –

– –

Rio led Agatha through the arcade until she stopped in front of a glossy, futuristic game that looked more like a light altar than anything else.

Agatha blinked. “What is this? Minority Report?”

“CHUNITHM…” Rio said, already tapping the screen to wake it. “...it’s a rhythm game.”

The machine came alive, beams of neon shooting from the base, a transparent runway of notes stretching out, motion sensors above, glowing pads below. It looked like it required either divine intervention or professional dance training.

“You’re not seriously expecting me to solo this…cunt game…” Agatha said, eyeing the setup like it might bite her.

Rio gave her a grin, all teeth and mischief. “Wouldn’t dream of it. I’ve got your six.”

Agatha stepped onto the platform, slowly placing her hands near the glowing controls. The interface buzzed under her fingertips like it was waiting for her to mess up.

Rio moved in behind her, close, too close, one hand resting lightly on the side rail, the other ready to jump in. Agatha could feel Rio’s breath at the back of her neck, steady and infuriatingly calm.

The game started.

Colors streaked down the transparent field, timing markers lighting up in sync with the rising beat. Agatha swiped late. Tapped too hard. Her hand missed a vertical air slash completely.

Rio reached around her, smooth and fast, catching it with an elegant sweep of her arm.

“You’re doing just fine…” Rio murmured into Agatha’s ear, like her voice had been designed specifically to melt posture.

Agatha’s hands stuttered over the pads. “I am not fine.”

“Then I’ll make you look like you are.” Rio said calmly.

Every time Agatha faltered, missed a beat, hesitated too long, Rio was there, stepping in with a flick of her fingers or a perfect air swipe, her movements seamless with Agatha’s. Not taking over. Just completing her.

The song ramped up, faster now. Agatha tried to keep up, but she was sweating, heart racing, arms flying. Rio matched her pace, bodies nearly in sync, her hands ghosting over Agatha’s when needed, guiding her like they were dancing.

“You’re doing better than you think…” Rio said, just behind her ear now. “...trust your hands.”

“My hands are panicking…” Agatha said with an incredulous laugh.

“But mine aren’t…” Rio quipped back.

Their arms moved as one, Agatha reaching forward, Rio sweeping above her, fingers brushing, a flash of contact, of heat. The music crescendoed. Their shared rhythm tightened. The machine lit up like it was about to ascend into orbit.

Agatha caught her reflection in the side of the display, flushed, breathless, Rio standing behind her like a shadow stitched to her back, impossibly calm and entirely too beautiful.

The song ended in a blaze of color.

Rio stepped back, just barely, but her presence still hummed against Agatha’s spine.

“You made it…” Rio said, not even out of breath.

Agatha turned around, dazed. “I blacked out…I think…I saw God?”

Rio tilted her head, lips curving slow. “...was she whispering behind your ear wearing a green glove?”

Agatha didn’t answer. She was too busy trying to remember how breathing worked.

The screen gave a final burst of color. Score: B+.

Agatha bent forward, panting, laughing breathlessly. “Jesus. Ok. That was...weirdly hot.”

Rio was already pulling her gloves off. “Told you we could play it together.”

Agatha laughed, still catching her breath, then straightened and reached out. Her hand found Rio’s wrist before she even thought about it.

In the bright, flickering light of the arcade, Agatha looked up at Rio, heart racing from something far more personal than gameplay. She didn’t say anything. She didn’t have to.

Rio leaned in first, just enough. Just far enough that Agatha could feel the shape of the invitation in the air. But she didn’t close the gap. Not yet.

Agatha smiled slowly. “Still think I’m harder to play?”

Rio’s voice was soft. “No...” She reached out, brushing Agatha’s hand with hers, lacing their fingers. “...I think I’ve figured out your pattern.”

Then Rio kissed her. Soft. Sure. Unhurried. Amidst the clamor of other machines, the beeping, the cheering, the chaos of a city built on speed and obsession, they made their own kind of music.

Something private. Perfectly synced, and theirs. – –

– –

The sun had barely climbed over the skyline when Rio declared, in a soft, unbothered voice, “I have an itinerary.”

Agatha blinked at her from the futon, one eye open, glancing at her phone. “Rio, it’s 6:30 in the morning.”

Rio stood fully dressed, already putting on her shoes. “If we leave now, we’ll make it to Nara before the crowds.”

“Nara?” Agatha groaned, flopping onto her back and pulling the covers over her face. “What’s in Nara that’s worth losing the will to live over?”

“Deer..." Rio said simply.

There was a long pause from under the blanket. Then a muffled voice, “Is this a revenge thing? Because I said that your stuffed llama…alpaca…whatever…was dumb?”

Rio didn’t answer. That was answer enough.

By the time Agatha sat upright, her hair was everywhere, her voice scratchy, her body profoundly resentful. But she was already reaching for her hoodie, muttering something about caffeine and divine punishment. Twenty minutes later, they were on a train slicing through the morning light, Agatha nursing a vending machine latte and Rio cross legged beside her, scrolling through photos of deer like they were tactical maps for a warzone.

“Some of them bow..." Rio said.

“Uh huh.” Agatha huffed.

“They’ll do it for crackers.” Rio continued excited.

“So would Scratchy.” Agatha quipped back.

“They can also…bite…and…headbutt.” Rio said slowly, a little nervous.

Agatha turned to stare at her. “I swear to god Rio….you’re literally dragging me to a wildlife ambush.”

“Don’t worry, I probably bite harder.” Rio smirked and gave a little wink.

The train rocked gently. The city gave way to green. By the time they arrived in Nara, the sun had warmed the pavement and the scent of trees drifted in on the breeze. The park was already alive families, couples, sleepy school groups gathering near the entrance. And, of course, deer. Dozens of them. Casual, purposeful, disturbingly intelligent looking.

Agatha took one cautious step into the park and immediately had her hoodie sleeve nibbled by a doe with no sense of boundaries.

“Rio..." Agatha hissed.

“They’re hungry..." Rio replied, handing her a packet of crackers.

The moment Agatha had the crackers, the deer swarmed.

“Jesus Christ…back! BACK!” Agatha cried, backing up, her arms above her head, crackers clutched like a life raft. One deer bowed politely, then nipped at her. Another stared at her with what could only be described as mild judgment.

Rio, several feet away and completely untouched, offered a serene bow to a buck who returned it with dignity before accepting a cracker with almost ceremonial grace.

“They respect confidence..." Rio said.

Agatha glared at her, pinned against a tree by three insistent deer, one of whom had stolen the plastic from her cracker bag and was now chewing it with vindictive eye contact.

“I am radiating pure dominance..." Agatha snapped. “...they just don’t understand it.”

“You’re radiating panic and submissive and breedable to the deer, Agatha...” Rio chuckled.

Eventually, Agatha escaped, hoodie rumpled, one sock slightly chewed, muttering something about herbivore coups. They sat together under a tree for a while, watching a toddler give up his crackers to a particularly pushy deer with the resigned grace of a martyr. Agatha leaned her head against Rio’s shoulder.

“If I get arrested for almost dropkicking a woodland creature, I’m blaming you…” Agatha sighed.

“You won’t get arrested..." Rio said.

“I might. That one licked my ankle.” Agatha scoffed.

“He bowed first. It was respectful.” Rio nodded.

After they escaped the park, Rio calmly, Agatha speed walking with one eye over her shoulder, they hopped on a train bound for Kyoto. The ride was quiet, the carriage softly rocking, a few other tourists clustered in corners murmuring over maps. Rio pulled out her headphones. Agatha stole one without asking.

They found a little shop tucked behind a market, the kind with a fabric curtain over the door and the smell of butter and soy wafting through the air. The cook was an older man with forearms like stone and an omurice technique that was surgical. Agatha watched, mesmerized, as he flipped the omelet with one hand, sliced it gently open with a butter knife, and let it unfold over the rice like a miracle.

Agatha took one bite and made a sound that should’ve been illegal in public.

Rio raised her eyebrows. “Do I need to leave you two alone?”

“I need to date this egg instead..." Agatha whispered.

Rio shook her head, but her smile was unmistakable.

After lunch, they wandered slowly toward Fushimi Inari. The torii gates stretched endlessly upward, red orange against the green of the trees, the path winding like a spell. Agatha fell silent after the first turn. The light filtered through the gates like something alive. She walked with her fingertips grazing the wood, mouth slightly open.

They didn’t speak until they reached a small overlook partway up the mountain, Kyoto visible through the trees below, hazy and soft.

Agatha sank onto a bench. “Ok..." she said. “...this lives up to the hype.”

Rio didn’t sit. She leaned standing against the back of the bench, quiet, her hand drifting to lay on Agatha’s shoulder. She watched the city below, but mostly she watched Agatha. Rio realized she was so hopelessly, stupidly in love with Agatha, the kind of love that made her want to memorize every sigh, every eye roll, every uneven breath. Being near her felt like standing in the middle of a lightning storm and daring it to strike twice. She didn’t just want Agatha, she wanted to understand her, to map the terrain of her mind like a song she could play perfectly if she listened hard enough.

Agatha looked up at her. “Whatcha thinkin bout Smashboy?”

Rio blinked once. Her mouth tugged, just a little. “I didn’t schedule any feelings into the itinerary.”

Agatha tapped Rio’s hand on her shoulder. “Guess you’ll have to improvise.”

They stayed a little longer than they meant to, then wandered back down through fox statues and stone lanterns, the sound of wind chimes faint in the distance.

Rio bought a fox mask at a shrine stall. Wore it tilted on her head like a smug festival ghost.

“You look ridiculous..." Agatha said.

“I look powerful.” Rio posed, arms wide. “Fear me, for I am the ghost of anime past.”

And Agatha, for once, didn’t say anything at all. She just reached for Rio, laced their arms together, and let the silence speak for them both as they strolled through the city streets.

– –

– –

That night, back at their room, after they'd scrubbed away the day's weight and curled up in their matching robes, hair damp and mussed, faces bare of anything but the glow of contentment, Rio sat cross legged on the tatami mat, fiddling nervously with a small black box in her lap.

The faint scent of wood and hot springs clung to them, threading through the air like an invisible tether to the peace they'd found earlier in the steaming baths. Outside, Tokyo hummed its endless, muffled song, far away neon reflections rippling across the glass windows.

Agatha sprawled across the futon with all the elegance of a starfish, one arm thrown carelessly over her eyes, the other draped across her stomach. The hem of her robe slipping higher on one thigh with every tiny, lazy shift.

Agatha heard the soft, uncertain rustling before she felt it, the strange charge that always rippled in the air when Rio was working up the nerve to do something, but couldn’t find the words fast enough.

Peeking out from beneath her arm, Agatha caught sight of Rio hunched over herself, the box turning anxiously in her hands. Her lips quirked into a half smile, fond and teasing.

“What’s that, champ?” she drawled, her voice rough edged from the heat and the hour, sleepiness curling at the edges.

Rio looked up, startled, caught in the act. She moved closer and sat across from Agatha, awkward and endearing, her cheeks blooming a visible pink even in the low light.

“I got you something..." Rio said, her voice pitched soft, like she was afraid speaking too loudly would make Agatha vanish.

Agatha sat up immediately, suspicion flashing across her face, cutting through the haze of relaxation like a blade. She squinted, seeing the box in Rio’s hands the previous humor disappearing, and nervousness rising bright in her chest.

“Is it…a…something for Scratchy?” Agatha said, her mouth suddenly dry and full of uncertainty.

Rio gave a huffed, nervous laugh, shaking her head. “No..." she said, taking a deep breath to ground herself. “...it’s…something…for us.”

Agatha tilted her head, her suspicion softening into intrigue. She folded her legs underneath herself and leaned forward slightly, her robe slipping from one shoulder without her noticing.

Rio exhaled slowly, steadying herself, and lifted the lid of the box.

Inside, resting against a bed of deep black velvet, were two silver necklaces, their chains delicate and fine, catching the muted light like threads of spun sugar. Each held a small pendant: one orange, one blue. L-shaped Tetris pieces. Rickys. The stubborn pieces.

Opposites…and yet designed to fit perfectly together.

Agatha’s breath hitched, a small, involuntary sound breaking from somewhere deep inside her. For a moment, she didn’t move. Couldn’t. She just stared, the meaning of it landing hard and silent in the hollow of her chest.

Rio fumbled a little, hands trembling as she lifted the orange piece between careful fingers, the tiny pendant swinging slightly from its chain.

“This one’s for you..." she said, her voice unsteady but certain.

Agatha swallowed thickly, her throat constricting around words she couldn't find yet. She leaned in without thinking, letting Rio slip the necklace around her neck with gentle, reverent hands.

The chain was cool against her skin, sending a shiver racing down her spine.

Rio sat back on her heels, her gaze never leaving Agatha’s face as she reached for the blue necklace and fumbled it around her own neck, her fingers clumsy with nerves and something deeper, something wordless and raw.

When Rio finally got it fastened, she let out a shaky breath and looked up at Agatha. For a few long, breathless seconds, neither of them said anything. The soft lamplight caught the twin pendants where they rested against their chests, glinting like two tiny beacons across the short distance between them.

Agatha raised a hand to her chest and looked down, her fingers brushing lightly over the small orange Ricky, feeling the delicate grooves of it, the weight far heavier than the metal itself could explain. Her eyes burned. She blinked hard, forcing the flood back before it could crest.

Rio watched her, something fragile and blazing naked in her expression, hope, fear, love, all laid out plain across every line of her face.

“I figured..." Rio said, so quietly Agatha almost missed it “...you’re the one who fits with me.”

The words hit Agatha like a blow and a balm all at once, stealing the air from her lungs.

Agatha laughed, a broken, helpless sound. “You are such a fucking nerd..." she whispered, the words thick with unshed tears and fierce, uncontainable affection.

Rio smiled, wide and dazzling now, the kind of smile that only ever appeared when she felt safe, when she let herself believe in the good things life had still in store for her.

“And you love me for it..." Rio murmured, her voice a tremor against the quiet around them.

Agatha kissed her then, slow and aching and deep, threading her fingers through Rio’s damp hair. The pendants brushed between their chests, cool and fleeting, like a promise exchanged without words.

When they finally broke apart, they didn’t move far, their foreheads pressed together, breathing the same warm air.

“God help me..." Agatha whispered against Rio’s lips, “I really, really do.”

They sat like that, tangled together in the futon, their hearts beating out a shared rhythm.

Minutes passed. Maybe hours. Time lost its grip on them entirely.

At some point, Rio pulled Agatha gently onto her lap, cradling her like something precious, and Agatha let herself be held without protest. She tucked her face against Rio’s shoulder, her arms slipping around her waist, feeling Rio’s steady heartbeat through the thin layer of her robe.

The city hummed on outside, vast and uncaring. But here, in the small, sacred pocket of space they carved out together, the world shrank to a single point of gravity.

Agatha sighed, a sound of pure contentment, and nuzzled closer. “I don't deserve you..." she murmured, barely audible.

Rio tightened her arms around her without hesitation, resting her chin atop Agatha’s head.

“You do..." she said simply. “...and I you.”

Agatha’s throat closed up again, but she didn’t fight it this time. She let the emotion crest and break, warm and overwhelming and safe.

They stayed there, wrapped around each other, until the city dimmed and the night deepened, until the fatigue of the day finally claimed them. – –

– –

On the plane ride home, Agatha wrote.

Her fingers moved steadily over the keys, the low hum of the engines and Rio’s quiet, rhythmic breathing beside her the only soundtrack she needed. Her article had started out as a simple piece, but what took shape under her hands was something else entirely.

Agatha stared at the screen for a long time, then looked over at Rio, asleep against the window, hair mussed, green glove still on one hand like a half finished sentence.

This story wasn’t what she had planned. It was better.

– –

The Grandmaster Who Played by No Rules

By Agatha Harkness

There’s something hypnotic about watching a master at work. The way they move, the way they breathe, the way their hands become an extension of something larger, something beyond muscle memory, beyond instinct. A master makes the impossible look easy.

And Rio Vidal? She makes Tetris look like a game designed for her.

I knew this going in. I had watched her dominate at the Classic Tetris World Championship, watched her obliterate the competition with such chilling efficiency that I wondered if she was even capable of error. She played like someone who had mapped every possibility, seen every outcome, and decided failure simply wasn’t an option.

I thought I had already seen her at her best. Then I followed her to Japan.

When you win Tetris at the highest level, Nintendo flies you out to their headquarters in Tokyo for a week long victory tour. And so, at Rio’s casual invitation, and my editor’s rabid enthusiasm, I tagged along. I expected the usual. Maybe some corporate meet and greet, some fancy events, a handshake or two.

Instead, I got a front row seat to something that should not be humanly possible.

Rio took me to Akihabara, the electric beating heart of the gaming world. It was loud, neon soaked, filled with the unmistakable hum of machines and the rhythmic clack clack clack of buttons being pressed at inhuman speeds.

Rio fit right in.

She led me through a maze of towering arcades, past rows of claw machines and fighting games, to the back of a dimly lit hall. There, tucked in a corner like it was waiting for her, was an old, flickering cabinet. Tetris: The Grand Master.

She slid a coin in. And then she showed me something I don’t think I’ll ever forget.

Rio didn’t just play the game...she became it. This is not just any version of Tetris. It’s one of the hardest, the kind that speeds up beyond reason, where pieces slam down instantly, giving you mere milliseconds to react. It’s designed to break you. To overwhelm you. To make you fail.

But Rio? She played like someone who had already won.

Her hands were flawless, moving so fast I almost couldn’t track them. The pieces barely had time to appear before she was slotting them into place, carving perfect paths with a precision that should be mathematically impossible. And when she reached the final test, the invisible stage, where the board erases itself, forcing you to remember every placement blindly, she didn’t even hesitate.

It was like watching an artist paint without seeing the canvas.

When she cleared the last line, the machine crowned her Grand Master. I had seen Rio Vidal win before. But this? This was different. This was something holy.

Rio is built for strategy. She optimizes. She calculates. Her world is predictability and control, where every variable can be measured.

Rio doesn’t waste movement. She doesn’t say things unless they matter. And so I realized, this wasn’t just about the game. It was about what happens outside of it.

Rio’s world has always been rules and patterns. But she had stepped outside of that, and not just for me, but for herself.

The most shocking thing I learned in Japan wasn’t how Rio plays Tetris. It’s that, for the first time, she wasn’t just playing to win. She was playing to see what happens next.

And I realized, this whole time, Rio’s real opponent wasn’t the game. It was the question: what happens when you let someone in? What happens when you stop optimizing and start feeling?

The most extraordinary thing about her isn’t that she can beat a game designed to be unbeatable. It’s that she let someone see her do it. It’s that she let me stand beside her while she did. And maybe that’s the real story. Not how she plays. But who she becomes when she stops trying to play it safe.

She’s still the grandmaster. Still the best. But now, she’s playing by no one’s rules. Not even her own.

Game on Rio Vidal.

– –

– –

– –

The cabin lights had dimmed to a gentle amber glow, the kind meant to mimic sunset, though the sun itself was somewhere far behind them, drowning in the clouds over the Pacific. The steady hum of the plane filled the quiet like white noise, lulling most of the cabin into that strange space between sleep and not quite.

Agatha leaned back in her seat, her laptop finally closed, her story sent.

Her fingers still tingled with the ghost of it, of typing fast and full, of writing something that felt less like reporting and more like telling the truth the way it wanted to be told.

Rio was awake now, curled comfortably sideways in her seat, she held a cobalt blue Game Boy Advance SP, flipped open and glowing faintly in the dark. The original Tetris theme tinkled out through the tiny speaker, soft, barely there, but just loud enough for Agatha to catch the familiar notes.

Rio’s thumbs moved steadily over the buttons, her face calm, focused, that faint furrow in her brow that meant she was exactly where she liked to be, solving something. Not saving the world. Just…stacking blocks.

Agatha watched her for a moment, chin resting on her hand. Rio didn’t notice. She was deep in her zone, perfectly herself.

And that was fine. That was everything, really.

Because Agatha didn’t need Rio to look up. Didn’t need a smile or a comment or anything at all. She was happy just being here, in this quiet moment at the end of something big, of something that might’ve been fate or might’ve just been a thousand little decisions, all aligning at the right time.

Agatha let herself smile, soft and real and hers alone. She let the silence settle, let the warmth of it spread in her chest like the afterglow of something just barely survivable.

Then she smirked to herself, thinking ‘turns out, the best part of Tetris…is finding just the right fit.’
